### PROJECT / PROGRAMME SUMMARY

#### A2 Project | Programme Executive Summary (max 100 words)

The objective of this project is to reduce the cost of climate change induced drought on Kenya's national economy by increasing resilience of the livestock and other land use sectors in restored and effectively governed landscapes. The project will be implemented under the framework of the National Drought Policy and the national policy of "Ending Drought Emergencies", as outlined in Kenya’s Vision 2030.

The project will strengthen climate change adaptation in Kenya's dry and semi-arid lands (e.g., ASALs, border regions, and parts of the coastal region) and parts of the national Inland Water Basin. Livestock contributions account for 80% of household incomes in arid and lands and 65% in semi-lands. Drought has been shown to reduce livestock numbers by an average of 24%, with a range from 13% to 29%. The loss is 2.4%, with 72% of the losses concentrated in the livestock sector. Frequency and intensity of drought are increasing as a result of climate change.

The project will be implemented in 2 landscapes encompassing 11 counties, which have derived powers under Kenya's new constitution. The project will benefit 625,000 people in 104,000 households and will protect or restore 500,000 hectares of degraded land.

The target landscapes are dry season grazing areas: critical resource zones that provide refuge during periods of drought. Their existence depends on availability of permanent water, which makes them hotspots for resource competition and land use conflicts. The project will address these challenges by strengthening the capacity of communities to manage following customary governance practices. Customary institutions have become weakened, leading to break down in natural resource management systems.

The target landscapes face challenges of weak capacities for landscape planning, poor access to climate data and analysis, and low access to markets and financial services. The project addresses this through three components:

- **Component 1:** Climate change adapted planning for drought resilience
- **Component 2:** Community capacity building for landscape-based adaptation
- **Component 3:** Climate change resilient ecosystem management for investments

# Climate Context

Kenya’s Vision 2030 provides a road map for becoming an upper middle income economy by the year 2030. One of the biggest threats to the achievement of this vision is the increasing recurrence of drought, causing substantial losses in agricultural production and livestock productivity. The most recent droughts have been severe (ASALs). Prolonged droughts have occurred in 1983/84, 1991/92, 1995/96, 2004/05, 2008/09, 2011/12 and 2017/18.

Drought is a major contributor to chronic food insecurity and poor nutrition in Kenya, which affects about 10 million people. The most recent droughts have been severe (ASALs). Prolonged droughts have occurred in 1983/84, 1991/92, 1995/96, 2004/05, 2008/09, 2011/12 and 2017/18.

Kenya’s ASALs are characterized by extremely high inter-annual variability of rainfall, with the majority of years varying by more than 30% of the mean; and this variability is projected to be increased by climate change. According to the Kenya Meteorological Department (KMD), the country has experienced a significant increase in drought risk over much of Eastern Africa by the 2050s, which ultimately will threaten climate-sensitive economic sectors such as agriculture, livestock production, water resources and energy.

Kenya’s ASALs are characterized by extremely high inter-annual variability of rainfall (see graphs in c.2). Temperature projections for East Africa indicate that mean annual temperatures could increase by 1 to 3°C over the period from 2050 to 2079 compared to the 1980 to 1999 mean. Changes in temperature and precipitation variability due to climate change will increase the frequency of droughts, with a corresponding increase in the number of dry spells, reducing recovery periods, and contributing to changing pressures and increasing impacts on key economic sectors such as agriculture, livestock production, water resources and energy.

### Government Response

The 2016 National Adaptation Plan (NAP) is Kenya’s response to climate change. The NAP was developed in line with the principles of the Paris Agreement and complements other national strategies. Vision 2030 was conceived as an overarching strategic document. Its second Medium Term Plan (MTP II, 2016-2020) sets out Kenya’s priorities for the next five years.

The NAP is a multi-sectoral plan that covers all sectors of the economy and includes adaptation measures in agriculture, livestock production, water resources, crop agriculture and energy sectors. The ASALs are characterized by extremely high inter-annual variability of rainfall (see graphs in c.2). Temperature projections for East Africa indicate that mean annual temperatures could increase by 1 to 3°C over the period from 2050 to 2079 compared to the 1980 to 1999 mean.

### Drought Management

Drought management is a key component of Kenya’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP). The NAP includes drought risk reduction and adaptation measures. These include improving water resources, increasing agricultural productivity, managing land use, promoting livestock mobility to manage climate risks and uncertainties. Kenya’s pastoralists are highly mobile and have developed strategies for coping with the effects of drought. However, these strategies rely heavily on risk management strategies and resources, including herd mobility and drought reserves. Climate change modelling suggests that the frequency and intensity of droughts will increase in East Africa over the next 50 years.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND WINDFUND PROPOSAL | PAGE 4 OF 20

### Reserves
Over time, customary institutions have become weakened, leading to breakdown in natural resource governance and loss of biodiversity.

### Healthy and productive landscapes are central to rangeland productivity and water security for and building resilience and creating livelihood opportunities for communities in the face of climate change. Ressure in pastoral territories is a key element of adaptation strategies that can be implemented at scale. The ability of rangelands to provide secondary (non-livestock) income, Options to generate income from range land resources is determined by access to markets and infrastructure. In Kenya, the majority of livestock are owned by smallholder farmers who are heavily dependent on the resilience of rangeland ecosystems, which is influenced how by herd movements are managed and how resource use conflicts are resolved. The increasing trend towards sedentarization, coupled with increased land use change and uncontrolled expansion of water infrastructure, is contributing to land degradation in the rangelands.

### Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EBA) approaches offer a way to integrate climate change resilience into large-scale landscape management strategies. These strategies can be implemented at scale through the adoption of an overall strategy to help people to adapt to the adverse effects of climate change (Conversion on Biodiversity and Ecosystem Services). The Kenya National Climate Change Action Plan (KNCCAP) recognizes humans, which promotes conservation and sustainable use in an equitable way. In the Kenyan context, EBA will provide a framework for integrating climate change adaptation into land management strategies at all levels, from sources to underpinning adaptive capacities and community resilience. EBA is well recognized as a critical element of adaptation strategy that can be implemented at scale. The Kenya National Climate Change Action Plan (KNCCAP) Risk Reduction (RR) and the Kenya National Adaptation Programme. Despite this attention, EBA was not well integrated into most national strategies and policies in Kenya until 2013 when the EBA plan was launched in 2013. EBA was a fairly emergent concept and was not popular in Kenya at that time. The EBA plan was strongly aligned with humanitarian disaster with droughts, dominating the conceptual thinking.

### Opportunities
Opportunities for scaling up EBA include: devolution of power to county governments, which is gaining autonomy by country governments; Kenya's 2010 Constitution mandates a shift towards decentralized and devolved governance. The new constitution also provides for the establishment of counties as the third tier of government, with elected governors and assemblies. This will provide an opportunity for EBA to be integrated planning with the participation of land users at the county level. Local governments are being negotiated to ensure that they have a role in implementing EBA. The devolution process is also expected to lead to increased investment in the ASALs and offers new opportunities for upholding the rights of pastoralists.

### In 2010 Kenya established the ASAL Secretariat as a permanent and specialized institution to champion and coordinate development in the ASALs. In 2012 Kenya adopted a strategy for Ending Drought Emergencies by the year 2025. The EBA project will be implemented within this framework, which is aligned with the KNCCAP. The warning. Rangeland management and climate change adaptation are not yet integrated into the medium term strategic plan of the Ministry of Environment and Forestry. The ASAL Secretariat has been established as a key Secretariat of the Common Program Framework for Ending Drought Emergencies.

### The EBA project will be implemented within this framework, which is aligned with the KNCCAP. The warning. Rangeland management and climate change adaptation are not yet integrated into the medium term strategic plan of the Ministry of Environment and Forestry. The ASAL Secretariat has been established as a key Secretariat of the Common Program Framework for Ending Drought Emergencies.

### This project responds to significant opportunities that have been created by the Kenyan government through adoption of key, favorable policies for sustainable rangelands management. The project will demonstrate implementation of EBA in the ASALs and will be implemented within the framework of the KNCCAP. It recognizes that pre-existing development failures in Kenya's ASALs leave populations more exposed to the risk of climate change impacts than they would have been if these areas had not experienced such rapid rates of climate variability. The project therefore strengthens climate change adaptation by reinforcing and enabling local institutions, improving access to information on the risks associated with climate change and rate of changes that are involved by climate change.

### C2.1.1 The Baseline Context in Kenya's Arid and Semi-Arid Lands (ASALs)
ASALs occupy 89% of Kenya and are home to about 38% of the population and 70% of the national livestock herd. This livestock sector contributes 5.6% to 12.0% of GDP and 30% to 4.4% of agricultural GDP, estimates

# FINANCING / COST INFORMATION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (PAGE 5 OF 76)

The ASALs are constrained by weak market penetration in the ASALs and poor data collection, but applying 70% to the livestock value chain would increase the income of smallholder farmers. The majority of the red meat consumed in Kenya comes from livestock raised in the ASALs. In pastoral production systems where animals graze on natural vegetation, the land is not used for other purposes such as agriculture or urbanization.

The ASALs are home to 90% of the wildlife that supports the country's thriving tourism sector (contributing more than $1 billion annually).

Of the 23 counties in the ASALs in Kenya, 9 counties are classified as Arid and, 14 as Semi-Arid. The ASAL ecosystems are characterized by low, erratic annual precipitation between 150mm to 550mm per year and areas of high evaporation rates (up to 2300 mm/year). These conditions make the ASALs highly vulnerable to climate change.

Kenya includes most of the lands forecast to be at very high-risk (38.1%), and at high risk (47.4%) of climate change impacts, as shown in Figure 1.

## Precipitation Projections and Evidence

Precipitation projections and evidence are inconsistent, with models projecting increases in some ASAL areas while others project decreases. The observed increase in temperatures has been linked to climate change.

### Climate Change and Drought
- **Source:** Meangi & Mufa 2015


## Evaporation Rates and Climate Variability

Evaporation rates have increased by up to 30% over the past decade, with some areas experiencing an even stronger link between climate change and observed increases in land degradation and drought events. Such evapotranspiration has led to a decrease in soil moisture content and increase in surface runoff and flood.

The frequency of drought has probably increased beyond the recovery period (5 years) for pastoralists systems in Kenya, with some areas experiencing more than 10 consecutive dry seasons. This is supported by other data, such as evapotranspiration and also land degradation to further strengthen Kenya's capacity to analyze its data set and develop response measures.

### Climate Change Impact on Kenya
- Kenya’s population of about 48 million at the high rate of 2.9% per year.
- About 10 million people suffer from food insecurity and poor nutrition with 2–4 million requiring emergency food assistance at any given time.

with proportionally more people affected in ASALs. Kenya's Food Security Steering Group estimated in 2017 that 25% of the population is food insecure, with 34% of children under five chronically malnourished. The most affected are counties in the east, with more than 50% of the population at risk, followed by Marsabit, Tana River and Samburu (both in the north), Turkana (in the northwest), Baringo, Nakuru, Elgeyo Marakwet, Isiolo, Mandera (28%), and Turkana and Keiwe (25%) (FEWS NET, 2017). The project counties are both pastoralists and agropastoralists. In the north, the ASALs include parts of Marsabit, Tana River and Samburu. Tana river meander and Chyulu landscapes comprise a mixed population of both pastoralists and agropastoralists.

Drought emergencies have an impact on economic growth in Kenya. The 2011 drought, for example, reduced GDP by about $3 billion (approximately 4% of the total GDP). This is equivalent to a loss of approximately 500,000 jobs in the sector. As mentioned in C1, climate change is increasing the frequency of drought in Kenya, which reduces the amount of rainfall and increases temperatures. The number of days with no rain has increased since 2008 and this frequency presents a serious risk to the resilience of the pastoral sector.

Economic development throughout Kenya is impacting on the ASALs, but in the absence of a clear understanding of how these changes will impact pastoral livelihoods, there are significant risks associated with maladaptation. Current development paradigms are poorly informed by the current knowledge on risk management and climate change adaptation strategies. The combination of economic development and climate change will probably aggravate that uncertainty. Without understanding this critical aspect of climate change and its impacts in ASALs, it is difficult to design appropriate policies and programs for livestock herding strategies that are designed to manage risk.

Many commercial projects are planned within the proposed LAPSSET corridor, which will involve the constructions of roads, railway, air, fiber optics transport and communication systems, electricity, oil pipelines and resort cities. These developments will have significant impacts on pastoral livelihoods in the ASALs; however, they also present numerous risks to the sustainable management of rangeland landscapes and to climate change adaptation.

The Kenyan government has major concerns over the state of degradation in the country's rangelands. Degradation of rangeland resources undermines the capacity of rangeland users to adapt to climate change and reduces their resilience to droughts, floods and other extreme weather events.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 7 OF 76

Action Program to Combat Desertification estimates land degradation at an average of 65% in pure ASAL counties. Overgrazing, poor soil management practices, and the conversion of natural grasslands into grazing lands have compromised regeneration of rangelands, leaving large expanses of natural pastureland degraded or lost.

Kerria does not systematically collect data on land degradation in the ASALs and the above figures are quite crude estimates, which places a major constraint on planning and investment. However, the National Drought Management Authority (NDMA) has identified four key drivers of land degradation: i) persistent drought and deteriorating vegetation condition across all livelihood zones; ii) increasing grazing pressure due to population growth and migration; iii) poor soil management practices such as overgrazing and successive failed rains; iv) low yields of shallow wells attributed to reduced recharge potential and; v) poor infrastructure, including the lack of boreholes and piped water systems in rural areas, which extend distances to water sources (NDMA, 2017).

Rangelands are a critical component of ASALs' soil carbon stocks, key indicator of soil health and regeneration potential, as critical low (below 3.5 kg/ha) in some northern rangelands. Given the scale of the ASALs, the degradation of these lands has significant implications for climate change adaptation. Rangeland restoration actions not only contribute to climate change adaptation but have the potential to make a significant contribution to carbon sequestration.

The impacts of climate change have led to more people depending on survival strategies to reinforce their livelihoods. This includes unsustainable practices, such as overharvesting trees for charcoal. Casualties of climate change include loss of livestock and crops due to drought and floods. In the absence of adequate land use planning, these people frequently adopt land use practices that compete against pastureland regeneration and soil health. These strategies are often adopted in response to economic pressures from farming. Such examples are on-going responses to a range of social and economic pressures that could contribute to further degradation of rangelands. This is why it is important to consider established adaptation strategies. Climate change impacts are forecast to further compound the already-fragile situation in these areas, with significant changes in the way rangeland landscapes are managed and governed (Kabare et al 2018* Nassef et al., 2009).

Current responses to drought respond poorly to climate change projections. Responses are constrained by the lack of adequate information systems to inform rangeland management and adaptation planning and to provide investment opportunities for sustainable land management, particularly in the context of the expansion of the rangelands.

At the same time, local governance systems, which hold the key to climate change adaptation, are being weakened due to loss of authority over resource and low recognition from outside actors. A significant concentration of resources is required to address drought responses in these fragile and complex landscapes. As a result, interventions responding to drought have frequently undermined drought management. The most vulnerable communities are those living on communal lands, which are often the least productive lands, which have led to unplanned settlement and high, sustained pressure on pasture resources, leading to their degradation. This has been exacerbated by poor land-use planning and lack of understanding of climate patterns which sometimes results in land degradation. Experiences with water infrastructure potential in drylands have also been mixed, with some projects being successful while others have failed. Limited hydro-meteorological data is often inadequate or impossible for project making, working over-estimates likely.

A crucial opportunity exists to strengthen local governance and build the capacity of communities to effectively use natural resources. This would provide a new foundation for development and an opportunity to strengthen local government, but this will not happen on its own. Past decades have seen eroded customary authority while the new Land Act has not been implemented and capacities are weak to secure natural resources. As a result, critical communal responses that underpin adaptation will continue to be eroded.

Land use planning in these areas can be complex as land tends to be used communally and seasonally by the majority of pastoralists. In addition, there are no comprehensive land-use plans for these areas. While new county-wide planning processes have yet to take place due to cost and capacity, and very little land-use planning has been provided to counties so far; with that which has been developed taking a participatory focus.

Despite this, in developing countries such as Kenya there are opportunities for more locally appropriate and integrated planning, but the lack of comprehensive plans means that these opportunities have not yet been fully realized. In addition, land use—having been impartially informed of the potential and alternative uses—other levels of governments can then make decisions on how to best utilize their resources. This is a key challenge in Kenya.

Participatory developed plans will need to be integrated with and support government planning processes, rather than substituting them. The participatory process will also provide an opportunity for more locally appropriate land-use planning processes at different levels but also makes it a necessity. This would allow for better integration of the various sectors involved in the development of these areas, including those in the transition scenario.

A further consequence of drought, which is exacerbated by climate change, is that herders have become locked into humanitarian response as the expense of overall economic development. As a result, penetration of livestock insurance and other financial services has been limited to date. This has also meant that there are few finance and investment and as a result value chains are poorly developed. There has been some progress in recent years with increased investment but investment in other commodities—both livestock and non-livestock goods and services - remains very low.

## C2.1.2 Climate impacts problem statement:

### Climate change is increasing the frequency and severity of drought events in Kenya's ASALs while contributing, directly and indirectly, to degrading the natural resources on which ASAL communities depend

Pastoralism is an adaptation to the natural conditions of ASALs, including high climate variability and aridity. Climate change is expected to increase both the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, with mechanisms to manage them. Traditional patterns of mobility are growing inadequate as key resource areas are decreasing in size and quality. This has led to increased competition for resources between pastoralists who migrate to new areas. Changing climate patterns are also modifying the rangeland landscape, contributing to localized desertification and degradation of land cover. These changes have a direct impact on livestock grazing in the key drought reserve areas.

Land degradation is caused by climate change as well as a host of other factors (e.g., economic and demographic drivers). Land degradation reduces capacity for climate change adaptation by undermining established strategies, reducing ecosystem services, and increasing vulnerability to extreme weather events. The consequences of land degradation in the rangelands is reduction in vegetation, and particularly grasses. This reduces the available resources for livestock grazing and increases competition between pastoralists as they migrate to new areas during the rains. A further consequence of land degradation is reduction in soil organic carbon, which not only affects the ability of soils to retain water but also makes them more susceptible to erosion. In addition, it leads the soil to store moisture. Climate change therefore not only contributes to meteorological drought (through periods of low rainfall) but also contributes to a trend towards desertification.

In the absence of land use planning in rangeland landscapes, land degradation is managed and pastoralists drop out (those who have lost livestock herds during drought for example) as well as people relocating from other areas into these areas. This has led to increased competition between pastoralists and farmers for resources, including changing competition for land and water and the emergence of new state institutions with parallel or overlapping mandates. These changes are also leading to a reduction in access to resources such as water and access and affecting the scarcity of resource use. Competition and conflict over resources among pastoralists and farmers is increasing, particularly during droughts when there is increased pressure on communal lands, especially women and youth.

Pastoralists have traditionally developed detailed devices designed to manage and conserve key resources such as pastures and water sources. Dry season grazing areas are a critical element of traditional land management systems in ASALs. These areas provide livestock with access to forage during the dry season when forage and water for livestock inevitably become scarce. Wet season pastures usually provide sufficient food for livestock, but these areas can be severely affected by drought, meaning that livestock can access even during severe droughts. Pastures designed for use by "home herds" (i.e., milking animals, young, weak or orphan livestock) are close to permanent settlements and sources of water, and

sometimes allocated to individual households rather than being managed at the community level. In the dry season, livestock are often sold or slaughtered in order to meet household food and cash needs.

However, these traditional pastoral production systems are under threat from increasingly erratic rainfall, drying grasslands, rapid population growth, restrictions on household and herd mobility, pastoralists becoming more sedentary, and increasing competition for resources. These challenges have led to the development of new models that incorporate newer links among globalisation, local markets, migration and economic opportunities—creating new dimensions in livelihoods and resource management. These are now an integral part of today’s pastoral households and are reshaping the practices of pastoralism in Kenya today.

### Information and capacity gaps limit the options of critical stakeholders for climate change adaptation (i.e. low access to information, weak capacity to act on the information that is available; weak capacity to influence rangeland planning across different sectors).

#### Land users have for many years lacked security of governance to implement sustainable rangeland management practices. The situation has improved with the establishment of stronger institutions to coordinate communal rangeland management; absence of mechanisms for coordinated planning of land use and water resources, as well as lack of integration of climate change into the 2013 Land Act and the 2016 Communities Land Act, the situation is improving and providing opportunities to improve the situation.

#### Rangeland management planning is not cognizant of future climate change scenarios (i.e. low access to information, weak capacity to act on the information that is available; weak capacity to influence rangeland planning across different sectors).

#### Critical water resources are overexploited and no longer support livestock production, seasonal pastures turn into other lands uses, pastures degraded through permanent/extended grazing, lack of alternative livelihoods for pastoralists. The situation is improving with the establishment of stronger institutions to coordinate communal rangeland management; absence of mechanisms for coordinated planning of land use and water resources.

#### Investments in rangelands are insufficient but in other cases such as land degradation it is reversible. In the case of permanent/extended grazing, the situation is irreversible without alternative livelihoods for pastoralists.

#### Investments in rangelands are insufficient and inappropriate to support adaptation (i.e. large-scale measures for livestock production and non-livestock products; lack of access to markets; low awareness of best practices; effective management of land and water to mitigate drought).

#### Poor infrastructure, limited access to credit and inputs, poor marketing systems for livestock products and non-livestock products; low availability of finance for local enterprises; low awareness of positive externalities and economic benefits of rangeland resources; lack of alternative livelihoods for pastoralists.

#### In the existing scenario (i.e. no GCF project) focus on agricultural development without considering the consequences of climate change, or the impact of crop sector development on pastoralists adaptation to climate change. The project will address these gaps and provide a framework that maximizes the potential for sustainable development outcomes. GCF support is therefore needed to make investments in socio-economic infrastructure and services for pastoralists.

### C.2.2 Alternative Scenario Proposed by this Project

The theory of change (see Figure 6) is designed to address the barriers listed in the preceding sub-section, in line with Kenya's institutional arrangements at national and county levels. The project will contribute to ensuring adaptation to climate change through improved information and capacity combined with stronger institutions.

#### Sustainable development will be centered on landscape-scale management of rangelands that is informed by climate change scenarios. At the community level, communities will have access to land use plans in these areas (land occupy and high number of pastoralists who manage them communally), and who may have provided inputs into the planning process. The project will provide a framework for improved governance at the community and inter-community level and at the level of county governments. Counties will develop the mechanisms that will enable communities to access land use plans, and to participate in decision-making on land reserves.

#### In the alternative scenario delivered through the project, improved information and capacities combined with stronger institutions will enable rangeland users to adapt more effectively to climate change. They will allow both better planning of land use and water resources; increased access to markets for pastoralists; greater accountability. Increased access to markets, through stronger value chains and improved access to finance, will increase the income of pastoralists. The project will provide a framework that enables communities to participate in decision-making on land reserves, and to provide inputs. The outcomes will include increased food and water security and greater income security. These outcomes contribute directly to poverty reduction.

#### Improved monitoring of rangeland health combined with improved analysis of climate change will enable communities and county governments to prioritize and implement appropriate restoration measures. This will include the development of land use plans that are informed by climate change scenarios, and which avoid herd movements for natural regeneration.

#### Improved monitoring will also have implications in the water sector, including siting of strategic and low-yield water resources that are commensurate with the associated pasture availability. Restoration of rangeland vegetation will

# C.3 Project / Programme Description

## Target landscapes

In rangeland management, the term "landscape" describes vast areas of nature that are managed by people and therefore influenced by human activities. A landscape approach is adopted at the scale of human management, which in the case of Kenya's ASALs may be areas of tens of thousands of square kilometers, in which the land use patterns are determined by the availability of water resources and other environmental factors.

These systems define simplistic management solutions or the imposition of easily measurable boundaries, and the landscape approach is more appropriate for addressing complex issues such as those described above. Within Kenya's ASALs, dry season grazing zones create islands of resources that are seasonally indispensable to pastoralists. These areas have a particularly important role during drought, as discussed above (check). The primary criteria for selection of the priority landscapes was, therefore, that they include an important dry season grazing area.

## Figure 5a Concept for the selection of priority landscapes focused on a dry season grazing area

Extensive consultation was required to determine the landscapes for intervention in this project. Originally seven such landscapes were identified, and finally two were selected with one being a cluster of two interlinked landscapes in semi-arid northern Kenya and the other including major dry season grazing zones, one in semi-arid southern Kenya and the second on the inland lands.

The landscapes met the following criteria:

- Areas that lie on or between wet season reserves and dry season grazing areas cutting across county boundaries.
- Retain traditional land management systems that are still functioning, at least partially.

### Objective and components in response to identified barriers

The GCF Fund-level project objectives for this proposal are improved resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services, enhanced livelihoods, and increased adaptive capacity at the landscape level. These objectives will be achieved through three components (A1.0) and regions (A1.0). The expected outcomes are strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks (AS.5), improved water management in anticipation of droughts (AS.6), and enhanced value-chain production and markets for livestock and other products (AS.8).

The overall objective of the project is to reduce the cost of climate change-induced drought on Kenya's national economy by improving resilience at landscape level, with a focus on small-scale users in restored and effectively governed rangeland ecosystems.

### The objectives are met through three components as outlined in Section H:

- **Climate change adapted planning for drought resilience**: Improved water management.
- **Restoration of rangeland landscapes for ecosystem based adaptation**: Enhanced value-chain production and markets for livestock and other products.
- **Community engagement and capacity building**: Increased adaptive capacity.

These components respond to the priorities raised during consultation with partners and key stakeholders. The components will be implemented in parallel to each other, focusing on improved water management in anticipation of droughts, enhanced value-chain production and markets for livestock and other products.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 18 OF 76

The key to ecosystem-based adaptation in Kenya's ASALs is to adopt restoration and sustainable management approaches that are cost-effective, scalable, and can be implemented on a landscape scale. These landscapes cover several thousand square kilometers and may involve tens of thousands of people. EBA approaches also have to be developed to deal with the two-way relationship between climate change and land degradation, which together contribute to increased vulnerability in these areas.

EBA provides an unique opportunity to ensure that Kenya's national commitment to "Eroding Drought" Environmental Programme (EDP) is fully implemented through landscape restoration. The project builds on approaches that have been piloted in Kenya but which have not yet gone to scale. It takes a landscape approach to restoration, recognizing that mismanagement of zones within a rangeland landscape can have significant impacts on the entire landscape.

The project will address the two-way relationship between climate change and land degradation, which together contribute to increased vulnerability in these areas. The project will also provide lessons for scaling up EBA approaches across Kenya's economy and will generate lessons that will inform the development of similar initiatives in many countries in Sub-Saharan Africa.

## Component 1: Climate change adapted planning for drought resilience

### Output 1: Coordinated transboundary rangeland management decisions are strengthened through landscape-scale planning (including land use and county planning)

The component contributes to addressing the barriers of weak capacities and inadequate governance institutions. The component will be led by the National Drought Management Authority.

#### Output 2: Enhanced information systems to inform climate change sensitive landscape planning and vulnerability assessment

This output will provide capacity building for local institutions to implement climate-sensitive landscape management Component 1 provides capacity building for local institutions to implement climate-sensitive landscape management (Component 1) through a range of tools and approaches. The component will be led by the National Drought Management Authority.

### Output 3: Strengthened community landscapes for ecosystem-based adaptation

The component will be led by the Government of Kenya, through the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation – State Department of Livestock and will deliver Output 2: Prioritized rangeland resources including water resources, are identified and prioritized at landscape scale. This primarily addresses the barriers of rangeland resource degradation and low investment in rangeland restoration and sustainable management actions.

The component will be led by the Government of Kenya, through the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation – State Department of Livestock and will deliver Output 3: Community-led landscapes for ecosystem-based adaptation

#### Output 4: Implement priority community-based rangeland restoration activities

This output will provide capacity building for local institutions to implement climate-sensitive landscape management (Component 1) through a range of tools and approaches. The component will be led by the National Drought Management Authority.

#### Output 5: Install community-validated strategic water sources for sustainable rangeland utilization

The component will be led by the Government of Kenya, through the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation – State Department of Livestock and will deliver Output 4: Implement priority community-based rangeland restoration activities. The component will also support integrated land/water management in Component 2.

#### Output 6: Apply landscape-scale approaches to sustainable rangeland regeneration

The component will be led by the Government of Kenya, through the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation – State Department of Livestock and will deliver Output 5: Install community-validated strategic water sources for sustainable rangeland utilization. The component will also support integrated land/water management in Component 2.

#### Output 7: Build capacity of local institutions to implement climate-sensitive landscape management

The component will be led by the National Drought Management Authority and will deliver Outputs 4, 5, and 6.

### C

**catchments, recognizing that a critical element in drought management is to protect scarce water resources by improving their quality and quantity. This will be achieved through the implementation of catchment-based soil conservation practices such as stone lines and earth bunds. Rangeland restoration will be supported by implementing agroforestry systems (e.g., tree planting, shrub establishment) on degraded rangelands (with limited duration bore holes e.g. closed during critical periods) adjusted to the seasonal availability of adjacent pasture lands and water sources.**

**A crucial element of Component 2 is to develop bylaws (through facilitated community planning) or other local resource management agreements that legitimize customary practices of resource sharing and regulation. Communities will be supported in developing these bylaws, which will provide a legal framework for managing resources, but they have come under strain due to a multitude of changes, including changing resource patterns as a result of climate change. The development of these bylaws will also support the implementation of measures under strengthening accountability and compliance over resource management as well as strengthening local governance structures in order to ensure that communities are able to manage their resources effectively.**

**An important aspect of Component 2 is to strengthen the capacity of local institutions to implement climate-sensitive land use planning, including the development of a landscape-scale land-use plan that addresses climate change scenarios in relation to rangeland landscape resource use. It also includes developing skills to negotiate for access to and management of resources within the wider rangeland landscape and as a viable component of the Kenyan economy at large.**

It is important to note here that components 1 and 2 are to be implemented in parallel and not consecutively. Nonetheless, it will be necessary to ensure that the activities under Component 1 are completed before they can be carried out in sequence, although this could have limitations. For example the establishment of a women's group for livestock value chains may need to be done prior to the development of bylaws which would then be decided as part of a community land-use plan. Tying the women's groups to the completion of the land-use plan will ensure that they are not left behind in terms of access to resources and services, which could be an impediment to the project to progress on one element before proceeding to another.

**Component 3: Establish restoration enterprises and value chains**

* **Component 3 will deliver Output Q. "Public, private and community investments in natural resources," addressing barriers related to insufficient investment in rangelands and poor access to markets and financial services. The component will support the development of agroforestry systems (e.g., tree planting, shrub establishment) chains that have been validated by local communities through Component 1. The value chains will be supported for the restoration enterprises established under Component 2 as well as other existing enterprises within the value chains that are dependent on them.**

* **Component 3 will establish investment in priority value chains that have been validated by local communities**
    * **Establishment to establish restoration enterprises created primarily by women's groups**
    * **Establish financial incentive mechanisms for sustainable land management**
    * **Promote and support the development of a market-based adaptation that could create opportunities for investments in the value chain**

**Priority value chains in the target landscapes include livestock value chains –for example meat and milk- as well as value chains for the secondary products of healthy rangeland ecosystems, such as gums and medicinal plants. The component will also support the development of agroforestry systems that provide alternative sources of income, which contributes to mitigating risks associated with climate change. Workshops on value chain analysis will be held in each target landscape to identify priority value chains and potential market opportunities for these actors to be assisted to evaluate and the value chain analysis, assess the potential further development of the value chains and develop a business plan for the enterprises that are established under Component 3.**

**Component 3 will establish restoration enterprises, such as community grass seed multiplication units or fodder reserves.**

It will additionally develop the community-level autonomous Community Restoration Facilitator, which operate per village and are a governance and financial mechanism to incentivize sustainable land management through community-based payments for ecosystem services (CBPS). The facilitators are trained in CBPS and founded on a well-known village savings groups and is coupled with the participatory rangeland catchment plans (CPCPs) developed under Component 1. The facilitators will be responsible for the implementation of the CPCPs and given to community members according to their endorsement and household level compliance with the component activities. The facilitator will also be responsible for the collection of payments from communities, from GCF funds, is used to establish and capitalize the solidarity-saving fund, and becomes a long-term and growing asset at village level, with the resulting funds delivery mechanism the most desirable and

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL - PAGE 20 OF 79

An efficient mechanism to ensure all households benefit is a combination of a 5-year well-established and near universal model for community-based enterprises. This model has been tried and tested and operational for over 5 years and now being used in 4 countries.

Building on the participatory planning at community and landscape level (Output 1) and the value chain assessment, the project will support the development of a business model that can be replicated by communities in, and associated with, the priority landscapes for supporting activities on sustainable land management and ecosystem restoration. The business model will include the following elements: i) a viable business model; ii) restoration enterprises that could create opportunities for investments in grass, gums, resins, livestock and other value added products; iii) an appropriate packaging of the investment opportunity (e.g., through a turnkey package); iv) quality control equipment/ laboratory. Constraints include identifying 'investment ready' deals, ensuring that the business model is robust enough to attract private sector investors, and meeting donor and most investor expectations.

The model will be complementary to that developed under GCF's new kind of responsible investment vehicle to deploy climate funds to assist restoration and land management businesses to upgrade their infrastructure. Improved infrastructure will enable these enterprises to scale up their operations. The development of the GCF investment will be complementary and additional to CI loans, as it will focus on the supporting and enabling environment for private sector investments in sustainable land management and ecosystem restoration.

More specifically, the arrangement between GCF funded grants and CI loans are as follows:

* It will most commonly be the case that GCF funded grants alone are provided to early stage enterprises. In this scenario, a CI loan would only be considered if the business model could be followed by a CI loan, should the business develop and become investable (from a CI perspective).
* It is more likely that a CI investment follows a grant; rather than simultaneous investment.
* The relationship between activities linked to the objectives, outputs and outcomes are presented in the Financial Plan.

The grants will be in the size 10-25% depending on competitiveness, value added and capacity of the company. These will be used as climate-smart mechanisms to catalyze growth of community-based enterprises as well as the development of a business model for scaling up.

Examples of grants projects to be supported by this component are presented in Annex 12.

### C.1. Background Information on Project/Program Sponsor (Executing Entity)

**(a) The Government of Kenya, through Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (Component 2)**

The Government of Kenya (GoK), through the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MoAL), has responsibility over the agricultural sector including livestock development, crop production, horticulture, cash crops, farming lands and through all aspects of the agricultural value chains. The MoAL also has direct links with the main land users: farmers, pastoralists, agribusinesses, input suppliers, traders, processors, and consumers. The MoAL is responsible for the implementation of interventions that promote SME especially in rural areas.

The State Department for Livestock (SDL) provides policy direction to livestock and irrigation projects, promotes, regulates and facilitates livestock production for socioeconomic development and industrialization. The State Department for Livestock is composed of four departments: Veterinary Services, Livestock Policy, Research and Regulations, and Livestock Resources and Market Development.

In Kenya, the Livestock Sub-Sector is informed by the Agricultural Sector Development Strategy (ASDS) and the National Drought Management Authority Act 2016. The key objectives encapsulated in the Agricultural Sector Development Strategy include increasing livestock productivity; increasing market access for livestock products; improving livestock value chains; and enhancing livestock production systems. The Department of Livestock implements this strategy through functions such as animal production, range management, disease control, feed resources development, extension services, apiculture and emerging livestock production, value addition and agriculture.

**(b) National Drought Management Authority (Component 1)**

The National Drought Management Authority (NDMA) is a statutory body established under the NDMA Act 2016. The Act gives the NDMA the mandate to establish mechanisms which ensure drought does not result in emergency situations, and that it can be managed effectively by all stakeholders. The NDMA has developed a drought-prone ASAL counties using local offices for long-term planning and action, as well as coordination across Government agencies. The National Drought Management Authority (NDM) Conversion secretariat pivotal the devolved climate finance mechanism is located at the NDMA.

**(c) Conservation International (Component 3)**

Conservation International, one of the world's largest conservation organizations, has been working in Kenya for over 20 years and works with local partners in several of the project priority landscapes. CI aims to leverage the power of public-private partnerships to achieve its mission of building a sustainable future for people and nature through natural capital conservation, sustainable production, and improving governance and local buy-in to specific subnational initiatives. The project will support the implementation of the Kenya Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (KBASP) in the context of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). CI is working in Chulipu Hills supporting a successful REDD+ project. CI also works with communities to transition production and sourcing practices to be sustainable, and to achieve impact at scale.

## C.4. Project Financing, Taxation and Insurance (if applicable)

### Structure of the project financing

The IUCN is the Accredited Entity for this Project. IUCN will therefore be accountable to the GCF on the use of funds, the overall management of the project and the achievement of the project's outcomes. Consequently, IUCN is responsible for providing strong technical oversight to this project and ensuring that all fiduciary rules, including procurement and financial reporting, are adhered to in accordance with the GCF’s requirements as well as in compliance with the IUCN environmental and social management system, which will be applied and monitored during the entire project lifetime.

### Principles

The Principal Entity (IUCN) implementing the project function is dedicated to provide oversight to the project implementation.

The Accredited Entity function for this project will be undertaken by IUCN building on the expertise of its regional office for Eastern and Southern Africa based in Nairobi Kenya, that has experience on drylands management and general environmental management. The Project Management Unit (PMU) will be hosted by IUCN.

### As an Accredited Entity, IUCN's responsibilities are as follows:

- Receiving, managing and disbursing GCF funds to the Project Management Unit (PMU) and project executing Entities, ensuring that these disbursements are made so the project reaches successive milestones, showing results and achieving its outcomes.
- Overseeing the preparation of the Project Document and Annual Work Plans and Budgets, procurement plans, disbursement plans and agreements with co-financers and each executing agency for the execution of the project activities in accordance with the GCF’s requirements and the Project Management Unit.
- Scheduling and monitoring technical progress reports to the GCF for all Project funds received from the GCF based on the project progress reports that it clears from the submissions of the Project Management Unit, which is responsible for the preparation of these reports. This scheduling and monitoring done in consultation with the executing entities of the project.
- Carrying out mid-term reviews and a mid-term project review.
- Entering into Sub-agreements between IUCN and NDMA, MwA-SDL and Conservation International where applicable to ensure that the project is implemented as per the Project Document.
- Managing the Landscape Coordination Hubs in each of the Landscapes.
- Monitoring the project implementation through regular visits, financial reporting; and
- Providing technical guidance to ensure that the appropriate technical quality is applied to all Project activities.

### Funds flow mechanisms

IUCN, as the project Accredited Entity, will request funds to be transferred from the GCF based on the Project Management Unit disbursement plan. All Executing Entities will propose project disbursement requests to the Project Management Unit and IUCN will review these requests in accordance with the Project Document. Each of these Executing Entities (MwA, NDMA and CII) have a Sub-agreement with IUCN as the AE.

Executing Entity will enter into service agreements with service providers.

Project funds flow will be as per the below diagram. There will be three models of funds flow. These will be channelled through (a) Direct Disbursement at the landscape level by IUCN in its role as an Accredited Entity

The WRA will also provide technical support including expertise and approaches to restoration of key water towers in selected landscapes, and vii) The World Agroforestry Centre (ICRAF) will provide technical tools, approaches and processes essential to community and county-level planning for land restoration.

### Project Steering Committee

A project steering committee (PSC) will be established to provide strategic-level project guidance, technical and policy advice to the Project Management Unit. The PSC will meet twice per year and ad hoc when needed. Meetings of the PSC will be convened by the Chief of Party who heads the Project Management Unit.

The Project Steering Committee will review and advise on annual work plans and budgets submitted by the Project Management Unit, and provide guidance for the development of project strategies to strengthen project execution and the achievement of results targeted. Project Steering Committee recommendations will be based on a clear understanding of the project’s objectives, expected development results, best value for money, fairness, integrity, transparency and effective competition.

The Project Steering Committee will also monitor progress at the end of each agreed period based on annual project reports that the Project Management Unit submits to the PSC. The Steering Committee is

The duties of the PSC will include (but are not limited to):

5. Receive and outputs from comprehensive project evaluations and outcomes of annual supervision and midterm reviews.
6. Approve amendments to Project Management Unit work plans.

The PSC is responsible for i) make strategic orientation decisions ii) support resource mobilization iii) facilitate integration of the results of programme into policy processes and iv) consolidate the annual work plans, budgets and reports.

## Project Management Unit

The project will have a Project Management Unit (PMU), coordinated and managed by IUCN in partnership with and housed by the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation (MoA).

The PMU will be responsible for the coordination of all project activities funded by the project and undertaken by the executing entities. The PMU, comprised of technical experts will be constituted and specifically perform the following functions:

1. Consolidate the annual work plans submitted by the executing entities.
2. Responsible for all fiduciary matters, including financial management, procurement and project administration.
3. Ensure coordinated delivery of the agreed projects outputs and activities through the coordination of all partners involved in the project implementation.
4. Overall monitoring, evaluation, learning and application of knowledge products with the support of relevant experts involved in the project delivery.
5. Ensure that the safeguard framework is used throughout the project and raise any potential safeguards violations.
6. Ensure operational staff and supervise their work programs.

Given the geographic distribution of the project, the PMU will work with beneficiaries through three landscapes coordinated by the respective Landscape Kikwetis Teams.

The PMU will be composed as follows:

- 1 Country Lead (Chair), responsible for overall coordination and management
- 2 Landscape Coordinators
- 3 Programme Officers

# D1.Value Added for GCF Involvement

Grant support from the GCF is justified for this project due to the limited purposeful investment to address ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) and resilience in the ASALs. The project can build on existing investments in related works, as outlined under Section C, but initiatives are fragmented and often lack institutional support. There is a gap between the need for action and the ability of countries to respond effectively due to limited resources and weak socio-ecological systems. Devolution creates powerful opportunities for change, but the ASAL counties suffer from a legacy of poor governance, low levels of public service delivery, and high poverty rates.

A scenario without GCF funding will likely affect the vulnerability of communities and the ecosystems services that are generated from the surrounding landscape and the gap will grow between determining and implementing solutions to climate change. The project will help address this by providing support for investments in livelihoods with interventions that address the impact of weather patterns, namely drought, and the subsequent soil and water degradation.

GCF grant support will be used to strengthen resilience in the target landscapes and for ASAL populations and will help sustain economic development in the ASALs and nationally. GCF funds will help to create an enabling environment that promotes climate-resilient land use planning and management practices. This will reduce the vulnerability of communities on the landscape and in turn, face less risk of land degradation as a result of a vicious cycle of historical weather patterns and human activities. The project will also provide support for investments in livelihoods with interventions to address challenges to their livelihoods, whereas in the GCF scenario, linkages with markets to increase investment opportunities for communities.

In the GCF-funded scenarios, climate risk and resilience are brought into this complex situation. With GDP financing, climate risks and resilience will be addressed at national planning focused at the county level. Climate information and early warning are key for planning in a way that factors in climate risk and opportunities for resilience at community level. The focus on climate resilient land use and water management planning means that the country would identify ways of using the landscape in such a way as to reduce vulnerability to climate change.

Based on climate resilient planning, areas that would be at higher risk from climate change such as water sources and livelihoods will be protected against climate change. GCF funds that help improve the resilience of water sources and the ecosystems that generate them

The project will thus make targeted interventions to capitalize on opportunities that enhance climate resilience whether in terms of reducing vulnerability or increasing adaptive capacity. The project will also support investments for a resilient value chain, particularly among pastoralists. Food security and income generation are among the benefits of a GCF scenario.

The project will developing enabling conditions for scaling up of investment and engagement for resilience. The grant will contribute to risk from climate change in order to enable long term investment. Pastoralism provides an important contribution to the national economy—estimated at between 3.8 and 8% of GDP—and is the dominant economic activity in ASAL counties. The rewards from creating an enabling environment for resilient pastoralism will be long lasting in the ASALs.

Kenya’s Vision-2030 and the national policy of “Ending Drought Emergencies” provide a clear demonstration of the commitment to address climate change and its impacts on food security, water resources, and human health. The severity of drought, presenting a challenge to existing strategies for drought management. The added value of GCF investment in pastoralism should support management to climate change from the level individual land users up to county and national governments.

Despite a huge amount of effort and financial commitment by the government and development partners over many years, there is still a significant number of people living below the poverty line in ASAL counties. There remains an increasing number of relief-dependent people, increased poverty levels and vulnerability to climate shocks and stresses. This trend is influenced by climate change, which increases the frequency and severity of drought and

# D. Exit Strategy

The objective of the project is to establish a more enabling environment through reducing climate-related risks for investments in the countries concerned by the project and resilient pastoralism in particular. Enhancing these enabling conditions will contribute to achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the Green Climate Fund’s Strategic Objectives.

## 1. Institutional Sustainability

The project will build on existing institutional arrangements, including those of the ASALs, to establish a more enabling environment for climate-resilient pastoralism in the countries concerned by the project. The project will also contribute to achieving the SDGs and the Green Climate Fund’s Strategic Objectives.

## 2. Economic Sustainability

The project will enhance economic sustainability through reducing the impact of drought on GDP and strengthening recovery rates and by addressing the underlying causes of vulnerability, including climate change.

## 3. Data Analysis and Climate Change Predictions

New data analysis on climate change predictions and for inclusive decision-making will be used to inform adaptation strategies in the ASALs.

## 4. Adaptive Management Mechanisms

The project will establish adaptive management mechanisms and building capacity for sustainable rangeland management.

## 5. Institutional Arrangements and Capacity Building

The project will strengthen institutional arrangements and capacities for rangeland management, including through capacity-building initiatives.

## 6. Economic Growth and Resilience

Economic growth and resilience in the ASALs will be enhanced by reducing climate-related risks to investments and enhancing adaptive capacity of pastoralists as the basis for adaptation – both reducing the severity of climate-change induced natural hazards like drought (e.g., improved livestock productivity) and hence product market development in the dryland regions. The benefits will be enhanced economic growth, increased resilience to climate change, and poverty reduction.

## 7. Project Outcomes

The project contributes to strengthening the following dimensions of sustainability:

- Improved adaptive capacity of pastoralists through the establishment of landscape management mechanisms.
- Building capacity for sustainable rangeland management.
- Institutional sustainability by strengthening institutional arrangements and capacities for rangeland management.
- Economic sustainability by reducing the impact of drought on GDP and strengthening recovery rates and by addressing the underlying causes of vulnerability, including climate change.
- Data analysis and climate change predictions to inform adaptation strategies in the ASALs.

The project will make the case for continued government investment to build on the project outcomes.

# E.1 Impact Potential

## E.1.2 Project Impacts and Alignment with GCF Adaptation Logic Model and Performance Indicators

This section outlines the project impacts and their alignment with the GCF's Adaptation Logic model and performance indicators. Impacts are listed in order of relevance to the project.

### Level I Impact A.0: Improved resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services.

#### Indicator 4.1: Coverage of ecosystems protected/rehabilitated against climate variability and change

Target: 50% of degraded land (1 million ha), which turns in repairs 40% of the total landscape (2,500 km²). Restoration will be achieved through rehabilitation e.g., as shrubland regeneration such as energizing and land degradation through revegetation. Through improved livestock management (e.g., improved grazing, fire and water management). The project will target the equivalent of the Land Degradation Neutrality commitment area in the two landscapes. These degradation processes are balanced by an equivalent area of land under improved management leading to restoration.

#### Indicator 1.2: Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions

Target: 5% of men and women in the target landscapes (150,000 people) reporting enhanced, more resilient livelihoods (e.g., a more resilient livestock production, diversification of income, increased access to markets etc.) as a result of project implementation (e.g. improved livestock products, harvesting and processing natural products).

## E.2. Paradigm Shift Momentum

Degree to which the proposed activity can catalyze impact beyond a single project-programme investment


### E.2.1. Current State

In the current paradigm, climate change information is centralized but it not widely available at local level. Capacities at the local level are comparatively weak, both institutionally and in terms of human resources, and there is limited access to information on climate change impacts and adaptation options.

### E.2.2. Proposed Activity

This project takes advantage of the devolution process in Kenya to strengthen capacities and institutions at the county level for landscape restoration and SLM actions. The project will also build capacity at the local level through cost-effectiveness by engaging community institutions in resilience building, strengthening community-based resource management systems that are more effective for community-led solutions.

The project will contribute to development of local resource agreements, bylaws and policies, including ones to address climate change risks, in all 25 target landscapes. The project will build cross-institutional collaboration in the long term, including through input to policy frameworks.


### E.2.3. Impact

Kenya has 80% of its ASALs—the regions that are most affected by climate change—and these regions are the most underdeveloped in the country, which compromises adaptive capacity. To secure a future in which resilient rangelands and livestock systems can be maintained, it is essential to strengthen capacities for landscape restoration and management in the ASALs. This transformation requires a paradigm shift away from treating rangelands as low value areas that are best left alone or only used for grazing, towards recognizing their potential for providing ecosystem services and the inherent adaptability and economic rationality of pastoralism as a system for managing extreme climate events.

The project will demonstrate how to scale up good practices that have been proven on a small scale but which need greater institutional support to be more widely adopted. In doing so the project will pave the way for comprehensive adoption of landscape restoration and SLM actions in Kenya’s ASALs, with lessons learned from these experiences to inspire similar approaches in other rangeland countries of Sub-Saharan Africa and even in other regions where arid and semi-arid conditions prevail.

The project will develop innovative financial instruments that specifically targetland restoration action and which have a strong focus on social inclusion. Access to finance is generally weak in the ASALs, particularly for natural resource management projects. The project will support the development of new financing mechanisms that provide access to resources and is tailored towards ensuring investment in natural resource management and conservation activities. This will be achieved by engaging with financial institutions, including microfinance institutions, in the natural resources sector, where entry barriers act as a constraint to scaling up.

The alternate model of Ecosystem-based Adaptation in rangeland ecosystems empowers improved access and use of resources for communities living within these landscapes. The project will support stronger community organizations to coordinate sustainable use of communal resources, and strengthened value chains and investments to support livelihoods.

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 34 OF 76

### Diversification and Incentivize Restoration Actions

Among the strengths of this approach are more secure land tenure, more secure access to seasonal grazing areas, protection of drought reserves, restored pastures in key landscapes, secured water resources, and improved livelihoods for pastoralists.

This project offers potential for replicating and scaling up by:

- Leveraging countries: The project will build capacities, incentives, institutions, and knowledge management at the county level and beyond. A shared methodology will be used to support landscape planning for restoration and protection.
- Knowledge management component: Will explicitly focus on institutionalization at country and national levels, and will support capacity building of key stakeholders in the development of a National Landscape Strategy (NLS) and scaling up the capacities of the recently devolved county governments, where there is clear demand, opportunity and potential for expansion.

### Platforms to take off

A major boost to take-off in other locations is the proposed reliance on models and approaches tested in similar context but that need fine tuning for this application. The project will scale up with the following experiences of existing initiatives:

- **NADMA** has developed drought-resilience practices in all 16 ASAL counties that will be scaled up.
- **The SHARED methodology focuses on changing behavior and building long-term capacity in evidence-based and participatory landscape management approaches.**
- **KEFIS** has extensive experience in gums and resin enterprise development in the ASALs.
- **LACHA** has developed a model for integrated land use planning, including livestock grazing, agroforestry, and conservation.
- **Conservation International** has worked with private sector and civil society to develop various interventions in landscape management, such as water harvesting, soil and water conservation, agroforestry, seed bank enterprises, and fodder production.

The project will also leverage changes in planning and decision-making around annual budgeting and planning, incorporating climate and land health data and community priorities to accelerate resilience returns on investment.

### Leveraging other initiatives

Existing experiences in landscape-scale Ecosystem-Based Adaptation (EbA) in Kenya could inform a coalition on which to build the project and expand out into other jurisdictions. Climate Smart Agriculture projects have also been implemented in some of these counties, with lessons learned that can be applied here. The project will also draw from several existing policy initiatives like the high-profile Born Challenge for restoration, in which each country sets a target for number of hectares planned by 2030 or earlier date.

### Potential for knowledge and learning

The project will support the development of collective learning, knowledge generation and dissemination at community, landscape county and national levels (Fig. 15). Participatory approaches, action learning and most significant change methodologies will underpin the collective learning process.

### Stakeholder engagement and information sharing for enhanced decision making

Decision-making will be supported by a visualized database in the form of a Climate Resilience Dashboard (Component 1). This is based on a landscape-scale land degradation assessment that will be conducted at the county level, with a focus on each of the four landscapes. It is underpinned by the Land Degradation Surveillance Frameworks (LDSF), which has a primary objective to provide an early warning system for drought and other climate-related hazards such as erosion, soil carbon, infiltration capacity, vegetation cover). The project will analyze data (livestock, water, conflict, sentinel sites) to inform landscape-scale land degradation planning. Participatory co-design of visually accessible information will enhance uptake and action.

### Stakeholder forums, extension and outreach

Stakeholder forums will be established in each landscape to provide a platform for dialogue on the project’s implementation and outcomes. The participatory approach will be augmented with broader outreach based on large-ranch radio programmes. Specifically, prepared presentations will be made by key stakeholders such as county government officials, pastoralists, extension services, primarily via pastoralist field schools and water resource users associations, will provide information, communication and education.

### Drought early warning and action information

Under Component 1, real-time information will be shared through a mobile phone messaging network to community leaders, CBOs, NGOs and outposted government staff. Messages will include restoration, land management and extension material. The system will be operated from the information centre.
