### Planning
Participatory approaches will be followed for co-designing visually accessible and simplified information to enhance awareness.

### Training in land systems management for climate resilience:
Under Component I, the project will train county, ward, and landscape-level actors in integrated systems management for climate resilience. Training will include interactive use of a decision support tool (DST) that is linked with a dashboard. The DST will be hosted on the landscape level web, with data dashboards into planning processes and data management.

### A Resilience Diagnostic and Decision Support Tool (RDST)
The RDST will provide an integrated land systems management approach for climate resilience. It will be visually accessible by any additional data from government (The Government of Kenya through the MoSLD, NODA, KERI, National Environment Management Authority (NEMA), County governments, CSOs, ICAWTs and bilateral agencies e.g. GIZ Uganda) with full attribution to enhance resilience for climate change.

### Land Degradation Surveillance Framework
The land component of the RDST is underpinned by the Land Degradation Surveillance Framework (LDSF). The LDSF will provide a framework for monitoring, mapping, and reporting on a range of indicators of ecosystem health (e.g. erosion, soil carbon, infiltration capacity, vegetation cover).

## E 2.3: Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment
The project will contribute to strengthening the enabling environment for implementing the national devolution process in order to strengthen ecosystem-based adaptation and drought resilience strategies in the ASALs.

### Project focus areas:
The project focuses on the ASALs where climate change is already a reality, with increasing frequency and intensity of climate-related events. The project will support initiatives that address elements of climate change resilience but also contribute towards sustainable land management practices to reduce vulnerability to climate change and mitigate drought itself (reducing the frequency and severity of drought by restoring hydrological cycles and soil moisture).

### Project activities:
The project will strengthen local institutions that can act as intermediaries between private investors and markets and build their capacity in terms of financial, technical, and legal skills. This will contribute to sustainable land management and economic growth.

### Landscape-level initiatives:
Landscape-level initiatives will be developed with the aim of strengthening the enabling environment for implementing landscape-level strategies. These include:

- **Restoration of degraded landscapes**: By restoring rangeland ecosystems as the foundation of pastoral livelihoods, the project will contribute towards reducing vulnerability to climate change and mitigating drought itself (reducing the frequency and severity of drought by restoring hydrological cycles and soil moisture).
- **Clean rights**: Clean rights can mitigate conflicts that arise over land boundaries and land use change, and ensure more equitable access to resources. The project will support initiatives that address elements of climate change resilience but also contribute towards sustainable land management practices.
- **Landscape planning**: Landscape planning strengthens the enabling environment by giving confidence to investors (including communities themselves) about the long-term vision for land use and investments in restoration and sustainable land management.

### Economic opportunities:
Economic opportunities will provide potential stakeholders with greater clarity on the opportunities and potential rewards of engagement and investment. They allow the public sector to track the impacts of investment on social, economic, environmental, and climate resilience outcomes.

### Climate information and resource centers:
Climate information and resource centers will integrate local and scientific sources of information on the state of land, soil, water and biological resources, and drivers of change. These will be hosted at the landscape level, with web dashboards into planning processes and data management.

### Community Resilience Facility:
The Community Resilience Facility will provide a platform for community-level initiatives that address elements of climate change resilience but also contribute towards sustainable land management. The Community Resilience Facility is one financial mechanism to build capacity in communities to implement landscape-level strategies, including the development of market-based and business-savvy plans to boost production and quality of natural products from restored landscapes.

### Small grants:
Small grants will also be considered for other community-based enterprise value chain actions.

### E2.4 Contribution to regulatory framework and policies

The project contributes to implementing Kenya's National Adaptation Plan (NAP), which prioritizes providing climate change adaptation services and support for communities to implement priority climate change actions.

The goal of the Kenya's National Adaptation Program (NAP) is enhanced climate resilience towards the attainment of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The NAP was developed in 2016, and its implementation plan was defined and costed for short term (1-2 years), medium term (3-5 years) and long term (>4 years). The priority actions for each sector are listed below. These priorities were aligned with the Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP) and are presented in the following table, clearly demonstrating how the project is aligned with the national priorities.

| Conduct capacity building in indigenous knowledge, livestock insurance schemes, early warning systems, animal health management and breeding |

### The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) builds on the National Climate Change Response Strategy (NCORS) National Climate Change Action Plan (NCACP), and is the basis for Kenya's NDCs response to the Paris Agreement. The MAP builds on the Adaptation Technical Analysis Report (ATAR) developed under the NCACAP, which provided a detailed assessment of climate change impacts in Kenya and identified adaptation options. The NAP was aligned with the Climate Change Act enacted into law in May 2016. In the MTP sector, climate change adaptation is responsible for mainstreaming and ending drought emergencies, environment, water, energy, agriculture, livestock, and fisheries sectors.

Kenya's intended Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) prohibits mainstreaming climate change adaptation into Country Development Plans (CDPs). The project will contribute to the implementation of Kenya's Climate Change Action Plan (CCIA) in line with the National CCIA framework, although the tree cover in rangeland landscapes the project will also contribute towards achieving a tree cover of at least 10% of the land area of Kenya.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 38 OF 76

The project contributes to implementation of Kenya Vision 2030 and the national policy on "Ending Drought Emergencies," which is included in the NAP. This will be accomplished by capitalizing on the national program of devolution, which provides a unique but underutilized opportunity for scaling up climate change interventions that take advantage of increased local autonomy, accountability, legal frameworks, larger budget allocations and enhanced coordination among government agencies. The project will support the implementation of devolution to achieve greater climate change resilience in the ASALs Kenya has a large body of research on the potential benefits of devolution, including improved access to services, better land use planning and strengthening the climate adaptation and rangeland management laws at the county level.

The county legal framework will be supported to specifically empower community land management rights. By linking these rights with the implementation of sustainable land management practices, it is possible to establish a system of legally mandated sub-catchment management plans (SCMPs). It is a priority of the project to support communities to formulate participatory land and water resilience plans to establish local bylaws, and to develop compliance with the bylaws for improved governance.

## E.3 Sustainable Development Potential

### 5.3.1 Environmental, social and economic co-benefits, including gender-sensitive development impact

#### Environmental co-benefits
- **Restoration of rangelands will reduce soil erosion** and enable more sustainable management of rangeland resources, allowing natural and assisted regeneration.
- **Restored rangelands will increase biodiversity**, rehabilitation and rehydration of ecosystem services.
- **Restored rangelands will sequester and store higher levels of carbon in soil and vegetation.**
- **Restored rangelands will provide a source for water recharge to aquifers** and boosting range productivity, biodiversity, and sequestration of atmospheric carbon.

#### Economic co-benefits
- **Ecosystem restoration will mitigate both drought and the effects of drought:**
  - Restored rangeland landscapes will conserve more water, reducing the severity of drought and reducing vulnerability.
  - Restored rangeland landscapes will provide a range of resources that are used to reinforce rangeland resilience in the face of climate change.
- **Increased infiltration in rangeland landscapes will recharge aquifers,** contributing to livestock productivity and health.
- **Restoration of rangelands will increase adaptive capacity at county, landscape and community level through climate-proofing value chains.**
  - Resilient production systems through adoption of climate-adaptive practices and technologies for pastoralists and other land users.
  - The diversification of markets of non-pastoral goods, improving producers' access to markets, and revenues generated.

#### Smallholder farmers
- **Improved market access** will be made at critical times in the year when livestock prices are low. This will allow pastoralists to trade surplus goods on local and regional markets at fair prices.
  - Activities will be targeted to address issues of poor infrastructure, lack of storage facilities, high transaction costs, and the risks of introducing new inputs or products to the market.

#### Smallholder farmers
- **Improved credit access** for smallholder farmers through microfinance services, addressing environment sustainability, increase access to livestock markets, and increase their competitiveness and ability to adapt to droughts and other climate-related shocks.
  - Improved grazing management in the selected landscapes will contribute to increased livestock health, productivity, and survival rates.

#### Smallholder pastoralists
- **Sustainable pastoralism** continued new climate smart agricultural features, featuring water management and reduced tillage, will reduce water demand for irrigation and crop multiplication.

### Social co-benefits
- **Gender benefits include partnerships with the private sector and stimulus programs targeting women,** youth and marginalised groups. The project will also support community-based organizations to ensure that their livelihood participation by these groups will be addressed, including insufficient access to product markets due to high transport costs from remote pastoral communities, and lack of market information.

### E.4 Needs of the Recipient

#### Vulnerability and financing needs of the beneficiary country and population

##### E.4.1 Vulnerability of country and beneficiary groups (Assumption only)

The recipient of the project is the government of Kenya, Kenya has recently become a lower middle income country and is projected to remain so for at least 20 years. Within this domain of countries, the country are projected to be particularly affected by climate change, and will be greatly affected due to a legacy of under investment. Kenya has experienced severe droughts in recent decades, with the most recent being in 1986-1993.

At the local level, beneficiaries of this project are rangeland committees, with a primary focus on pastoralists, and specific attention to the roles, responsibilities, and needs of pastoralist women. Pastoralists are among the most vulnerable groups in Kenya due to their reliance on natural resources for livelihoods. They have limited access to education and literacy services. Kenya's ASALs have fewer social services per capita and less infrastructure. Livestock production is a major source of income, but livestock are under-valued and under-marked in the ASALs due to challenges of market access.

Severe droughts have been recorded in Kenya for 23 of the 51 years from 1960 to 2011. Recurrent and persistent droughts have led to a decline in livestock numbers, with the number of cattle falling by more than half since 1987. The ASALs is coping with unprecedented rainfall, climate variability and changes. These climate-related challenges contribute to food insecurity and water scarcity. This has been combined with weakening of natural resource governance, low investment in basic services, and revenue conflicts.

Food security and nutrition crises resulting from protracted drought underpin pastoralist coping capacities and exacerbate vulnerability. The effects of the drought are most severe in the ASALs where there is a high density of animals in search of water and pastures and heightened incidence of livestock diseases. Drought continues to decrease the carrying capacity of rangelands, with increased numbers of cattle dying from thirst. This has resulted in increased return distances for water trucking, sometimes more than double; increasing waiting time at water sources for cattle; and a decline in milk production. The number of cattle in the ASALs has decreased by 50 percent since 1987 to reach Ksh 50 (80,000) times the normal cost of Ksh 30 (60,000). Traditional mechanisms for managing climate variability and change have been weakened or eroded.

Intensive land uses are expanding and ASALs are becoming convertible to arable land at an increasing rate. Soil carbon stocks in semi-arid environments can decrease by 30 percent in less than five years when native vegetation or pastures

### E.2.4. Financial, economic and social institutional needs

Kenya's economic growth has been robust in recent years but climate change is contributing to an increasing frequency of droughts and floods. The economy grew at a compound annual rate of 5.5% per annum from 2011 to 2016 (compared with 3.8% for the period 2007–2010). Agricultural land has increased by about 4 million hectares since 2000, and agricultural output has grown at an average annual rate of around 5.5% per annum in 2011 to 2016 (compared with 3.8% for the period 2007–2010). Agricultural land has increased by about 4 million hectares since 2000, and agricultural output has grown at an average annual rate of around 5.5% per annum in 2011 to 2016 (compared with 3.8% for the period 2007–2010). Agricultural land has increased by about 4 million hectares since 2000, and agricultural output has grown at an average annual rate of around 5.5% per annum in 2011 to 2016 (compared with 3.8% for the period 2007–2010).

Major contributors to GDP in 2016 were agriculture, forestry and fishing (15.2%), manufacturing (6.9%), real estate (12.0%) and construction (11.4%). The agricultural sector is dominated by smallholder farming (83% of the total), with a total land area of about 40% of Kenya's GDP, but the global share is very small. The bulk of the exports are raw materials and primary products; while imports are high-value capital and finished products, sustaining persistent trade deficits. The agricultural sector has been constrained by low productivity, poor infrastructure, limited access to credit, lack of suitable funding prevents the ASAL counties form addressing urgent needs related to climate change and environmental degradation.

Drought is a recurring problem in Kenya. It is mostly uniplantar and quite opportunistic. Drought has particularly damaging impacts on rain-fed farming; but irrigation farmers can have even more harmful effects than those who rely solely on rainfall. The most widespread non-pastoral livelihood activities are dominated by women. Effective management of rangeland landscapes is constrained by the lack of a supporting institutional environment, including weak land tenure security and poor access to credit. This proposal provides an important opportunity for strengthening inter-sectoral planning. Effective implementation of programs is constrained

Kenya offers a rich climate and drought policy environment upon which to build the new project. The Climate Change Act (2016) provides for the impacts of climate change being observed, and establishes structures to coordinate climate change activities from national to county levels. Kenya has been in the forefront of advocating for policy reforms that will ensure that climate change is mainstreamed into all sectors of development. This includes the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) in 2013. The NCCAP performed an adaptation technical analysis of the sectoral plans and strategies to identify gaps, opportunities and constraints in the implementation of policies and implementation of activities at national and county levels. Some counties are developing or new implementing climate change action plans. These include: Machakos County (2015), Siaya County (2016), Bomet County (2017) and Kitui County Act (2016). This work was spearheaded by the Department for International Development's Strengthening Adaptation Management Authority (NDAMA).

Kenya’s Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC) prioritized mainstreaming climate change adaptation into Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP) 2013-2022. The CCAP was developed in line with the NCCAP and the INDC, which prioritized promoting access to climate finance by county governments and vulnerable communities to implement priority climate change actions. Kenya has also been a key player in the implementation of the Adaptation Action Agenda within the NCCAP Development actions, consistent with the Vision 2030 Medium Term Plans action and low regret options if implemented. These were defined and closed out for short term (1-2 years), medium term (3-5 years) and long term (>6 years).

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 42 OF 76

This project builds on IUCN’s extensive experience in implementing landscape projects and in supporting the process of developing national strategies for sustainable land management. It aims to support the Government of Kenya in strengthening its capacity to manage and restore forest landscapes, including mangroves, while also promoting income from non-timber forest products (NTFPs) and improving livelihoods through improved forest governance.

The project will work with key stakeholders at all levels to develop a national strategy for sustainable land management and forest landscape restoration in Kenya. In 2008, IUCN began partnership with government and NGOs to pilot the development of a National Forest Landscape Restoration Strategy (NFLRS) that would address the challenges facing rangelands and forests landscapes, strengthening value chains at the local level, promoting incomes from NTFPs, biodiversity conservation, enhancing ASAL ecosystems services, and promoting sustainable livelihoods.

IUCN’s experience in climate change adaptation in Kenya has seen IUCN support to the Ministry of Water to adopt sub-catchment planning management for the dry and semi-arid lands; cross-border adaption dialogue between pastoral communities in Kenya and Ethiopia; development of a national strategy on climate change adaptation in ASALs; and Garissa County, developing a resilience framework to support climate change adaptation at M. Elgon region in western Kenya. IUCN has also supported the Ministry of Environment and Forestry to develop a National Forest Landscape Restoration Strategy for the country.

IUCN will work with the Kenya Forest Landscape Hub for Forest Landscape Restoration directed by the Nairobi office, to support Kenya and other nearby countries, organizations, communities and enterprises in experimenting with and adopting landscape approaches.

The Government of Kenya (Govt), through the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation (MoAil):

- The State Department for Livestock (SDL), under the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation, is a line ministry of the Government of Kenya. SDL’s mandate is to provide policy direction on livestock development and sustainable management of rangeland resources through the Regional Pastoral Livelihoods Resilience Project (RPLRP). RPLRP is in its third phase and has been implemented by IUCN since 2013, with support from USAID and other donor members states: Ethiopia; Kenya and Uganda. The objectives of the RPLRP are improved livelihoods for pastoral and agro-pastoral communities through sustainable land management practices that reduce vulnerability to drought crisis or emergency.
- Project’s coordination unit is hosted in the MoAil and the project is implemented in 14 counties including Baringo, Busia, Isiolo, Kalolwai, Laikipia, Laniu, Mandera, Marsabit, Meru, Samburu, Tana River, Wajir, West Pokot, and Turkana.

National Drought Management Authority (NDA):

- NDA is the lead agency in Kenya for droughts affecting household food security and livelihoods of more than ten million people. It provides a platform for long-term planning and action; as well as a mechanism for solid coordination among stakeholders at all levels.
- NDA has been working with IUCN to support sustainable land management in Kenya together with adaptation and resilience building in the Arid and Semi-Arid Lands (ASALs).
- NDA administers the National Drought Contingency Fund in Kenya and disburses Ksh 53 million in seven ASAL counties.

Conservation International (CI):

- CI, through its Conservation Finance Division (CFD), has invested more than US$400 million in over 80 countries to support conservation projects and instruments, helping secure the protection of more than 90 million hectares of high-value habitat. CFD has supported over 2300 partner organizations globally through direct grants and loans totaling $157 million. These investments have leveraged an additional US$460 million in public sector funding and US$800 million in private sector investment.
- CI’s work in Kenya is focused on the development of innovative financing mechanisms to ensure long-term financial sustainability of the conservation projects, including the development of a portfolio of carbon finance projects, biodiversity offsets, ecosystem services, including more than US$300 million of private sector investments.

The project will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder partnership that provides critical start-up and growth-stage enterprises that operate in key conservation areas. Focused on impact for CI’s work up to now is the network of investment partners to secure follow-on financing and define paths to financial sustainability for portfolio of projects.

## E.6. Efficiency and Effectiveness

### E.6.1 Financial soundness of the project/programme

#### E.6.1.1 Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

Private investment is highly constrained in the ASALs of Kenya and the project will establish enabling conditions to attract private sector investments into these areas. The ASALs are a neglected regional market and thus has led to limitations on the potential for returns on commercial investments.

The share of GDP from the ASALs could be high and investment in resilience in this area has the potential to give good returns to Kenya’s economy. The alternative scenario will be continued, and growing, costs to Kenya economy as a result of droughts and other climate shocks. This is because the ASALs are highly vulnerable to droughts and the cost of restoration, with most of the losses related to dies in the ASAL livestock sector.

The potential for upscaling this project is high and the target landscapes were identified based on the potential for upscaling and the ability to attract private investments into these areas. The project will work closely with all ASAL counties and provide a platform upon which similar investments can further advance ecosystem-based adaptation in other ASAL regions. The work is easily scalable because the project will work within established national policies, strategies, and frameworks that are already in place.

The grant from the GCF will help promote transformative change while enhancing private and public investment into the ASALs of Kenya. This will be achieved by addressing the underlying causes of land use or which are of a public good nature, and which therefore cannot be addressed by the private sector. The combination of GCF grant financing and stakeholder involvement has been designed to stimulate systemic change that can be replicated.

The costs of the activities identified are determined in a way to cover the minimum cost possible to achieve project objectives. This is because the project will not only address the immediate needs but also build capacity for future interventions. Thus, the project is intrinsically efficient and cost-effective. Activities to be undertaken by the project specifically include: 1) stakeholder consultations; 2) awareness raising and capacity building; 3) policy development; and 4) creating awareness and capacity building as well as actual implementation of actions on the ground. Thus, they are expected to have positive effects on long-term investments to be made by the stakeholders.

A low cost approach is being adopted in terms of project management, which is a low cost approach with minimal material intervention, and a strong focus on creating a legacy of capacity for communal resource management and adaptive action. This will ensure that the ASALs are resilient and capable of supporting local communities by establishing conditions that enable them to deploy their knowledge and skills more effectively.

### E.6.2 Co-financing, leveraging and mobilized long-term investments (mitigation only)

### E.6.3 Financial viability

Without this grant from GCF, it is unlikely that the ASALs of Kenya will become resilient to climate change in the foreseeable future. Further development, based on weak governance of rangeland landscapes, is likely to see further conversion of land for other uses, leaving pastoral populations increasingly vulnerable to the effects of climate change and drought. This would result in increased food insecurity and migration from these areas.

Calculating economic rates of return is challenging due to the economic uncertainty and increasing regularity of drought events in the ASALs of Kenya (Government of Kenya, 2012).

The main emphasis of this project is to contribute to Kenya's national goal of ending drought emergencies. The project will reduce vulnerability by improving access to water resources and increasing incomes through value chain investments in the agricultural and natural resources sectors.

Economic losses due to climate change in Kenya are currently estimated at 13% - 15% of GDP (Wakida, Downing & Omondi, 2014). The economic costs associated with droughts in Kenya have been estimated as high as US$8.6 million per year during the last decade (Government of Kenya, 2012). In 2011 alone, the country experienced a severe drought at 2.8% per annum with the agricultural sector being among the most affected (Government of Kenya, 2012).

Based on the assumption that the project will reduce vulnerability to climate change and drought as usual scenario (i.e., absence of GCF), the expected economic losses due to drought over the next 15 years in the target ASALs are estimated at US$8.6 million per year.

To assess the future scenarios resulting from the project implementation, the analysis relied on the documented evidence of past projects and the findings of the Kenya Climate Change Vulnerability Assessment (Government of Kenya, 2012). The study also estimated the total costs of droughts experienced in Turkana and Northeast Livelihood zones in the late humanitarian response scenario at US$1 million per year (i.e., US$6.85 million over 15 years). A resilience building intervention is expected to reduce these costs by 90% or US$627,340 per year.

The study also estimated the total costs of droughts experienced in Turkana and Northeast Livelihood zones in the late humanitarian response scenario at US$1 million per year (i.e., US$6.85 million over 15 years). A resilience building intervention is expected to reduce these costs by 90% or US$627,340 per year.

The study thus concluded that interventions that build people's resilience through an increase in household income of US$840 per year will be cost-effective. The analysis also showed that a resilience building intervention that builds right resilience saves money and should be the priority in the ASALs of Kenya (Venton’s 2018 findings can be relied upon to support this conclusion). The study further concluded that the project is likely to be cost-effective under any conservative position and assesses three scenarios to ensure robustness: i.e., realistic, pessimistic and optimistic scenario. The most conservative scenario was adopted as the realistic position of the impact expected from the project – representing a conservative estimate.

### E.6.4 Application of best practice

The project will provide practices across several domains and includes the following:

- **Sub-Council Area Management Plans (SCAMP):** SCAMP are the community-level land use planning process legally mandated by the Water Resources Authority. SCMP for ASALs is the versions developed jointly by WRA and SUCN dryland communities. The plans will be developed with clear climate change adaptation impacts.
- **Community-based range management:** SUCN has pioneered community-based approaches to securing and restoring rangelands in Kenya's ASALs, including in Garissa and Isiolo counties. This approach will be incorporated into the SCAMP process as a means of ensuring that land use plans are consistent with climate change impacts.
- **Drought Early Warning:** The Land Allotments Resource Management Project, implemented by the International Centre for Research in Agroforestry (ICRAF) and funded by the UK Department for International Development, has developed approaches for community-validation of water infrastructure development, ensuring that yields are commensurate with available pasture resources in order to minimize the risk of over grazing, and to minimize conflict over natural resources. This includes small scale irrigation systems, rainwater harvesting, and improved livestock management practices.
- **Drought Early Warning:** The Land Allotments Resource Management Project, implemented by the International Centre for Research in Agroforestry (ICRAF) and funded by the UK Department for International Development, has developed approaches for community-validation of water infrastructure development, ensuring that yields are commensurate with available pasture resources in order to minimize the risk of over grazing, and to minimize conflict over natural resources. This includes small scale irrigation systems, rainwater harvesting, and improved livestock management practices.
- **Value chains:** Scaling up work undertaken by the IRI Feed the Future program. These models include the Kenya Value Chain Finance Centre on strengthening and climate-proofing livelihood value chains; and the MAMASE project, Mini-Market Access for Smallholder Entrepreneurs, which has been in operation since 2013.
- **Community Resilience Facility (CRF):** The CRF that builds on the successful VISA model matches a community's capacity to adapt with its climate change vulnerability. The CRF is currently being piloted by SUCN in Kenya; a Shoria kampilan version has been developed.
- **County Climate Change Action Plans:** These are currently being developed county-wide, with institutions and funding mechanisms. Makurumai allocates 1% of its revenue and Wajir County 2%; other counties are following suit.

### F1. Economic and Financial Analysis

The detailed economic analysis and assumptions are presented in Annex 3.

As noted in section E.6.3 an economic analysis was conducted for the proposed project. Best practices in appraisal for public sector projects have been followed. The Economic Analysis - Annex 3 shows the conclusions of the economic analysis, including the net present value (NPV) and the economic rate of return (ERR).

Economic net present value (ENPV) is calculated using a 12% discount rate. The ENPV at a 12% discount rate at 14 years for the project is USD 11,809,623 and ERR at 14 years is 57%. At an 10% discount rate, the ENPV is USD 14,300,595 and ERR is 61.3%. At a 14.5% discount rate, the ENPV is USD 9,165,194 and ERR is 28%.

The economic net present value (ENPV) calculated using a 12% discount rate for 5 years is USD 3,255,274 and ERR in 20.98%. In this project lives, the project is economically viable. However the project’s viability is even higher under the 14 years life project as it is expected to bring benefits beyond the 14 years of analysis implying that the net benefits are not fully consumed within the period.

Table 6: Summary of economic net present value (ENPV) and economic internal rate of return (ERR) for the project, at 12% discount rate and two sensitivity cases

| Discount rate | ENPV (14 years) | ERR (14 years) | ENPV (5 years) | ERR (5 years) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Discount rate = 12% (base case) | USD 11,809,623 | 57.0% | USD 3,255,274 | 20.98% |
| Discount rate = 10% | USD 14,300,595 | 61.3% | USD 4,307,843 | - |
| Discount rate = 14.5% | USD 9,165,194 | 28.0% | USD 2,176,283 | - |

The sensitivity analysis shows that the economic viability is less sensitive to the discount rates generally accepted for appraising public projects as all the ENPVs remain positive.

The net benefits of the project are considered underestimates; however, for the reasons explained in Annex 3. These include:

1. The period considered for the economic analysis is 14 years. Several of the project interventions are expected to bring benefits beyond the 14 years of the analysis implying that the net benefits are not fully consumed within the period.
2. There are some benefits not captured in the economic analysis, including the economic value of different greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions reductions and sequestration, avoided groundwater recharge, reduced soil degradation and mitigation of floods on low catchments. GHG emission reduction from restoration of pasture or Climate-Smart Agriculture practices also are not included as benefits.

The project cannot be accomplished without the Fund’s support. Project partners are contributing co-finance for the implementation of the proposed project. It is expected that a portion of the resources required for implementation will come from other sources.

The proposal is expected to be financially viable when all activities are implemented together in the project area. Taking these points into account, and noting that Kenya is a low middle income country with limited resources to devote to climate change adaptation, the concessionality that the GCF provides is justified.

# F.2 Technical Evaluation

The approaches outlined in this proposal are recognized by IUCN as "Nature Based Solutions" (NbS): actions to provide multiple benefits that address social and environmental challenges effectively and adaptively, simultaneously providing human well-being and biodiversity benefits.

### NBS approaches are the most appropriate for ASAL countries for four reasons:

- **1. Reach:** The project can include and empower a large number of stakeholders to directly engage with adaptive decision-making processes.
  
- **2. Scalability:** These are the types of interventions that have the least chance of going to scale quickly given the limited resources available in these contexts.

- **3. Cost effectiveness and time responsiveness:** The actions can be deployed immediately and at reasonable cost.

- **4. Complementary:** They are no regret options and can provide a solid foundation for other types of interventions (not provided for in this project) such as livestock insurance, as they become viable options.

Range of NBS that could be considered include: agroforestry, soil conservation, water harvesting, tree planting, regeneration, re- cost approaches that are suited to having an impact on a large scale, as demanded in the ASALs. A key challenge is ensuring that these interventions are not only technically feasible but can also be directly delivered through strengthening of local governance. By empowering local level governance (through by-laws, water resource management structures such as sand dams, shallow wells with rock catchments and spring protection). Potential risks include: lack of technical capacity to implement NBS at scale; lack of political will to support these mechanisms and increasing functionality of restoration-based value chains a virtual feed-back is established. Stronger local governance in turn provides multiple benefits beyond restoration, including improvements in security and livelihoods.

# F.3 Environmental, Social Assessment, Including Gender Considerations

This project has completed the screening procedures of IUCN Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) and has been assessed by an independent third party environmental and social management panel and validated as being compliant with GCF requirements for analyzing low and medium risk projects.

The project aims to improve resilience of communities and ecosystems in Arid and Semi-Arid Lands (ASALs) in Kenya. The project is considered to be a high-risk project, but the ESMS screening process has identified it as an overall highly unlikely. It is considered as highly unlikely that the activities carried out under this project will have any significant adverse environmental or social impacts on the communities and ecosystems involved. There are no (hence no high risk). However, physical interventions planned under components 2 and 3 must be carefully planned and monitored to ensure they do not cause negative impacts.

The project emphasizes low-risk interventions and favors community approaches in order to mitigate the risks associated with these activities. The project is unlikely to have any significant adverse environmental or social impacts on communities involved as it does not involve any social or environmental risks, some which might mild adverse environmental and/or social risks and/or impacts. These risks are considered to be low risk (hence no high risk). However, the risks associated with these activities must be addressed through mitigation measures. Hence the ESMS Screening report classified the project as moderate risk.

### Component 1

- Is not expected to cause negative impacts that are more than minimal as it aims at improving decision making by strengthening diagnosis and integrated landscape planning at the county level; providing climate data, prioritizing areas for action, and developing a landscape plan. The project will also provide technical assistance in the development of the landscape plan.

The main focus of this management is to strengthen community institutions and enabling them to participate effectively in the implementation of the landscape plan. This will be achieved through capacity building and knowledge sharing activities. The project includes resources for training in conflict management and facilitation of conflict resolution in landscape planning.

### Component 2

- Aims to provide ecosystem-based adaptation and includes concrete measures for rehabilitation and restoration, such as tree planting, soil conservation, water harvesting, and the establishment of improved water management structure such as sand dams, shallow wells with rock catchments and spring protection. Potential risks include: lack of technical capacity to implement NBS at scale; lack of political will to support these mechanisms and increasing functionality of restoration-based value chains a virtual feed-back is established. Stronger local governance in turn provides multiple benefits beyond restoration, including improvements in security and livelihoods.

The project includes resources for training in conflict management and facilitation of conflict resolution in landscape planning.

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 49 OF 76

In actions in order to mitigate localized disputes, risks are overall not expected to be significant. In line with the participatory character of the project specific on the ground interventions and sites for the individual interventions will be determined by the communities themselves.

Component 3 involves the development of sustainable value chains (e.g., livestock-based, gums, resin and other production systems) that will be supported through a combination of two different types of interventions: 1) grants to communities in the 2 landscapes and 2) grants to cooperative and community-based enterprises actors to unlock barriers to market access. The project will support the establishment of a revolving fund in each landscape, an autonomously managed revolving fund which provides an incentive to implement the restoration land use plans that each community has developed. This revolving fund is established by the communities themselves with the support of each community to establish this revolving fund. That includes the training and set up of the fund and the small-scale investments that will be made in order to generate income for the fund. The funds generated from these small-scale investments are used to make further investments, in activities that support restoration. The loan sizes are small (typically $10-30) and households can use them in a variety of ways: to purchase livestock feed, to buy seeds or other inputs needed for their agricultural activities engaged in. Being a revolving fund, all members of the community will benefit and this community owned asset will grow and provide income to each member of the community over time.

The grants to value chain actors based on the C model will be much larger grants in the range of $10,000-250,000. These grants are intended to help remove investment barriers that are currently impeding the operation of the value chain and larger and more consistent investment.

### ESMF Gender Analysis

The implementation of the provisions of EUKN’s ESMF Grievance Mechanism will provide an additional safety net for ensuring that people will not suffer from any unforeseen negative social or environmental impacts. 

#### Gender Analysis

The Gender Analysis undertaken for this proposal noted existing gender gaps, inequalities and challenges, and proposed measures to address them. The analysis was based on two tools: the Gender Analysis Tool (GAT) and an understanding that women and youth are affected by climate change and development activities differently, the gender gap in access to resources and services is a major barrier to their participation in the landscape matrix. Through these two tools, the typical daily activities of women, men, girls and boys were investigated, as well as the barriers they face when accessing land, water and other productive assets. The results from this gender analysis will be further complemented through the vulnerability assessments carried out on community level.

Herd management comprises up to 70% of work time for women while women have simultaneous and competing demands in terms of household responsibilities and access to resources. Women are also responsible for most productive assets in the project area, where women use or maintain the same assets; hence the need to include both men and women in decision-making processes is critical.

When designing the actual ecosystem restoration measures together with the communities, the project will ensure appropriate participation of women and youth by ensuring that their voices are heard. This will be achieved through decision-making not being dominated by specific gender groups. In designing the restoration measures the project will be sensitive to the needs of women and youth in terms of access to resources and services. The project will not unilaterally or increase the time burden of women, men and youth; it unintentionally exacerbate other inequalities.

Given that women are responsible for most productive assets in the project area, they should be included as targeted by project benefits (e.g., through training), in decisions about water infrastructure, through incentives related to livelihoods, etc.

To ensure that all expenditures are made in accordance with the approved budget, monthly review of project expenditures will be conducted. The project will apply IUCN’s Financial Rules and Regulations for Grant Recipients. At the end of each reporting period or at any other desired period of reporting, the report can also group and present expenses/transactions pertaining to the respective reporting period in a separate table.

2. Goods and Services (http://www.iucn.org/procedures), that is a standard professional procurement policy and essential for ensuring that all procurements are conducted in an efficient and cost-effective manner while respecting sustainability, the environment and social responsibility. The IUCN’s “Financial Rules and Procedure for Grant Recipients” will be applied, which is a specific set of procedures for grant recipients to implement grant projects, and at the same time it will act as a capacity building measure for grantees.

3. There will also be an annual statutory audit conducted by Waterhouse Coopers, which will form part of the annual statutory audit undertaken by IUCN’s corporate auditor. Price Waterhouse Coopers, in terms of their professional judgment and experience with the sector, will be invited around the end of the project and conducted in line with ISA. To supplement these audits, periodic financial reviews will be conducted as part of regular monitoring.

# G1 Risk Assessment Summary

The project was designed considering the potential social and environmental risks as well as financial and operational risks.

## Social Risks

### Lack of access to alternative grazing resources for communities may arise.
- This might be a result of:
    - The implementation of community restoration plans, land-use plans, and grazing management guidelines that may involve restrictions on livestock grazing in certain areas or seasons.
    - Increased competition over natural resource use, especially water and pasture. Resource competition is an issue when the project area has limited resources to meet the needs of all users (e.g., pastoralists, farmers). This can be mitigated through:
        - The promotion of value chains as communities take steps to strengthen their rights and claims on specific resources.
    - Incompatibility with cultural practices of the local communities in the project sites when implementing restoration activities. This could lead to conflicts over land use or resource access if not managed properly, especially for ecosystem restoration measures and water management infrastructure to ensure compatibility with cultural and traditional uses.

### Potential for operational health and safety considerations due to the location of the project.
- The project area is located in a region where there are no formal health services. This could lead to:
    - Inadequate access to healthcare facilities, which may result in higher mortality rates among workers.

## Environmental Risks

### Development of new water points or harvesting structures in drylands. If not well planned and implemented, can lead to localized impacts on biodiversity during construction phase or by disturbances caused by the project activities.
- This could also lead to:
    - The potential for over-exploitation of water resources from constructed boreholes and wells.

### Measures for controlling invasive species as part of ecosystem restoration may restore the targeted application of herbicides, which can be harmful to human health. In addition, harvesting of natural products harvested from natural habitats could lead to over-harvesting.
- This is particularly relevant for the harvest of gums and resins which frequently causes over-harvesting in some areas.

### Providing access to new water points or harvesting structures may benefit communities where the project does not influence harvest rates but who still benefit from enhanced market access.

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

### Grazing Management Guidelines that May Lead to Seasonal Restricted Access to Range and Pastures

| Mitigation Measures |
| --- |

The project's approach to ensure active participation of the communities in the development of the land-use plans and grazing guidelines will be vital to prevent social impacts. The vulnerability assessments undertaken in each village/commune will identify the most vulnerable groups, including women and youth, who are often excluded from decision-making processes. These assessments will also provide information on the existing access rights and tenure arrangements for pastoral lands. The project will include provisions ensuring that any land-use decisions and all particular decisions about potential use restrictions of certain areas will be made in a participatory manner with full involvement of affected communities and other stakeholders. The participatory approach to be used in community planning brings together all the relevant stakeholders from the local communities, including women and the youth in the planning and decision making process.

However, residual risks relate to the uncertainty whether rehabilitation or restoration of ecosystems will provide sufficient benefits for pastoralists and other users of natural resources. This risk is particularly high where areas are strategically chosen (e.g., the most degraded and least productive areas) to ensure maximum benefits within the project time cycle (restoration of grasslands brings very quick benefits). Second, there is a low risk that there might be vulnerable groups who will not benefit from the project because they do not have access to resources or infrastructure. If this risk is verified in certain community, measures will be identified together with the affected groups to mitigate social and environmental risks.

### Selected Risk Factor 2

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Increased competition over resource use especially water and pasture. Resource competition is an existing issue in the project area, which will be exacerbated as economic activities increase (e.g., through the promotion of livestock production). The lack of water infrastructure and the provision of other project benefits may also act as starting points for conflicts. | Social and environmental | Low (<5% of project value) | Medium |

### Mitigation Measures

The project has been and will continue to be designed as participatory community consultations, and implemented largely by community organizations and pastoralist user groups. These groups will identify which water retention or management structures are needed in their areas for the sustainable use of resources. The design of infrastructure development and other benefits provided by the project will be defined with involvement of relevant groups applying transparent processes. In addition, the project will ensure that SUCI's experience in managing negotiated and mutually acceptable outcomes is vital to mitigate this risk. These mitigation measures are expected to reduce the probability of the risk occurring to 'low'.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

This risk will be mitigated through the use of participatory approaches in planning and implementing activities at the local level. This approach incorporates extensive and inclusive consultation of different stakeholders including indigenous peoples, women, youth, and other vulnerable groups to ensure that their voices are heard on issues relevant to their livelihoods as well as through actions that strengthen their participation and rights in natural resource management and decision-making processes. The measures will be designed with the guidance of the UNDRIP and how provisions from the Indigenous Peoples Standard are addressed. As such it provides a detailed account of the participatory approaches used, including the types of analysis on potential social, cultural or economic impacts (incorporated in the vulnerability assessments) and when FPC from respective groups will need to be obtained. These measures are expected to further reduce the probability of the risk.

## Selected Risk Factor 4

### Description
Potential for operational health and safety risks due to the location of the project.

### Risk category
Social and environmental

### Level of impact
Medium (5-12% of project value)

### Probability of risk occurring
Low

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The risk is managed by IUCN security protocols and monitoring of government and NGO intelligence. An operational health and safety handbook will provide preventive and protective measures and emergency prevention and preparedness plans for the project staff, contractors, local communities, contracted workers and project staff. These measures are expected to further reduce the low probability of the risk.

## Selected Risk Factor 5

### Description
Dominance by men in community dialogue and decision-making. Decision-making in cultural institutions is traditionally dominated by older males, with women and youth partially excluded from the process.

### Risk category
Social and environmental

### Level of impact
Low (20% of project value)

### Probability of risk occurring
Medium

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Kenyan institutions are now aligning to the new constitutional mandate of gender equity. The project includes intensive sensitization towards the need for women's participation in land-use planning and practice gender equity in decision-making processes influenced by the project. These measures are expected to lower the probability of risk occurring to "low".

## Selected Risk Factor 6

### Description
Increased degradation as a result of developing and rehabilitating water harvesting structures and water points. The project includes rehabilitation of water infrastructure and influx of communities and livestock to newly established water points.

### Risk category
Social and environmental

### Level of impact
Low (<5% of project value)

### Probability of risk occurring
Low

### Installation of boreholes and wells could lead to over-abstraction and a lowering of the water table

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The risk during construction phase will be addressed through careful planning of infrastructure works, incorporating strong technical guidance, appropriate planning of infrastructure within wider landscape management plans, and ensuring that all boreholes are installed in accordance with the ESMF. Detailed guidance is provided in the ESMF. These measures are expected to further reduce the low probability of risk occurring.

## Selected Risk Factor 1

### Description
Increase in the spread of invasive species when implementing ecosystem restoration measures.
#### Risk category: Social and environmental
#### Level of impact: Low (<5% of project value)
#### Probability of risk occurring: Low

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
This risk is managed by the use of a protocol with clear guidelines regarding the selection of tree species included in the ESMF. For the grass establishment, the project will conduct a risk assessment following IUCN guidelines for reproduction and translocation of species. These mitigation measures are expected to further reduce the low probability of risk occurring.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

### Description
Providing value chain support and developing markets for products harvested from natural habitats could lead to over-harvesting of plants, leading to a decline in the harvest of gums and resins which frequently causes human-wildlife conflict. This risk might be significant in particular for communities when they are dependent on natural resources but who still benefit from enhanced market access.
#### Risk category: Social and environmental
#### Level of impact: Low (<5% of project value)
#### Probability of risk occurring: Medium

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The project will support communities in determining thresholds for harvest rates for natural resources extracted from natural habitats – specified in the community management plan. The project will also include requirements for monitoring actual extractions. The project will further work with authorities and relevant stakeholders outside the project site to ensure that harvesting rates are monitored and monitor harvesting rates. These measures are expected to lower the probability of the risk occurring to 'low'.

## Selected Risk Factor 9

### Description
Natural disaster events such as prolonged droughts or higher frequency of intense rainfall, potentially causing delays or stoppage in project interventions.
#### Risk category: Technical and operational
#### Level of impact: Low (<5% of project value)
#### Probability of risk occurring: High

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 55 OF 76

### Mitigation Measures (G)

The project is designed to operate within the context of recurrent drought and it is likely that drought will occur during the project period. The occurrence of drought will inevitably have consequences for project activities, but will also strengthen the resilience of the landscapes in which they are implemented. Enhanced financial support and early warning systems will strengthen drought management while restoration of natural resources in drought reserve will reinforce adaptive capacity and resilience.

The main risk is that some investments by relief agencies implementing actions that undermine adaptive capacity and degrade resources, for example through inappropriate siting of water points. The project will develop planning tools that are explicitly designed to mitigate this recurrent risk.


## Selected Risk Factor 10

### Description
Government/donor/private sector invest in irrigation schemes which are technically not appropriate for dryland environments, reducing reliability and effectiveness of project interventions.

### Mitigation Measures (G)

There is a medium probability that some investments in irrigation will be made in the target landscapes, although the overall scale of such investments is likely to be low. The project does not stand in complete opposition to irrigation, but it provides an opportunity for dialogue with relevant authorities at national and county level about managing the negative externalities associated with irrigation. The project will develop a set of escalation mechanisms for improved planning of natural resources to offset the risk of negative externalities.


## Selected Risk Factor 11

### Description
Infrastructure development or economic activities such as mining activities, concessions, and claims may affect the effectiveness of the restoration measures.

### Mitigation Measures (G)

Although no major mining or infrastructure investments are foreseen in the target landscapes, they cannot be ruled out in the long term. The project will establish landscape management mechanisms which are designed to manage competing land uses and ensure that any future development is planned for and coordinated with respect to seasonal grazing access. Those mechanisms deliberately operate at a scale larger than individual farms or ranches, so as to provide cover over land reallocations. These measures are expected to lower the probability of the risk occurring to 'low'.

### H.2 Arrangements for Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

The activities on monitoring, outcome learning, evaluation and reporting are part of Component 1 (Activity 1.4). IUCN will be responsible for the design and implementation of a results-based management system.

## Project Intervention Logic

The logic framework shows the main project activities and the expected outputs, outcomes and impacts. Activities will contribute to three outputs: (i) communities and county governments develop rangeland landscape management plans; (ii) communities and county governments restore degraded rangeland resources, including water resources, are restored, safeguarded and sustainably managed; and (iii) public awareness is raised on the importance of sustainable land use practices, climate change adaptation, ecosystem services. The outputs lead to three intermediate outcomes namely strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for landscape management and restoration; improved capacity of communities and county governments in climate risks; and strengthened awareness of climate threats and risk reduction processes. The outcomes are expected to lead to two long-term impacts: (i) enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions. Ultimately, the project will contribute to reducing land degradation and enhancing ecosystem services, which will help communities and counties to restore rangeland ecosystems and increase water security and land productivity, in order to adapt better to climate change.

The project logical framework outlines the expected results, indicators, means of verification, baseline values and target values at the end of the project period. The following are the key elements:

- There is a high level of recognition of the main, women and vulnerable populations to the content and timeliness of Early Warning Systems.
- Livelihoods, land management and climate change adaptation is adopted as a high-level agenda by the county governments and this is reflected in county budgets.

### (a) Project Intervention Logic

Baseline information for the indicators was identified during project preparation and provided in the logical framework. Baseline data gaps will be addressed through a comprehensive programmatic baseline during the inception phase. The following are the key elements:

- Outputs: A simple progress ranking tool will be used to assess the delivery of project outputs. This ranking tool will be part of the project results dashboard. A description of the progress ranking tool is provided below.

| Ranking | Description |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Activities and results exceed workplan targets |
| 2 | Activities are on track, but results may not meet workplan efficiencies |
| 3 | Results are being achieved significantly faster than expected |
| 4 | Activities are progressing well, but results are falling short of expectations |
| 5 | Activities are proceeding slowly or have been abandoned |
| 6 | Activities are proceeding slowly and results are falling short of expectations (e.g., among non-target beneficiaries, or outside target areas) |

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 67 OF 76

### On target
- Activities and results align with workplan
- Activities and results fall below watershed targets
- There are significant delays in achieving results
- Results have been achieved but not reported
- Activities and results have been completed
- Activities have been cancelled (a justification should be provided)

## Behavioural Outcomes:
It is anticipated that target beneficiaries' knowledge, attitudes, and ultimately behaviour will change as a result of the project. The following indicators will track watershed protection practices being adopted and shared: Change in knowledge, attitude and skills will be tracked with:
- understanding change as it emerges and in making real-time adjustments (quick feedback loop)
- rapid assessment of changes in knowledge, attitudes and skills
- insights into different perspectives, attitudes and values associated with a set of pre-defined domains of interest
- awareness of the project's outputs and outcomes and opportunities and threats
- generating evidence-based 'hard' and 'soft' data

## Policy Influence:
The project will contribute to the development or review of County-level policies and laws that will support integration of drought risk, natural resource management and climate adaptation. A Planning and Policy Analysis (PAPA) will be conducted.

## Impact:
The project will quantify the realization of projected benefits of sustainable forest management and watershed protection practices being adopted and shared: Improved livelihoods and contributions to climate change mitigation.

## Tools to be used include:
- Participatory Assessment of Land Degradation and Sustainable Land Management in Eastern Africa (PALSMA)
- The Shared Action Research for Ecosystem Health (SAREH) tool
- The Systematic Analysis of Resource Use Efficiency (SHAREd) tool
- Saku² and iWSET. These tools are user-friendly and customizable for specific data collection and analysis.

## iWSET:
iWSET is an ecosystem services analysis and mapping tool that is effective for balancing competing environmental and economic goals.

## Learning:
This will be guided by a learning strategy which will include learning thematic modules and questions.
- Two aspects: capturing unexpected results as they arise, and to make use of evidence from the MERL system to inform decision-making on adaptive management, and inform future project design.
- Lessons learned will be integrated into project planning and implementation at every stage in the frame around project meetings, case studies and mid-term or other evaluations.

## Case Studies:
Case studies will be developed to package evidence of change resulting from the project's interventions. They will present a sample of success and failure cases.

**(h) Data collection, management and reporting system:** A IS for purpose data collection, management and reporting system will be deployed. Mobile-data collection apps will be integrated in our course based on needs. Progress reporting will be done to send reports to the GCF. The reports will include information about the project activities and how they were addressed. They will also include a compilation of lessons learned and financial expenditure details.

(i) Methodology for monitoring and reporting of key outcomes

In addition to the above mentioned data collection and management tools, various approaches will be used to monitor the project performance and report on its progress and results:

- **Participatory landscape assessments:** combined with remote sensing to specifically monitor area covered by rehabilitated/protected ecosystems.
- **Rehabilitation indicators:** will be used to document and validate the number and coverage of ecosystem-based adaptation systems that have been established/enhanced through programme activities;
- **Knowledge management tools:** will be used to document lessons learned from project implementation
- **Scorecards:** to assess degree of awareness and integration of climate change to national sector plans, and vulnerability assessments to monitor adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks.

**(j) Monitoring and Evaluation Plan**

ICCN is overall responsible for the monitoring and evaluation of the project and these responsibilities will be detailed in the M&E Plan. ICCN will report to the GCF as follows:

- **Annual Management Reports:** ICCN will submit annual financial management reports, which will include dates and amounts disbursed for each funded activity and compliance with financial covenants;
- **An interim evaluation report and a final evaluation report for the project:** The evaluations will assess the performance of the project against its objectives and expected results. They will also document lessons learned from the project including financial/economic performance as part of the project efficiency and effectiveness criterion.

The M&E Plan will detail the tools mentioned above, including the progress ranking tool, the KAP survey, the policy influence scorecard, PRAIGA, SHARAD, Sauri, Invist, DevResilience and will have set targets for specific deliverables and indicators. ### (a) Interim Evaluation and Final Evaluation:

#### Interim Evaluation (IE): An independent Interim Evaluation will be carried out no later than Year-3 of programme implementation. The interim evaluation will be an important milestone of the project and will be guided by the DAC/GECD criteria for evaluating development effectiveness, including relevance, adequacy, efficiency, sustainability and impact orientation. The evaluations also pin-point issues requiring decisions and actions, restrictions on resources, and other constraints that may affect the achievement of the overall project objective. The evaluation report will include a summary of findings, conclusions and recommendations. The evaluation report and recommendation will be disseminated to stakeholders. Furthermore, a management response to the interim evaluation review will be prepared by the implementing agency in consultation with the Project Management Unit (PMU) in accordance with GCF procurement policies/regulations.

#### Final Evaluation: An independent end-of-project evaluation will take place three (3) months prior to project close-out date and will be conducted according to DAC/GECD criteria, as the Main Focus. However, it will also explore indications of impact and sustainability of results, including the contribution to achievement of the overall project objective in a broader context. The final evaluation report will include a summary of findings, conclusions and recommendations on relevant management, strategic, and/or policy actions. Management response to issues raised in the end-of-project evaluation review will be prepared by the implementing agency in consultation with the Project Management Unit (PMU) in consultation with implementing and executing agencies, after which the report will be submitted to the Steering Committee for further review and comments. The final report will eventually be submitted to GCF.

### DAC - Development Assistance Committee
### GECD - Economic Cooperation and Development
