# Secretariat’s assessment of FP113

## Proposal name: TWENDE - Towards Ending Drought Emergencies: Ecosystem Based Adaptation in Kenya's Arid and Semi-Arid Rangelands

Accredited entity: International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN)

Country/ies): Kenya

Project/programme size: Small

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project strengthens the capacity of county governments established in 2010, to address the threats of climate change. | The project operates at a low level, working directly with the communities, which is considered critical for long-term sustainability. |
| The project aims to increase the value to the local community enterprises and microenterprises through the development of value chains for livestock industries and support to micro-enterprises. | The project operates in 11 counties over two “landscapes,” which may lead to complicated arrangements for implementation. |
| The administrative and financial structure of the on-granting and loan award will need to be well coordinated. |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions outlined below, as a stand-alone and additional AID, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations.”

### II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

#### 2.1 Project background

The "TWENDE - Towards Ending Drought Emergencies: Ecosystem Based Adaptation in Kenya's Arid and Semi-Arid Rangelands" project aims to increase the resilience of livestock sector enterprises, particularly smallholder farmers, pastoralists, and other rural communities in Kenya’s arid and semi-arid lands (ASALs). The project targets eleven counties in the ASALs. The intervention will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach involving government institutions to develop evidence-based landscape planning by increasing accessibility to climate data and information, strengthening local governance, and promoting community-based land management practices to access markets and financial services by developing value chains.


---
**Page 87**
---

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Climate change adopted-planning for drought resilience (Total cost: USD 7.91 million; GCF cost: USD 5.23 million)

- Under this component, the project will strengthen the management capacity of government institutions to make evidence-based decisions when developing climate sensitive landscape and management plans. Interventions will build on established information systems for drought risk assessment, vulnerability analysis and downscale climate data, thereby increasing coordination of cross-country landscape planning.

- The proposed interventions strengthen the local county administrations, which were established after the adoption of the new constitution in 2010 and which tend to lack adequate knowledge of climate change issues. This component is considered critical for the sustainability of the project’s interventions over time.

#### Component 2: Restoration of rangeland landscapes for ecosystem-based adaptation (Total cost: USD 14.86 million; GCF cost: USD 9.40 million)

- This component focuses on strengthening the implementation of climate change sensitive landscape management plans through evidence-based planning. Interventions under this component include supporting communities to implement drought management activities, supporting communities to develop bylaws and incorporate county laws, and increasing community engagement.

- The proposed interventions build on proven approaches and technologies for ecosystem-based adaptation in dry areas, and the interventions are very likely to be effective in reducing vulnerability of pastoralists. Increased knowledge and capacity as well as increased community engagement strengthens the sustainability of the interventions as well as the cost-effectiveness.

#### Component 3: Investments and incentives for climate change resilient ecosystem management (Total cost: USD 9.57 million; GCF cost: USD 7.01 million)


---
**Page 88**
---

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High
The proposed project has adaptation benefits for local communities and institutions through improved livelihoods planning and implementation, enabling evidence-based decision-making and creating incentives for community-led solutions.

The grants scheme provides incentives to create sustainable value chains by improving the entrepreneurship of local private sector enterprises.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High
The project demonstrates opportunities for scaling up and knowledge-sharing beyond the project timeline and area, to cover a larger extent of landscape through adaptive actions aiming to enhance resilience in the region.

The emphasis on creating more value in the local communities through the development of cottage industries and support to micro and small enterprises is considered highly effective in breaking the dependency on natural resources and thus for holding risks against crop failures.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High


---
**Page 89**
---

29. The project will increase environmental resilience through restoration activities and long-term adaptive capacity-building at county and community level from planning through to implementation of sustainable rangeland management and practices.

30. The development of cottage industries and support to micro enterprises delivers a long-term benefit that will persist beyond the lifetime of the project.


### 3.4 Needs of the recipient

21. Reduced recovery periods between droughts owing to an increase in their frequency and severity exacerbates the deterioration of vegetation condition, leading to further environmental degradation and direct benefits on food security and income for poorer households in Kenya.

22. The administrative verticality of governance after the adoption of the constitution in 2010 means that county governments have little knowledge and operational skills in dealing with climate change issues. This project addresses that gap through institutional capacity-building.


### 3.5 Country ownership

23. The proposal aligns well with national policies and strategies on climate change, such as the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP), Kenya’s Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) to the Paris Agreement, Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture Strategy, Kenya Strategic Investment Framework for Sustainable Land Management, the ASAL policy, the livestock policy and others. It also shows strong alignment with the county’s own climate change adaptation plan.


### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

24. The project takes a participatory approach and uses a community-based approach, enabling autonomous country-led solutions. A high level of stakeholder engagement and consultation during the identification of priorities and target areas are well referenced.

25. The project demonstrates financial adequacy and minimum concessionality. The project seeks to address a market failure of collective action related to the management of communal rangelands. It is expected that the revenue generated from the project will be used for non-commercial activities that generate public benefits but cannot generate sufficient revenues to service a loan. Revenue-generating components, such as financial support to private enterprises and community-based organizations, are included in the proposal.

26. An economic analysis of the proposal shows high cost-effectiveness through a base case economic internal rate of return of 31 per cent over 14 years. The key economic benefits are reduced vulnerability to droughts, increased livestock productivity and income generation for both pastoralists and communities through up-scaling nature-based enterprises. However, a sensitivity analysis indicates that the economic internal rate of return is low in a pessimistic scenario where the effect on incomes is 16 per cent lower than expected and reductions in losses from drought are 30 per cent less than expected.


---
**Page 90**
---

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The project aims to reduce the cost of climate change drought on Kenya's national economy by increasing the resilience of the livestock and other land-use sectors in restored and effectively governed rangeland ecosystems. The project will cover over 11 counties in two priority ASALs of Kenya: [1] climate change adapted planning for drought resilience; [2] restoration of rangeland landscapes.

The accredited entity (AE) has classified the project as likely to generate moderate environmental and social risks, equivalent to category B, following its environmental and social management plan (ESMP). The AE anticipates that there will be no significant adverse risks and impacts. Instead, the risks and impacts are expected to be of a low level in terms of severity, largely reversible and readily addressed through generally accepted mitigation measures.

While the landscapes and counties have been selected, the specific interventions and the exact locations have yet to be determined. To address this, the AE developed an environmental and social risk management plan (ESRMP) that identifies the likely environmental and social risks and impacts, measures considered in the ESMP to mitigate these risks and impacts, and the process for implementation and the implementation arrangements. The AE identified seven activities that are expected of the project. The ESMP is consistent with the GCF’s environmental and social standards and applies the Standard on Indigenous Peoples of the AE as well as the GCF’s interim environmental and social safeguard policy. The ESMP also incorporates the requirements and processes for further assurance and management of risks and impacts to indigenous peoples, and for meaningful consultations leading to free, prior and informed consent (FPIC) in accordance with the UN Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples.

The ESMP describes the environmental and social due diligence process that will be undertaken by the AE. The process is consistent with the national requirements under the Environment Management and Coordination Act 1999 and associated regulations, as well as the GCF’s interim environmental and social safeguard policy and interim ESM standards. The process for screening, including screening criteria, is included in the ESMP. The ESMP also provides the list of excluded activities, including high environmental and social risk activities such as large-scale land acquisitions, resettlement, involuntary or economic displacement, restrictions on water use, degradation of critical natural habitats, activities that result in significant adverse impacts to indigenous peoples, and activities with potential impacts on natural resources, among others. The eligibility criteria are also included as part of the screening process.

For moderate risk activities, the due diligence process will involve the conduct of an impact assessment and risk management, based on the identified risks and impacts. In instances where there is a high likelihood that the project may involve land acquisition by indigenous peoples in the project or that may involve land or livelihood destructions, a social impact assessment (SIA) will be undertaken. The environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) and/or an SIA will form part of the ESMP. The ESMP also provides for other measures such as environmental and social management plans or other specific plans, such as inclusion plans for marginalized communities. The impact assessment process will be concluded with the issuance of ESMs


---
**Page 91**
---

clearance follows a review of the adequacy of the ESIA and the ESIA process by the Project Management Unit (PMU).

### Salient environmental and social impacts

The project is expected to generate moderate environmental and social impacts taking into account the small-scale nature of the activities, the limited scale of the interventions, and the existing institutional organization and institutional arrangements. The project is also expected to deliver positive impacts from the restoration activities, land and water conservation and management, and investments in priority areas for biodiversity conservation. The ESIA will include a description of grants for private sector enterprises. As the design and locations of the interventions will be determined through consultations with the parties of the consultation, the specific risks and impacts will be detailed through ESIA.

### Expected environmental impacts

The expected environmental impacts include habitat fragmentation, potential loss of biodiversity, disturbances of natural habitats, soil erosion, and localized impacts from value chain activities. These, however, are readily mitigated through the implementation of mitigation measures as part of the project design and operational processes. On the other hand, the social impacts may include temporary losses of livelihood, resource competition, impacts on marginalized and vulnerable communities, and impacts on cultural heritage.

### Impact on indigenous peoples

An important impact of the project is the impact on the indigenous peoples. The ESF provides a framework for consultations, further assessment and planning for mitigation. Further assessments will be conducted to identify the specific needs of indigenous peoples. Stakeholder identification and analysis will provide an adequate basis for the application of the AE and ESF GES criteria. The process for obtaining FFC in the context of the project will be developed as may be necessary. FFC will be obtained at various levels and will apply to indigenous peoples as well as to a wider group of marginalized peoples. The process for obtaining FFC in the context of the project is currently under development.

### Conflicts around resource use

Conflicts around resource use are also anticipated in the project. The measures identified in the screening process provide an avenue for conflict sensitivity (including participation and consultation) and will be incorporated into the project-level grievance redress.

### Restrictions to access resources

While some of the activities may involve potential temporary restrictions to access to resources, these restrictions will not change land uses or rights to the resource, and such restrictions will be collectively decided by the community as part of their community land-use plans. Where land may be required for small-scale infrastructure development, the project will seek to obtain FFC in a voluntary basis following the provision of documented consent from the landowner.

### Stakeholder engagement

The ESF describes the information disclosure requirements, which follow the principle of the AE on accountability. The disclosure of information on activities, including the likely environmental and social risks and impacts, will involve local disclosure at locations


---
**Page 92**
---

### Gender policy

The proposal contains an extensive and well-structured gender analysis and therefore complies with international guidelines of the GCF Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan.

Kenya presents a favorable enabling environment, giving recognition to women's rights and human rights. The principle of equality and non-discrimination is established as a core value.

Used gender-related information is given for the two landscapes where the project will be operating, and the information clearly illustrates the challenges and opportunities for the project to address and invest on. The gender analysis which was done using gender analysis tools, has highlighted that women in the two landscapes have limited access to land (especially women who are married) and control issues in what are regarded as critical resources such as land (meant for agriculture). Women also face challenges with respect to access to credit facilities since they are associated with it (e.g. animal health, agricultural extension services, markets, market information systems). The analysis also highlights that women have limited decision-making power (time or poverty) and decision-making levels of women compared with those of men in the two landscapes. The assessment highlights that women lack capital and assets that can be put up as collateral for loans and therefore are unable to access credit facilities. This is a challenge since there is a need for the project to address this. The assessment also stated that women and youth face many challenges in accessing markets, especially those who live in remote areas. It emphasizes the importance of addressing these issues by ensuring that gender equality is considered by each project at the activity implementation level. The assessment demonstrates the structural barriers that exist within the communities which are currently preventing women from current positions, which needs to be improved by strongly recognizing that these changes will have to come with the involvement of them. The analysis indicates that literacy levels among women and girls are high and therefore recommends appropriate communication modalities to ensure

### Risks

#### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

- The funding proposal requests a GCF grant of USD 23.15 million, accounting for 67 per cent of the total financing. The total project cost is USD 34.58 million, with co-financing by way of grants from the EEA/Development Fund of Kenya, grant from the AE and subordinated loans from CI and G.
- There are three EFIs for the three outputs; namely, MoEFi for component 1, NDMa for component 2, and CI for component 3. Given the large project areas (11 counties; 500,000 hectares) and the number of stakeholders involved, it is important to ensure efficient coordination among the AE, the EFIs and other relevant stakeholders. A coordination mechanism will be put in place at the national level with a focal point for each EFI. The AE states that NDMa has a coordination mandate and has staff in all counties in project target areas. As a main EFI, MoEFi will also provide a coordination function supported by the coordination hubs that divide the countries into manageable units.


---
**Page 94**
---

### 4.3.2. AE/EE capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

58. IUCN has an extensive track record in implementing climate change adaptation in the region. The three EAs have partnered with government agencies and environmental organizations on ASAL management and forest landscape restoration in Kenya since 2008. The GCF Executive Board approved a grant of USD 13 million to IUCN as the implementing partner, through its Global Nature-based Solutions Group, will be responsible for technical quality and ensuring progress. It is noted that while the experience of the AE working with the Government of Kenya in ASAL management and forest landscape restoration has been positive, it was not sufficient to support the project implementation; the three readiness grants under implementation by IUCN demonstrate a more comprehensive understanding of the context, and subsequently IUCN has shown improvement in implementation of these readiness projects.

59. The three EAs have track records in project implementation. Mwai has experience in managing climate change adaptation projects in Kenya through a consortium financed by external donor funding for 2015-2019. The annual budget in 2017/2018 of NOMA was USD 59 million, and the AE is currently implementing projects under this fund. Mwai has been managing and administering the National Biodiversity Conservation Fund in Kenya. Fatai has been working in Kenya for 20 years and works with local partners in the landscapes. It has invested more than USD 600 million in landscape restoration.

### 4.3.3. Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

52. Grant and loan financing under component 3: this includes (1) technical assistance; (2) on an granting mechanism (small grants to community-based women’s organizations and large grants for the Community Resilience Facilities); and (3) a revolving fund.

The funding proposal states that mostly grants from GCF will be provided in the early stage and loans from CI will be provided later. The AE has requested that the information provided by the AE, there will be opportunities to take a blended finance approach (grant and loan) on certain investments. During the implementation of component 3: the AE is reliant upon the availability of GCF grants and CI loans. If these are not available, this could strengthen the climate resilience of the beneficiaries. The AE is also expected to ensure that the GCF grant and CI loans are used for their intended purpose.

53. Refers from the revolving fund: regarding the Community Resilience Facilities under activity 3.3; the funding proposal states that the grant to communities will be retained as an asset to the community-managed revolving fund, which is a financial mechanism that establishes and capitalizes the solidarity revolving fund. The AE states that this is not commercial microfinance but for no profit taking. In case loans from it are used for scaling up the Community Resilience Facilities, the GCF grant and CI loans are attributed proportionally to the community-managed revolving fund.

54. Selection criteria: the AE states that the grants to communities will be based on the criteria to be developed by communities for the climate-resilient land use/renovation plan. The funding proposal states that the selection process should include a participatory approach and bylaws/laws, which would also include climate change elements. The selection and eligibility criteria in this regard are not yet clear. It is recommended that the AE and ERE ensure that climate change criteria will be detailed in the grant and loan assessment as well as country/national plans;

55. Natural disaster: the AE has identified the risk of natural disasters such as prolonged droughts and torrential rainfall affecting or destroying the project interventions. However, the


---
**Page 95**
---

funding principal states that the project is not expected to insure the project intervention. It is expected that drought will occur during the project period. The AE is requested to explore the possibility of securing insurance coverage for the project to cover any potential damages during the implementation of the project.

### Possible other investment decisions that may affect the sustainability of the project: the AE has identified the risk of other investment decisions regarding irrigation that may be negative externalities and affect the effectiveness of the project. It also identified the risk of economic activities in the area, which could lead to land use conflicts with the project. The proposed measures will support policy dialogues with relevant authorities and will also establish landscape management plans. These measures are designed to manage competing land uses and claims, thus reducing the probability of the risk such interferences.

#### Economic viability: the AE has provided an economic analysis that yielded the economic internal rate of return of 31.35 per cent for the project over a 14-year period as a base case. The analysis also considered the likelihood of droughts over 14 years based on data for the past 20 years, which resulted in an overall positive net present value for the project.

##### Compliance risk (medium risk): no adverse hits have been found in a preliminary sanctions screening for the programme. However, further screening should be done prior to any funding disbursements and as additional portions are identified in the project; paragraph 35 above, recognises the potential for conflicts to arise over resource use. No additional elaboration on the governance structure of the project, such as who will make decisions regarding water allocation, has been provided by the AE or how this information will be made available to stakeholders. Additional information is provided by the AE as to how corruption risks will be managed and prevented. In view of the importance of good governance in capacity-building exercises, it is suggested that training include compliance-related issues, including but not limited to, water allocation, land use conflicts, and other aspects related to the prevention of preventing corruption, fraud, and waste, which could compound the risk of conflict over the access of, allocation of, and use of limited resources.

##### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk): in case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio remains non-material and within the risk appetite at the level of concentration level; results area or single proposal.

#### Recommendation: it is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.

# Fiduciary

## The EEIs for the project are the Government of Kenya (GoK), through the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation (MAI), the National Drought Management Authority (NDMA) and Conservancies Association (CA), which is also an AE of GCF.

### Each of the EEIs has entered into a sub-agreement with IUCN for the AE for the project. As EE, they will enter into a service agreement with service providers and be accountable for the delivery of associated outputs under the three project components. Each of the EEIs will also enter into separate agreements with relevant EEs to provide technical assistance and sub-contracts under the procurement and supervision of relevant EEIs, while all other activities will be delivered by the relevant EEI directly.

### The AE is responsible for ensuring that the grant financing is beneficial to the end beneficiaries.

## Fiduciary

### The project will be implemented in the region operated by the regional office for Eastern and Southern Africa (ESARO) of IUCN, based in Nairobi, Kenya, while the thematic expertise on drylands management and general oversight will be from the Global Finance Unit and the IUCN Global Environment Facility (GEF) Secretariat. The AE will provide overall project coordination and providing strong technical oversight to the project and ensuring that all fiduciary rules, including procurement, are followed.

### The PMU will be established by IUCN in partnership with the MoI and will be responsible for the overall implementation of the ground and achievement of the project’s objectives. The PMU will establish a Project Management Unit (PMU), coordinated and managed by IUCN in partnership and housed by the MoI. The PMU will be responsible for the coordination of all activities funded by the project and undertaken by EEIs. All members of the PMU will be employed on a full-time basis, except for the landscape hub coordinators. During implementation of the project, the PMU will work with three landscape hub coordinators to ensure the smooth running of the project. The PMU will also provide technical support to the landscape hubs and consultants, service providers and other partners. The hubs will be hosted by one of the county governments in each landscape area. The PMU will be responsible for ensuring that all fiduciary rules (e.g., procurement) are followed.

### The PSC will be established to provide strategic-level project guidance, technical and policy advice to the PMU, and to ensure the effective implementation of the project. The PSC will consist of members representing the MoI, IUCN and the PMU for the implementation of the project.

## Results monitoring and reporting

This proposal addresses adaptation on two result areas—most vulnerable people/countries and enhanced ecosystem services—with the expected number of direct and indirect beneficiaries at 155,000 and 620,000, respectively.

In terms of the logical framework, the funding proposal adequately designs the indicators to measure progress towards achieving the project’s outputs and outcome level. For monitoring, reporting, and evaluation arrangements, an M&E plan has been developed to measure and track impact and project indicators, including time of availability, reporting frequency, and data quality. The M&E plan is aligned with the proposed project M&E. The alignment of the M&E plan and relevant sections in the funding proposal has also been addressed.

### Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement (‘AMA’) was signed with the Accredited Entity on 11 October 2017, and became effective on 11 January 2017.

The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project. It is recommended that the GCF Secretariat request the Accredited Entity (AE) to provide a letter of assurance that the AE has obtained all its internal approvals and the Fund has received a certificate or legal opinion from the Accredited Entity in form and substance satisfactory to the Fund confirming that the AE has obtained all necessary internal approvals, including any required by law, and that the entity has the authority and capacity to implement the project. Pursuant to section A of the AMA, the Fund will request the Accredited Entity obtain its internal approvals for the proposed project on 25 May 2018.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Republic of Kenya, a country in which GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation. The GCF Secretariat has confirmed that GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed. The Secretariat sent a draft agreement on privileges and immunities, together with a letter of request for the Fund’s approval, to the Attorney General’s Office. Bilateral exchanges have taken place to respond to comments received from the Attorney-General’s Office and the Kenyan Ministry of Foreign Affairs. In this regard, discussions on the draft agreement on privileges and immunities are ongoing.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress


---
**Page 98**
---

activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant strategies and innovations. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expatriation of the project manager or other key personnel to third-party jurisdictions.

The proposed projects envisage the financing by GCF of the provision of grants and loans under four different activities within the same Project component, for which Conservation International (CI) will act as the executing entity. However, the eligibility criteria for the selection of the implementing partner and the conditions for disbursing funds and the flow of funds are not entirely or clearly described in the funding proposal. Accordingly, for purposes of this project, it is recommended that CI, the GCF, the Secretariat, and defining how the granting and lending mechanisms will be implemented by CI, the Accredited Entity shall agree on a term sheet setting out the conditions for disbursing funds and defining how the granting and lending mechanisms will be implemented by CI, the Accredited Entity prior to first disbursement of funds. It is also recommended that the terms set forth in this term sheet provisions have been agreed with the Accredited Entity in the term sheet to ensure that the above matters are timely addressed and clarified prior to the signing of the funded activity agreement and completed with during the implementation of the Project.

### 4.7 List of proposed conditions (including legal)

1. In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:
   - Delivery by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all its internal approvals within 120 days of the Board approval;
   - Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat, which shall be provided to the Fund upon receipt from CI. The Fund shall confirm to the Accredited Entity has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals, whichever is later; and
   - Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP113

## Proposal name: TWENDE - Towards Ending Drought Emergencies: Ecosystem Based Adaptation in Kenya’s Arid and Semi-Arid Rangelands

### Accredited entity: International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN)

### Project/programme size: Small

## Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: High

1. **Kenya is a country impacted by climate change**. The country is located in Eastern Africa, which receives little rainfall. However, it has arid and semi-arid regions, which are not particularly suitable for agriculture or livestock production. Historically, pastoralism and crop agriculture, these arid and semi-arid lands (ASALs) used to be more suitable for livestock management. The ASALs were traditionally managed by communities who had owned them. However, with increasing population, many people have started to take advantage of such mini commons and the traditional grazing systems have been eroded or abandoned in some areas. This resulted in competition over resources, gradually destroyed community-managed governance norms and practices, subsequently led to much reduced availability of water in such reservoirs for the livestock.

2. **ASALs occupy 70% per cent of Kenya, and such lands are home to 36 per cent of the population**. Pastoralism has been the dominant livelihood in ASALs, providing for 70 per cent of the national livestock production. Livestock production contributes to 25 per cent of total GDP, while production contributes to 80 per cent of household income in arid and 65 per cent of income in semi-arid areas. However, the region has experienced frequent droughts and increased occurrences of droughts, which has affected livestock rearing. It is estimated that drought has been responsible for decelerating Kenya’s economic growth by 2.8 per cent points.

3. **To make things worse**, climate variability and change placed further stress on water resources in the ASALs. The region has experienced a decline in rainfall, leading to reduced water reservoirs for both pastures and crop agriculture. As a consequence, both sources of livelihood have been eroded. This has led to competition between pastoralists and farmers over land use between two severer drought episodes. As a result, the economic conditions of rangeland pastoralism have become more precarious in recent years. The communities are now forced to consider distress coping modalities. As a result the affected rangeland population has become further marginalized.

4. **The funding proposal presents evidence through the trend analysis of meteorological data for the period 1960 to 2009, which informs us that (a) there has been a warming trend and (b) total rainfall has been declining with intensification of rainfall episodes during the wet periods**. The maps generated by the geographical information system clearly identify the areas where droughts are more likely to occur in future. This is based on the analysis of meteorological data that support the scientifically represented results on the changing climate system.


---
**Page 100**
---

5. The projected climate change is most likely to increase drought-related problems in Kenya. While water availability is likely to decline further due to dwindling rainfall, the projected data suggest that drought intensity will increase with increasing evapo-transpiration rates and reduced soil moisture content. For example, a 20-year period of 40 per cent loss of water availability in community-managed ponds in the rangelands may be attributed to climate change. This will have severe impacts on livestock production, vegetation and grazing grounds, which will be significantly worse under climate change. Therefore, the productivity from livestock management and crop agriculture will decline further with increasing drought intensity and reduced water availability in the pastoral and agro-pastoral regions. This warrants immediate actions in the ASAL regions.

6. The objective of the project is to reduce the cost of climate change-based drought on Kenya’s rangelands by restoring degraded land, improving livestock and other land-use sectors in restored and effectively governed rangeland ecosystems. The project is expected to strengthen community resilience against climate change impacts through a multi-pronged approach at community and institutional levels; the project aims at strengthening institutional capacities to support poor communities by providing them with early information on climate change, and interventions that will help them adapt to it. This includes the construction of small water reservoirs to facilitate both pastures and crop agriculture. Moreover, the project aims at developing a market-based approach for livestock products through micro-enterprises that are run and managed by the affected communities in target ASAL.

7. The project comprises the following three components:

(a) Component 1: Climate change adapted planning for drought resilience;

(b) Component 2: Restoration of rangeland landscape for ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA);

(c) Component 3: Investment and incentives for climate change resilient ecosystem management.

8. Under component 1, the project will strengthen management capacity of governance and local institutions so that informed decisions are made to develop climate-change-resilient landscapes. This will be achieved through a participatory approach in the development of an information system on use of downscaled climate data in improved analyses regarding climate change sensitivity and vulnerability, with the latter being integrated in cross-county landscape planning processes. The project will also support the development of county-level sustainability of county-level processes related to landscape planning and implementation.

9. Component 2 focuses on implementation of landscape plans that are centred around EBA by identifying key ecosystem services and their associated land uses. The focus of this component are: (a) develop operational hydrams; (b) incorporate such hydrams into county laws and policies, and (c) implement the hydrams in a participatory manner. This will be based on considering proven approaches and technologies for EBA in dry areas. Interventions which are generally proven in ASAL conditions as effective towards reducing vulnerabilities in a local context will be prioritized. The project will use a participatory, consultative and transparency process will be the key delivery modality.

10. Component 3 will focus on removal of investment barriers and creating enabling markets for livestock products that is envisaged that; investment barriers are removed and access to markets and financial services are ensured simultaneously, the local producers will participate in the development of market-based approaches and receive present and future gains in terms of incremental income. To this end, the project will encourage the development of

18. An associated theme, component 2 is designed to create an enabling environment along with contributing to the reduction of community vulnerability to drought. There are clear indications that some of the institutional mechanisms, including the development and effective application of land use plans, have been undermined by the lack of a coherent approach from diminishing water pockets in the rangelands in ASALs. Such interventions along with sustainable livelihoods and income generation activities will be critical for communities which are critical for livestock, forestry, and agricultural production in these areas. If production cannot be streamlined to contrast the impacts of climate-induced droughts, it will not be possible to benefit from the project.

17. Since the forest regeneration, restoration and rehabilitation processes are planned as incentive-based community endeavours, subject to available modern resources in the future, their replication is likely to be limited by the lack of a coherent approach from diminishing water pockets in the rangelands in ASALs. Such interventions along with sustainable livelihoods and income generation activities will be critical for communities which are critical for livestock, forestry, and agricultural production in these areas.

18. The project does not directly create new markets for goods and services generated from the restoration of rangelands. However, the incentives designed for micro-enterprises, facilitating access to credit and other financial services, as well as providing technical support, will immensely help them to make financial profits from ecosystem-based products. Under the current scenario, local people are unlikely to be able to generate sufficient income from these activities without market-related support; local micro-scale businesses will flourish and are more likely to penetrate the value chain by participating in it while acting as stakeholders at the local level. This also offers significant learning opportunities.

20. Project component 3 addresses removal of barriers to access financial support so that local people can establish and run micro-scale enterprises. In addition, component 1 addresses removal of barriers to access land for grazing livestock and other activities. These are two key practices which are likely to help in overcoming systemic barriers that currently hinder progress for local people in the rangelands in ASALs.

20. As indicated earlier, the regulatory barriers and diminishing community governance practices will be addressed under component 2. As suggested in the funding proposal, if the local level is not able to address these issues, then it may be necessary to involve higher levels. They could represent significant dividends in re-establishment of fair and just community-wide practices towards managing scarce resources that are critical for all the competing users. This can contribute significantly to the sustainability of the project.

21. EBA has not been given due importance in the formulation of policies and strategies towards managing natural resources in degraded ASALs in Kenya. This project provides an opportunity to address this issue by involving local communities, including pastoralists, their land managers, and their direct users. If the project establishes norms and practices for the sustainable use and management of natural resources, it will have a significant impact on the ecological potential within and beyond Kenya as similar degraded ecosystems are abundant throughout Africa.

22. In view of the above discussion, the ITAP understands that the paradigm shift potential of the project is 'high'.

25. The project is expected to result in some environmental co-benefits: if improved government policies are put into place, this project could contribute to more sustainable management of rangeland resources. Restoration of degraded rangelands is expected to increase soil moisture content and improve water availability, leading to ecosystem services and an increase in biodiversity. Although the focus of the project is to achieve adaptation, there will be some sequestration of carbon in restored forests and rangelands. The improved land management practices under this project’s conditions will be conducive to produce higher levels of moisture storage and infiltration, which in turn is expected to improve soil quality and increase water availability. Consequently, the changed conditions will boost productivity of the rangelands and its biodiversity.

26. Although the potential rehabilitation of the water cycle is expected only in the medium to longer term, this project has a number of co-benefits that are also on social aspects. For example, increased water availability through increased infiltration will enhance access to water, reduce social tensions and competition and increase quality of life for the rangeland users; and improved soil moisture content will improve surface water quality with positive effects on the communities, especially during dry seasons when scarcity is experienced.

27. It is expected that the project will contribute to strengthening of local governance, participation and accountability, which in turn will contribute to wider processes of citizen engagement and empowerment. The project’s emphasis on conflict resolution mechanisms will reinforce the traditional social fabric of the pastoralists, which is a cornerstone of life in rangelands in Kenya.

### Needs of the recipient

**Soil:** High


14. Kenya, as a country with arid and semi-arid regions, has been facing severe droughts. The country experienced 23 drought events in 51 consecutive years between 1960 and 2011. The rangelands in ASALs are particularly vulnerable to drought, which is being exacerbated due to climate change. In the arid and semi-arid regions of Kenya, pastoralists and crop producers are the primary victims of climate change. Pastoralists are among the most vulnerable groups as they depend on livestock for their livelihoods. Development indicators such as mortality rates and literacy levels in Kenya’s ASALs have fewer social services per capita, while the availability of infrastructure is much less than elsewhere. Having sparse access to water resources, pastoralists are responsible for 80% of the national livestock production in terms of milk, meat and hide.

15. Due to frequent exposure to severe droughts in ASALs, people are forced to consider distress migration as a coping strategy. This has led to the formation of communities of pastoralist households. Food insecurity and erosion of nutrition resulting from frequent droughts further undermine coping capacities of the pastoralist households.


36. In the wake of climate-related water shortages in ASALs, crop producers also face frequent crop losses and food insecurity. The adverse effects of drought are often


---
**Page 105**
---

characterized by complete depletion of pastures, lack of water for livestock, massive movement of animals in search of water and pasture and much aggravated incidences of livestock diseases. Crops often perish when the top soil loses moisture without being replenished to compensate for evaporation. The combined effects of climate change and land degradation related risks are very high in ASALs and the need of the population for adaptive measures is high.

36. ASAL regions are generally lagging behind in Kenya in economic terms, compared to other areas. Moreover, the backwardness in ASAL regions cannot make much progress due to weak infrastructure and poor access to markets. The rural economy has been further decimated in recent times. Agriculture, forestry and fishing contribute 15.2 per cent of gross domestic product (GDP) as against 30.4 per cent for the urban areas which is a major economic product. However, pastoralism is struggling under climate change and making way for the expansion of agriculture and livestock production in the rangelands, thereby over and over diminishing water resources in the rangelands. In such conditions, neither livestock management nor crop production can be sustained without depleting resources on the rangelands. Consequently, poverty is perpetuated in the rangelands of ASAL regions.

37. The request for financing to GCF will be for the investment in creating public goods and services. As an African country, a financing gap exists in Kenya in this regard. Unfortunately, there seems to be no other source of financing that can be tapped into. Therefore, such a sector cannot be leveraged if the state-owned institutions are not able to fill the critical initial financing gap. The project is therefore designed with the aim of addressing and overcoming current barriers. Although it is a developing country, Kenya is entitled to access adaptation finance from GCF on the same basis as an African country. Therefore, the requested grant financing appears fully justified.

38. Component 2 of the project is intended to create an enabling environment through the strengthening of institutional mechanisms. Such opportunities to strengthen institutional and implementation capacity will be used to provide a conducive environment towards provisioning of better services to overcome climate change-related vulnerabilities.

39. The situational analysis clearly indicates that the needs of the recipients for the project are "high".

1.5 Country ownership Scale: High

40. The current project is designed on the foundation of a rich policy framework on climate change and sustainable land management in Kenya, which was initiated with the Climate Change Response Strategy 2010 and its National Climate Change Action Plan in 2013. Kenya carried out an adaptation technical analysis of economic sectors and prioritized adaptive actions, including those related to water resources and livestock production, both at national and country levels. The current project is a reflection of such policy elements.

41. The proposed specific objectives and outcomes are in synergy with national policies and strategies on climate change and sustainable land management as well as the National Strategy for Sustainable Land Management. For example, the project aligns with the National Climate Change Response Strategy 2010 and its National Climate Change Action Plan, the Nationally Determined Contributions and National Adaptation Plan. The project also aligns with the Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture strategy as well as Kenya Strategic Investment Framework for Sustainable Land Management, Livestock Policy and ASAL Policy. Therefore, the project

### Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: Medium to high**

- **Pasture management and crop agriculture are both micro-scale private activities in ASAL regions of Kenya.** These sectors have been highly constrained by droughts due to high risks associated with frequent droughts.
- The project therefore requests USD 23.15 million from GCF, which is equivalent to approximately KES 29.39 million. **The co-financing ratio appears on the higher side than most GCF financed projects.**

# Executive Summary

The Gender analysis in this report was carried out for the initially identified four project landscapes namely Moyale-Wajir North Baringo Hills; Sabotawas; Mid Tana River; and Chyulu Hills. These landscapes were selected because they are representative of the four major agro-ecological zones in Kenya, and two other landscapes were combined. The project will therefore focus on two landscapes namely (i) Mid Tana River and (ii) Chyulu Hills. The landscape analysis was carried out for the Moyale-Wajir North Baringo Hills landscape located in the semi-arid zone. Much of the analysis in this report refers to the original 4 landscapes but the analysis in the report on the project proposed as now described remains relevant.

Gender equality is a fundamental human right and an essential element of sustainable development. Gender equality is also a key principle for the implementation of the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs). The gender analysis aims at identifying gender differences, their causes, and the implications of these differences in relation to the project landscape. This analysis will be based on the following areas: sex-disaggregated data; knowledge, beliefs and perceptions; practices and participation; time and space; legal rights and status; power and decision-making; and access to resources.

The gender analysis was carried out using a participatory approach involving women, men, boys and girls spending a typical 24-hour day. While the Rani Parker’s Gender Analysis Matrix was used to gather insight into power relations and interests based on gender roles. Specific activities were undertaken in each landscape to collect data from different groups of people.

Ethical considerations were applied during the exercise. Overall, the collection and analysis of sex-disaggregated data is a key element for understanding the project landscapes and addressing differential needs of men and women.

Women in drylands face numerous gender inequalities, which included restricted access to productive assets; unequal distribution of land; reduced access to credit as compared to men; glaring differences in workload difference between men and women; and lack of power and decision to spend income. Further, women are more vulnerable than men to the effects of climate change.

Additional challenges faced by women in drylands include uneven access to health education and social protection services; limited access to markets for their produce; lack of land tenure security; uneven access to markets and producer groups; lack of recognition of women’s roles, knowledge and representation on decision-making bodies; and lack of information. The gender analysis will take into address the gender inequalities and challenges include education as a long term driver of livelihoods and income generation; improved access to health services; improved access to social protection services; improved access to risk management and insurance products; improved access to markets for crops and processing; and capacity strengthening through GIZ.

The socio-cultural context is an important element in the analysis. The key socio-cultural indicators presented, namely population size and composition, population type; population growth rate; literacy; housing; land tenure security; livestock ownership; livestock marketing; livestock trade; livestock breed; fishing activities; industry; cultural practices; poverty; Human Development Index (HDI); and Youth unemployment. The gender analysis will take into account the socio-cultural context of the project area is to enable an appreciation of the socio-cultural fabric that community members live and the effects of this on their livelihoods and day-to-day activities.

The Gender Analysis in this report was carried out based on existing data, which demonstrated disadvantage in the areas of reproductive health; empowerment and the labour market. It was used to demonstrate gender issues in the project landscapes. The analysis also showed that women were more vulnerable than men. Women demonstrated higher gender inequality than the national average, except Isiolo, Meru, Tharaka Nithi and Kilu Counties. Most of the rights were attributed to socio-cultural factors. Gill scores were not available for Mid Tana River and Chyulu Hills.

In most of the project area, women are involved in household chores and activities, while men primarily engage in income generating activities such as livestock keeping. Women spend more time with their mothers and females relatives in household chores and activities; while boys similarly support their fathers and males relatives in income generating activities. In addition to these productive and socio-economic resources, women in the project area have access to resources that include land, water, livestock (milking), crops, shelter, farming tools and some economic resources. However, these same resources are controlled by men.

## Page Number
iv


---
**Page 118**
---

Despite the above situation, women in Kenya are valued and protected through the legal and administrative framework, such as the Constitution, and Kenya’s ratification of international instruments that promote gender equity and equality. Various institutions have also been established by the Government to ensure that women participate fully in all aspects of development, including men and women in the development process. These include the Directorate of Gender in the Ministry of Devolution and Planning, the National Council for Women’s Development (NCWD), the Human Rights Commission (KHIRC) and the Anti-Female Genital Mutilation (FGM) Board. Civil Society Organizations (CSOs), private sector and religious organizations are also responsible for both implementation and monitoring.

Within the project area, resilience is demonstrated through traditional early warning systems, county information centers, community-based disaster risk management committees, and efforts to increase seed varieties for food, destocking, and awareness and education, among other measures. Pre-establishment of seed funds could prove to be very beneficial, although within a drought dryland projects funding would need to be directed towards development and research building priorities. Arrangements that increase women’s and men’s authority over resources should be promoted, and indigenous knowledge systems respected.

Men control access to most productive assets in Kenya, with dryland communities in Kenya sharing a patriarchal system of land ownership and management. Women are often excluded from decision-making as key stakeholders. Access to credit and markets has been identified as a key constraint in improving economic productivity; therefore improving women’s access to credit and opening up markets for products they produce is critical. This could be achieved by providing women with the means to go further to fetch water or firewood or that would increase the workload of women and girls in a household negatively, such as planting trees on steep slopes. Women’s participation in agricultural marketing unions may require first working with the husbands to gain agreement on any proposed activities. The project therefore needs to consider this.

The gender analysis report generated a Gender Action Plan in section 19. This provides entry points for gender-responsive actions to be taken under each of the activity areas of the Twende project. The action plan will be developed and finalized by the end of the baseline survey, during implementation and at that point will become a Gender and Youth Action plan in the inception phase of the project. To avoid duplication with other existing plans, the gender-responsive actions and interventions identified here will be incorporated into the detailed M&E plan to be developed at the start of implementation. The gender action plan will also be reviewed regularly during the course of the project so that gender disaggregated data is collected and subject to the requisite quality assessment process.

A gender task group will be established early in the project drawn from different institutions of the project. IUCN will lead that task group that will be responsible for implementing the GAP. Specific support will be provided by WFP’s Gender Section Office at the gender task group.

# Introduction

The goal of the proposed project is to contribute to ending recurring national drought emergencies in Kenya. This will be achieved, in part, by building the resilience of communities and ecosystems to future climate change impacts, while also taking advantage of opportunities for sustainable development and opportunities of dry rangelands. These include: climate uncertainty, ecological fragility, seasonal and annual variability, and a lack of infrastructure (e.g., roads, water supply systems) compared with more rapidly developing developed public structures, and strong cultural grass-roots natural resource management institutions and practices.

## 1.1 Gender Analysis in the Context of the Proposed Project

Gender analysis is a systematic analytical process used to identify, understand, and analyze gender differences in access to resources and opportunities for participation in a proposed programme. This involves examining the differential impacts of development policies and programs on women and men, and may include an assessment of the different roles, rights, responsibilities, and opportunities that define the different roles, rights, and opportunities of men and women and relations between them (USAID, 2011).

This analysis will employ the Six Domains of Gender Analysis Framework namely: Access, Knowledge, beliefs and perceptions, Practices participation, time and space, legal rights and status, power and decision making.

### 1.1.1 Access

This domain as used in this gender analysis looks at a person's or community's ability to use the necessary resources to be a fully active and productive member of the socio-society, economically, and politically. It will look at access to resources, income, services, employment, information, and benefits.

### 1.1.2 Knowledge, Beliefs, and Perception

This domain will look at knowledge that men and women possess; the beliefs that shape gender identities and behavior, and the different perceptions that guide people's understanding of their lives, depending upon their gender identity.

### 1.1.3 Practices and participation

This domain will look at behaviors, practices, and actions in terms of what they actually do and how this varies by gender. It will examine the roles men and women play within a community, including not only the ways in which men and women may engage differently in development activities. Some of these types of activities include attending training courses, accepting or seeking out services. Their participation can be both active and passive.

### 1.1.4 Time and Space

This domain looks at recognizing gender differences in the availability and allocation of time and the socialization that occurs over a space. It will analyze the division of both productive and reproductive labor; identification of how time is spent during the day (weekly, monthly or yearly), and in different seasons; and the ways in which men and women are constrained by their roles within family, community, and society. The overall objective of this domain is to determine how men and women manage their time and what implications their time commitments have on their availability for program activities.

# Methodology

Gender analysis requires the collecting and analysing sex-disaggregated information in order to understand gender differences. Gender analysis explores gender differences so policies, programs or projects can be designed with women’s needs and priorities in mind. It also facilitates the strategic use of the distinct knowledge and skills women possess.

A gender analysis was proposed and carried out in order to:
- Determine the extent to which women and men benefit from development and are equally empowered.
- Understand how gender roles, responsibilities and inequities affect the project’s effectiveness and the sustainability of its results.


The analysis adopted the gender analysis framework approach elaborated in the previous chapter. Three (3) key steps were followed:

1. Preparing for the gender study: This involved developing the scope of work and work plan; assembling the team, clarifying stakeholder expectations, acquiring necessary resources, conducting a desk review and preparing the project’s baseline.
2. Clarifying client expectations: This involved taking time to understand the client and proposal requirements, as well as the client’s expectations for the gender analysis (e.g., gender technical teams) and directors. These interviews were arranged at the beginning and throughout the process of the gender analysis in order to ensure that all parties understood the client team(s).
3. Conducting the desk review: This provided us with an opportunity to make sure you are clear on required deliverables and timelines.


Preparation for the work and scheduling of timelines. Once the team was clear on priorities and expectations, a schedule of timelines and intended deliverables was developed jointly by the gender analysis team and the client. Regular check-ins were done both sides. Regular check-ins were done both by phone, email and face to face conversations.

Conducting desk review: The desk review is an important step in understanding the target population and the context in which the project proposal is operating. The Desk review helped to gather information on the following areas (i) gender statistics and data; (ii) policy documents; (iii) landscape analysis; and (iv) priority landscapes and courses as base information to complement the results and findings of interviews with key stakeholders.

The desk review documents included: statistical reports from government departments

# Gender Analysis Study

## Type of Study: What type of docs were reviewed? How the documents helped

### Program, project or organizational description, baseline study report, barrier analysis, gender equality action plan and other start-up reports
- Understanding project context and how gender equality issues have been integrated into planning

### Quantitative data: demographic and health surveys from the World Bank, UN and government
- Quantitative, contextual information on gender dynamics within the country

### Third-party gender studies: gender analyses, assessments or research papers.
- Qualitative, contextual information on gender dynamics within the counties and priority landscapes

## Conducting field work: Fieldwork is one of the most significant stages of a gender study. Several tools were used to collect data including focus group discussions, interviews, and quantitative surveys.

### Focus group discussions
- These involved small groups of participants and community members in each county and priority landscape.
- The focus groups provide information relating to activity schedules, access and control of resources, and the role of women and men within households and communities. The focus groups were conducted using different groups from numerous regions, a wide range of ages, and both men-only, with only women, and mixed groups of men and women together.
- Some topics that emerged in these discussions included: who makes decisions about household resources; who controls the income earned by each group of the "voiceless" heard. Most communities interviewed have a dominant patriarchal society where they interacted with women with full participation and airing of views.

### Informant interviews
- These were semi-structured interviews conducted with key informants across selected counties and priority landscapes.
- These were semi-structured, allowing them to remain focused on the objectives of the interview and use the interview as a tool for exploring issues that arose during the discussion. A core set of semi-structured interview questions was developed in advance, but each session was unique and allowed the opportunity to answer at their own pace thus helping to control, probe, direct and gather information as accurately.


---
**Page 122**
---

3) Stakeholder consultations: This involved organizing workshop stakeholder meetings with key persons and professionals at all the targeted thirteen countries plus SCN and other government representatives. The meetings discussed and came up with key priority areas for intervention. Stakeholder consultation and meeting reports were developed and shared with SCN and partners.

4) Note-taking and recording All fieldwork was properly documented and recorded to assist in triangulating findings against gender study deliverables. The study used assistant note-takers to sit in on fieldwork sessions.

2.1 Ethical considerations

A number of ethical considerations were outlined and considered when conducting the gender study:
- Respect for informants - The project team and interviewers demonstrated respect for participants by gaining informed consent from them before beginning fieldwork.
- Anonymity and confidentiality - All data collected was anonymized, coded, and adhered to confidentiality in the reporting and sharing of data.
- Motivation and empowerment - The study aimed to motivate participants to discuss attitudes, beliefs and experiences as important processes of self-reflection and empowerment.
- Nudge for informed consent - The study aimed to nudge expectations of participants by giving realistic estimates of what participants would expect and when resources will be available.

1) Analysing and validating gender field data: Data analysis was carried out using activity schedules, access and control tables, constraints and opportunities tables to clearly bring out the different groups’ responses. This involved comparing the same group’s responses across all sites and compare the different groups' responses from the same county and landscape priority site.

2) Developing findings & recommendations: The gender analyst identified actions that are most likely to be effective in addressing the gender equality and empowerment issues. Gender recommendations should be specific, action-oriented and realistic. Recommendations focused on the following:
- Development of a Results framework and associated indicators that could be incorporated into program/project planning and activity design.
- A logical framework for monitoring and evaluation of project activities and expected outcomes, outputs and products.
- Identification of required financial resources by county governments, national government, SCN and other partners.
- Development of strategies to address gender equality and equity that should be incorporated within the program/project's current activities to achieve greater equality and empowerment for women.

3 Background

The project is proposed to be located in eleven of Kenya’s Arid and Semi-Arid (ASALs) counties: Garissa, Tana River, Isiolo, Marsabit, Samburu, Kilifi, Kitui, Makueni, Tharaka, Nithi, Meru and Taita Taveta. The focus is on the drylands where livestock development and land use across county boundaries to coordinate movement of livestock across different counties. The priority landscapes are:

- 1. Tana River/Samburu
- 2. Chyulu Hills

The project will use an Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EBA) approach with emphasis on integration of restoration, livelihoods, and ecosystem services in the drylands. It aims to address issues such as land degradation, agriculture, forests, and water - as well as mainstreaming rangeland and forest concerns into other sectors. The project will also focus on integrating climate change adaptation strategies across the latter sectors to mitigate the impact of large infrastructure on dryland social and ecological systems.

The project area is shown in the map below.


![](image.png)

Figure 3.1 Priority Landscapes

### Description of the Drylands of Kenya and Project Area

Drylands - commonly referred to Arid and Semi-arid regions (ASALs) - in Kenya make up to 80% of the country’s total land surface and are mainly found in the Northern, Eastern and Rift Valley regions. This region is characterized by low rainfall, high evaporation rates, and a predominantly semi-arid environment dominating the region. The drylands also support nearly 75% of wildlife population and consequently account for over 80% of Kenya’s livestock production. Despite their importance as an important resource base, people of the drylands regions are relatively poorer with fewer social services and less infrastructure than other parts of the country. This is partly due to the vulnerability of these areas to climate change. Livestock production accounts for 24% of agricultural outputs in Kenya with over 70% of the countries livestock coming from the ASALs region. Despite this, drylands receive insufficient investment and support compared to other regions. The project area will focus on improving livelihoods in poverty reduction and national development.

# Existing Gender Inequalities facing women in drylands

## Access to productive assets

### Women control access to most productive assets in Kenya (World Bank 2003). The main ethnic groups in Kenya have different inheritance laws and customs regarding land ownership, which affects the distribution of assets such as land, livestock, and businesses. A woman, for example, may milk the family cow and sell the milk at a market but will not call the cow itself because it is "owned" by her husband.

## Women land ownership

Only 3% of the land is owned by women (CEDAW 2011) in Kenya. With land being the primary asset in Kenya and 70% of households in agriculture and rigid inheritance traditions, means that men own almost all of the land. This makes it difficult for women to have their name on a land deed, but women's access to land is still controlled by men.

## Social stipulations regarding land inheritance

In Kenya, social stipulations prohibit a woman inheriting land (CEDAW 2011). While women can legally inherit land under the 2010 constitution, in practice it is forbidden and rare. Among Kenya’s 47 counties, majority of them have laws that prevent women from inheriting land. The main reason for this stipulation is that women inheriting land is that she will have access to land in place of marriage and that her to inherit land is a sign that she is not married or has no husband. This assumption is that the land would be unfair to her male siblings. This assumes a woman will marry (5% of Kenyan women never do), will not be widowed, and will not have any children. The Kenyan government says that this access and ownership are equal (which they clearly are not) (CEDAW 2011).

## Access to credit by Women as compared to Men

Women have less access to credit than men (CEDAW 2011). This is because women rarely have land deeds, which makes it difficult for them to secure loans. The government has engaged in a study in recent years, and all the major commercial banks in Kenya now have at least one financial credit or mortgage product targeting women. The government has initiated several programs to provide women with credit facilities, but these are still not sufficient to meet the needs of women living in remote areas of Kenya. It works by women group members pooling resources, with each member contributing a small amount to borrow the pooled balance.

## Time and space

One of the greatest gender difference in Kenya is the workload difference between of men and women. Data from the agriculture sector shows that women do 85% of the food production, 50% of cash crop production, 90% of post-harvest activities such as cleaning, sorting, packaging, and marketing, and 60% of the harvesting and marketing of crops (ADB 2007). Women have simultaneous and competing roles in agriculture, livestock rearing, child care, and household chores. These roles and income poverty often reinforce each other. This means any activity that adds to a woman's time burden may negatively impact other areas of her life. Avoiding such activities, reducing them to a minimum, or mitigating their effects is critical for women’s empowerment. The amount of time spent on daily time use are critical for designing local activities and avoid deepening the time poverty of women.


---
**Page 125**
---

### 4.1.8 Power and decision to spend income

42% of women who earn cash income say they mainly decide how to spend it (DHS 2010). Many women can decide on their own how to spend money they earn, but for almost half the women who earn cash income (49%), their spending decisions are made jointly with the husband. For 9% of women with cash income, the decision is made by someone else in the household. In addition, more women in Kenya invest more of their cash income in family needs and children's education than men do.

### 4.1.7 Female headed households face greater inequalities

In Kenya, 29% of households are female-headed (DHS 2010). Poverty among female headed households is higher than that for male headed households. Female heads of household also face greater time and mobility constraints than do male heads or other women. The majority of female headed households in Kenya are below the poverty line. Including female headed households in this project can help ensure project benefits are distributed to both poor and better off households.
