# Gender Challenges facing women in drylands

Women living in drylands face a myriad of challenges namely: Unequal distribution of resources and access to services, uneven distribution of land and natural resource rights, uneven livestock ownership and access to related products, uneven access to health, education and social protection, uneven access to markets and producer groups, neglect of women's traditional knowledge.

## 5.2 Challenges facing women in drylands

Source: UNESCO (Achieving gender equality women empowerment, 2015)

### 5.1 Unequal distribution of resources and access to services

With rising uncertainty caused by climate and other rural stressors, households need resources to cope and adapt. However, women are more vulnerable as they have less access to these necessary resources: e i land for cropping, and livestock.

### 5.2 Uneven distribution of land and natural resource rights

Land is important for the resilience of women and their related communities. Women tend to have access to and control over small plots of land, while men have more extensive landholdings. Most women have adequate access to land but do not have absolute control rights over it. Land tenure, legal, natural resource and land resource rights can be as important as women's livelihoods.

### 5.3 Uneven livestock ownership and access to related products

In drylands, livestock ownership and access to associated products is very important for ensuring survival of households in the face of climate change and other rural stressors. Women are more vulnerable than men when it comes to livestock ownership and access to livestock products. While access to livestock for pastoralist women may be more restricted than that of men, compared to other natural resources, they do have livestock rights – often for smaller stock – and rights over associated products such as milk and meat. However, women are less likely to own livestock or to be livestock managers (Kipuri and Ridgewell, 2008).


---
**Page 127**
---

5.4 Uneven access to health, education and social protection services

A key challenge facing drylands is ineffective delivery of both curative and preventive health services to dryland communities especially women. This is compounded by the fact that pastoralist women face particular challenges in accessing these services due to their mobility patterns, which can cause stress to the body (Fritan, 2006). The designs of current education systems are inappropriate for nomadic groups and schools are not sufficiently secure and decent (see Reidy, 2012; Tigheen et al., 2013). Women in drylands also face challenges related to the lack of infrastructure making learning and education difficult. Social protection programmes in dryland zones need to accommodate women’s mobility patterns and be designed with consideration for their reproductive roles and mobility of women and them taking care of elderly ones at a household level (Milton and Kervan, 2013).

5.5 Uneven access to climate change responses and disaster risk reduction measures

Pastoralists and other dryland communities are limited seasonal forecast information for understanding climate variability and its impact on development, in coordinating input and credit supply, food crisis management, trade and agricultural insurance (Dasgupta et al., 2014). It is important to design gender-specific climate change adaptation strategies that take into account the different roles of men and women in a forecast of rainfall cessation, onset, not and that the choice of communication channels must relate to socio-cultural norms and practices. The role of women in dryland communities should inform the design of new climate services in order to increase resilience (CGIAR CCAFS, 2015).

5.6 Uneven access to animal health care services

Animal health services are mainly received by men when receiving training and replacement stock. Most time information is shared through male dominated committees or livestock producer groups. There is limited access for women in dryland communities to receive training on animal health services, focused training on small ruminant and poultry target women, training of female veterinarians to take care of animals at the household level, and training through adult education for women to participate in animal health services at the household level.

5.7 Uneven access to markets and producer groups

Dryland women tend to have more limited access to markets than their male counterparts. This is mainly because: • Limited access to productive assets such as livestock and land; low literacy levels; lack of expertise in marketing and business skills; lack of experience in capturing value in value chains; limited access to markets; a lack of time for income-generating activities; lack of financial services; lack of information on market prices and trends; lack of linking including in development projects (Rota et al., 2012). Specifically, dryland women have limited access to markets for the following reasons:

• A relative lack of contacts and information

• Limited participation in livestock committees reducing their influence

• Limited financial services such as credit and savings schemes for livestock keeping

• Higher illiteracy levels, lack of experience and limited financial skills

• Lower prices offered by traders who know they have fewer options due to their limited mobility and access to markets

• Need to have a husband’s permission to make livestock sales

• A common practice in dryland communities is that women are not allowed to sell her woman's animal or products

• Milk sales by private sector dairy collection plants usually consists of households' heads

• Government regulations on informal milk sales and other foods of animal origin Miller.


---
**Page 128**
---

5.8 Lack of recognition of women’s roles, knowledge and barriers to Gender roles

Traditional gender norms often mean that women have heavier workloads, as well as the resultant unequal allocation and control of resources. In most dryland communities, there is a lack of recognition of women as rights holders and members of a community group, who are entitled to land and natural resource use (Miller, 2011). Women also face barriers in accessing education and participating in decision-making processes due to social norms and practices such as sexual and gender-based violence and harmful practices (Miller, 2011).

5.9 Education as a long-term driver of livelihood diversification

Dryland girls and women tend to have much less access to education. For pastoralists, especially girls, a combination of adult education, community and locally relevant broadcast programmes and participation in income-generating activities can help improve their educational status (Mills and Over, 2009).

5.10 Neglect of dryland women’s traditional knowledge

In general, pastoralists at both ends have extensive knowledge of livestock and production i.e., tending animals, e.g., milking or caring for sick animals (Firth, 2008). In the past, women's traditional knowledge was not recognized as a valuable resource. However, in recent years, there has been greater recognition of indigenous knowledge and women’s knowledge. While recognition of the gendered nature of indigenous knowledge and women’s knowledge has grown in recent years, further recognition is still needed. Currently, most institutions do not accord much value to women's traditional knowledge and role in dryland resilience (Miller, 2011)

# Action to address Gender Inequalities and Challenges

1. It is important for the project to include and target female headed households so as to ensure project benefits are distributed to both poor and better off households. Providing offsets greater than the cost of land acquisition can help in this regard.
2. Improving drinking water quality and quantity may have direct benefits to women and girls.
3. Sensitivity to the traditional divisions of labor may help gender initiatives to be more socially acceptable and sustainable over time.
4. It is fundamentally critical to understand season calendars and daily use time for designing local activities that will not deepen the time poverty within a project.
5. Activities which include the use of private resources like land or livestock should include men as well as women in order to ensure that it is better understood how access and control between men and women of resources.
6. The role of women in dryland systems, will continue for some time hence it is important to include both local men and women as key stakeholders even though women may have no legal title to the land in question.
7. It is important that women's access to agricultural inputs and integrated access to credit elements in dry land projects so as to improve women's economic productivity.
8. Providing support for the Ministry of Gender, Child and Social Development's program to eliminate child marriage is another option that will improve the health of girls locally and through this project

## Improving dryland women’s access to health services to underpin dryland resilience

Improving health services for women in dryland zones can improve household resilience due to their critical role as caregivers. In addition, women also need for inclusion of on nutrition education in pastoral women's health and indeed that of the wider family so women tend to pay greater attention to ensuring children are fed properly. A package of interventions aimed at improving the livelihood systems of the household (Fintan,2008).

## Improving gender-equitable social protection services for drylands resilience

There is need to understand the gendered nature of the risks women can even face, and how gender dynamics can exacerbate or ameliorate their vulnerability. This includes tapping into increasing their access to financial resources, such as micro-credit and micro-finance (World Bank, 2012). Social protection schemes have shown great promise in supporting vulnerable groups in dryland areas especially women. The Horn of Africa region has been a beneficiary of the climate change adaptation government funded by the World Bank is a promising example of a large-scale safety net programme, which has strengthened household and environmental resilience in a gender equitable manner.

## Improving women’s access to risk management and insurance products

A key priority for improving women's resilience to climate variability and shocks through investment in their access to and uptake of risk management and insurance products. For example, the Horn of


---
**Page 130**
---

# Improving Women’s access to animal health services

Improving animal health is important for pastoralists and agro-pastoralists. In improving women access to animal health services there is need to ensure that more female Community-based Animal Health Workers (CAHWs) are trained in the use of modern veterinary medicines, such as vaccines and veterinary medicine which reach women in remote locations including pastoralists.

# Improving dryland women’s access to livestock markets and development

Dryland women's empowerment can be supported by linking women to markets for livestock related products in a sustainable and economic environment. In pastoralist societies, although livestock assets are not equally distributed, compared to other productive resources, women do own and have rights to livestock (Morten & Keeney 2013). This is because women are the primary custodians of livestock and they play an important role in managing them. Women also have a key way to engineering access to livestock markets (Miller, 2011). Further, this can be strengthened through creation of women agricultural and market groups that enhance leadership and market skills (Miller, 2011).

# Improving dryland women’s access to markets for crops and processing

Promoting income generation for pastoralist women through value addition of dryland crops and processing is another way to empower them. This can be done by linking women farmers sometimes in the wider community (IFAD, 2009). Investment in women producers makes commercial sense for companies that operate at both the low-value, high volume end of the market (Chen, 2011).

# Improving support for women’s participation in alternative livelihoods and their ability to capture value

Investment is needed in women's access to alternative livelihoods, to ensure that their rights and interests are recognized. For example, there is significant potential for enhancing the collection and processing of natural products from dryland areas i.e. gum arabic. This needs to have good quality support to ensure that it does not fall into the same trap as other sectors which were neglected (Morten & Keeney, 2013).

# Improving our understanding of gender relations to inform policy and programming through the devolution structure-county governments

There is increasing demand for gender equality around the world, but also investment and political will is important, and understanding how change might happen or could be facilitated is important. Through the county government structure, there are opportunities at the sub-county level to facilitate women's empowerment through affirmative action at the county, sub-county and ward levels.

# Capacity strengthening actions through CSOs

Capacity strengthening actions are required to strengthen the resilience of drylands and to empower women.


---
**Page 131**
---

- CSOs to support gender justice involving whole communities, men, and local leaders, to challenge discriminatory social norms and harmful practices.
- CSOs, academic institutions and the media to increase awareness of gender, pastoralist, and environmental sustainability issues in the drylands.
- This will help to counteract negative stereotypes of dryland areas to culturally revalue them and women's knowledge and equal rights in particular

# Socio-economic Background of the Project Area

The Gender analysis in this report was carried out for the initially identified four project landscapes namely Moyale-Wajir North Banisa Hills; Sabawawa; Mid Tana River; and Chiyulu Hills. These landscapes were selected because they are representative of the semi-arid zone where most of the project support and two other landscapes were combined. The project will therefore focus on two landscapes namely (i) to Mabwale in Moyale-Wajir North Banisa Hills landscape located in the semi-arid zone and (ii) Chiyulu Hills landscape located in the semi-arid zone. Much of the analysis in this report refers to the original 4 landscapes but the analysis in the report to the project proposal on now described remains relevant.

The purpose of this section is to provide a background of the socio-economic situation, enable an appreciation of the socio-cultural fabric that community members live, and the effects of this on their livelihoods and day-to-day activities.

8 Gender issues in the project area

The Gender Inequality Index (GII) reflects gender-based disadvantage in three dimensions – reproductive health, empowerment and the labour market. The index also demonstrates the loss of potential human development due to these disadvantages. The GII is a composite measure of these dimensions. It varies between 0 – when women and men fare equally, and 1 – where one gender fares as poorly as possible.

Kenya has an overall GII of 0.651 (draft 7th Human Development Report). This is however, not equal everywhere, as there are regional disparities with Counties located in and around semi-lands having high Gender Inequality Indexes.

The assessment of the gender inequality index was done for the initially identified four project landscapes namely Moyale-Wajir North Banisa Hills; Sabawawa; Mid Tana River; and Chyulu Hills. The target landscape for this study is the Moyale-Wajir North Banisa Hills landscape, which is the Moyale-Wajir North Banisa Hills landscape dropped. The project will therefore focus two landscapes namely the Mid Tana River (semi-lands) and (semi-)landscapes located in the end zone and the Chyulu Hills landscape located in the semi-and lands.

8.1 Moyale-Wajir North Banisa Hills Landscape Site

The Counties represented in this landscape site also have a higher GII than the national level, with Marsabit having the highest of 0.732. The high GII witnessed in this landscape site are attributed mainly to social and cultural factors.

While HHs headed by women have fewer economic resources than men. Women also do not enjoy equal rights in the use of assets like land; and are thus denied economic power. | The basic gender concerns in the County relates to limited access to education, traditional cultural practices that hinder women’s participation in productive activities and decision making. |

#### 8.3 Mid Tana River Landscape Site

Isiolo, Meru, Tharaka Nithi and Kilifi Counties have Gils that are lower than the national level, with Kilifi having the lowest Gil among the eastern and coastal counties. Conversely, Tana River County has the highest Gil. Similar to the other landscape sites, the Gils may be attributed primarily to socio-cultural practices rather than economic development or lack thereof experienced by women.

# Gender Assessment of Project Area

A gender assessment was carried out through the Harrop Gender Analysis Framework and Raci Parker’s Gender Analysis Matrix. With the understanding that women and men are affected by development projects in different ways, the analysis is based on three types of profiles: Activity Profiles-Schedules, and Access and Control Profiles. The use of Activity Schedules helps to explain how men and women spend their time during a typical day (e.g., from when they wake up until they go to bed [24-hour day]). Access and Control Profiles, on the other hand, are used to analyse content and potential power relations between individuals and groups within and against each other, at the level of individual households and communities. The analysis is based on data collected in 1998.

The Gender analysis in this report was carried out for the initially identified four project landscapes namely the Chiyu Hills landscape, the Mid Tana River landscape, the Sabiebene landscape and the Chiyu Hills landscape. These landscapes were reduced to two landcapes mainly: 1) the Mid Tana River and Sabiebene landscapes and the 2) Chiyu Hills landscape. Much of the analysis in this report refers to the original 4 landscapes but as the analysis is based on the project proposals as now described remains relevant.

## 9.1 Methodology for Activity Schedules

### Step 1: Identification of Seasons
- Identifying the busiest and the quietest time of the year in terms of women’s workload.
- Preparing a daily activity chart for the busiest season and a separate one for the quietest season.
- Making an assumption about the type of household (e.g. marital status, number of adults and children) that will be represented by the information.

### Step 2: Preparation of Busiest Schedule: Women describe their own activities
1. Asking the group what time they wake up each morning during the busiest time of the year.
2. Asking the group to describe their day, starting from when they wake up and concluding with where they go to bed. Recording all the activities they undertake around an approximate timing.

### Step 3: Preparation of Busiest Schedule: Women describe men’s activities
1. Continuously with the busiest season, repeat step 2 but focusing on the opposite sex with women describing the activities of the male members in their households.
2. Repeat steps 2 and 3 for the quietest time of the year.

### Step 4: Preparation of Quietest Schedule

### Step 5: Analysis
1. Concluding the exercise by asking the group to calculate (with reference to the information collected) the number of hours worked by women and men during the busiest and quietest times of the year, and their respective hours of rest during the day.
2. Reflecting on the findings.

**Page 156**
---

9.2 Moyale-Wajir North Banisa Hills Landscape Site-Marsabit and Mandera Counties

Presented below are the Activity Schedules for Marsabit and Mandera Counties. In Marsabit County, women are involved in most household chores and activities, while boys participate in crop cultivation, nearly all of which is done by hand. Girls take care of livestock and water collection, roles often overburden women. Men primarily take care of mature livestock and ensure herds seek for suitable grazing and watering points even if it means moving away with their livestock for several months and hundreds of kilometers from home. Livestock wealth is considered a source of security in Marsabit County, being considered as a source of wealth (ICDP Marsabit, 2013). There is low enrollment and high drop-out rates in schools due to the heavy workload on girls who are expected to balance their school responsibilities and training. Most Girls in Marsabit County spend their time sourcing for fuel wood, fetching water, child care for younger siblings, and other household chores.

In Mandera County, men, women, boys and girls participate in all economic activities, and most household activities. Boys and girls do not participate in community meetings, while all except girls take a household role. Girls are expected to balance their school responsibilities with household chores such as food preparation. Long distances travelled by Girls in search of fuelwood and water can expose them to Gender based violence and also make them miss school and education or end performing poorly if enrolled.

### 3.2.1 Activity Schedules

#### MARSABIT COUNTY
In Marsabit county, Men spend most of their daily time carrying out activities related to: land clearing, crop cultivation, livestock management, and herding. Women are involved in most household chores such as the Men in land clearing however most of their daily time is spent on family care-related activities. Girls in Marsabit County take care of livestock and water collection, roles often overburden women. Boys participate in food preparation. Long distances travelled by Girls in search of fuelwood and water can expose them to Gender based violence and also make them miss school and education or end performing poorly if enrolled.

## Methodology: Access and Control Profiles

### Step 1: Resources
1. With the group, draw up a list of all resources available to people in the village for domestic use (such as water or off-farm work).
2. Record the list in the left-hand column of the matrix.

### Step 2: Explain the difference between access and control
Access represents the ability to use a resource (such as an axe, or to work on the land) without having the authority to make decisions about its use.
Control represents the full authority to make decisions about the use of a resource.


---
**Page 160**
---

# Step 3: Demonstrate Access to Resources

1. Using 10 stones to represent 10 points, ask the group to indicate relative access to a resource to women and men. For example, 10 stones allocated to women and zero to men indicates that women have exclusive access to a particular resource; five stones to women and five to men means equal access for both genders. Two stones more in favor of women and eight to men indicates that men have more access to a resource than women.

# Step 3: Demonstrate Control over Resources

1. Determine who has control over each resource, again allocating 10 points between women and men.
2. In some cases, control of a resource may lie outside the household. For instance, an institution determines who receives credit or attends a training course. Such situations are indicated by the term *others*.

# Step 4: Analyze

1. Once it is established who has access to and control over all the different resources on the list, rank the top five resources. Note who has access to and who has control over these five resources.
2. Ask the group to note the types of resources women and men tend to have either access to, or control over.

# Step 5: Reflect on the findings.

## 9.2.3 Access and Control Profiles

Presented below are the Access and Control Profiles for Marsabit and Mandera Counties. In summary, in Marsabit men do not access/ use resources, but women do; yet men exclusively control the resources. In Mandera, both men and women have equal access to all resources, but only men control the resources, including land. These means that the Women in Marsabit are in a better position when it comes to resource access, power and decision making at household level.

**MARSABIT COUNTY**

In Marsabit County, women have access to utilization of resources namely: land, Water, Livestock (Milking), Crops, Shelter (Houses), Farming tools and other economic resources. However, the same women do not control these resources; men control all these resources in the household. This places women in a vulnerable position in terms of Gender equality because they lack the power and position to make decisions on how these resources are used. In addition, when it comes to deciding ownership of resources, the man automatically controls power and decision making in the household.

# Opportunities and Constraints

Opinions on factors that presented opportunities or acted as constraints to both women and men were sought, and are presented below for the Counties of Marsabit and Mandera.

## Marsabit County

Key opportunities for women are related to access of resources like young livestock, land, crop and farming tools. The main constraint is that the household is food secure but nutritionally insecure. Each harvest can be sold or traded to bring income. Combined with affirmative action in the new constitution through devolution, there will be more opportunities for women to participate in development activities through welfare groups and other county government structures. There is need to raise awareness on need to educate the girl child while using influential Men in the community as change agents.

In Kitui County, land clearing and cultivation is a family affair done by all members. ### Access and Control Profiles

Presented below are the Access and Control Profiles for Tharaka Nithi, Kilifi and Tana River Counties.

In Tharaka Nithi County, men and women use most of the resources, but control of most of the resources is taken by men. Only water and crops are owned by women.

Similarly in Kilifi County, men have overall control over all resources. Control of most of the resources is taken by men, but women also control some resources, particularly water and crops.

In Tana River County, men access and control almost all the resources, except water which is accessed and controlled equally between men and women.


#### THARAKA NITHI COUNTY

In Tharaka Nithi County, Women have access to utilization of resources namely: land, Water, Livestock, Crops, Skills, Tools/equipment, Economic resources (small scale enterprises), Education, Health, Community leadership, Political representation. However, the same women do not have control over these resources. The men have overall control these resources. This places women in a vulnerable position in terms of Gender equality because they lack decision making power and control over resources which is at the household level. By controlling ownership of resources, the man automatically controls power and decision making within the family.

NB: Women have a unique control and ownership of water and crops. This means that women gender status can be improved by leveraging on their opportunity to control what is cultivated and sold in the market. Women are also able to access credit facilities from banks and microfinance institutions, as well as participate in women groups, SHGs and CSOs. They are also able to take up collateral loans using crops sold in the market.

In Kitul County, Women have access to utilization of resources namely land, Water, Livestock (Milkings), Crops, Shelter (Houses); Farming tools and other economic resources. However, the same women do not have control over these resources. The men have overall control these resources.

Presented below are the Access and Control Profiles for Taita Taveta County. Women control water, crops, education, and health. On the other hand, men control all the resources except water and crops, while women control water, crops, education, and health.

### TAITA TAVETA COUNTY

Woman have access to utilization of resources namely: land, Water, Livestock (Milkings), Crops, Shelter (Houses). Farming tools and other economic resources. However, the same woman do not have control over these resources. This places women in a vulnerable position in terms of Gender equality because they lack the power and authority to make decisions on how these resources are used. Women can only gain control by controlling ownership of resources, which automatically controls power and decision making in the household.

NB: Women have a unique control and ownership of water, education, health and crops. This means that women gender status can be improved by leveraging on their opportunity to control what they own and use for their benefit and the benefit of their families and communities.

# Summary of gender domains relationships across the priority landscapes

## Access

- **Moyowe-Wajir Landscape Site**: Limited access to resources and land.
- **North Nile**: Women have limited control over resources but can earn income from selling wood and water.
- **Suba-Swara Scope Site**: Women have limited control over resources, with most of their time spent on household activities.
- **Land Mid Tana River Landscape Site**: Men and women have equal access to land resources but limited control. Most women work in informal jobs.
- **Chyulu Hills Landscape Site**: Women have good access to land resources but limited control.

## Knowledge & beliefs/ perceptions

- **Moyowe-Wajir Landscape Site**: Most women have little knowledge or belief about land and its importance for their livelihoods, while men and girls consider it important to get educated compared to women.
- **North Nile**: Women lack knowledge of land use and its importance in society; women and girls are not considered important to get education compared to men.
- **Suba-Swara Scope Site**: Most cultural laws allow women as equals or more than men, with women taking control over land and water.
- **Land Mid Tana River Landscape Site**: Most cultural laws allow women to own land, crops, and farming tools. Women have a unique control in the area of cattle and cropping; women participate in decision-making. SinDa and GAD are common.
- **Chyulu Hills Landscape Site**: Most women education is provided by men, modifying patriarchal society.

## Practices & participation

- **Moyowe-Wajir Landscape Site**: Women perform low-level activities like taking care of livestock; power is a big issue.
- **North Nile**: Women perform low-level activities such as caring for children, cattle, and crop raising. FGM is common. FGM is also practiced on women.
- **Suba-Swara Scope Site**: Women have less control over land resources and decision-making.
- **Land Mid Tana River Landscape Site**: Women have less control of land resources and decision-making; the right to own land is limited.
- **Chyulu Hills Landscape Site**: Women perform low-level activities like taking care of livestock, with power being a big issue.

## Legal rights

- **Moyowe-Wajir Landscape Site**: 83% of inhabitants believe that women are being majority discriminated against in the use and control over land resources.
- **North Nile**: Women have less access to land resources and decision-making; the right to own land is limited.
- **Suba-Swara Scope Site**: Women have less control over land resources and decision-making. The right to own land is limited.
- **Land Mid Tana River Landscape Site**: Women have less control of land resources and decision-making, with gender attitudes being a big issue for women.
- **Chyulu Hills Landscape Site**: Women have less access to land resources and decision-making; the right to own land is limited.

## Power & decision making

- **Moyowe-Wajir Landscape Site**: Lack of power and position in society and influence power over household decisions. Gender attitudes are a big issue for women.
- **North Nile**: Women lack power and position in society, with less control of land resources; the right to own land is limited.
- **Suba-Swara Scope Site**: Women have less control of land resources and decision-making; the right to own land is limited. Gender attitudes are a big issue for women.
- **Land Mid Tana River Landscape Site**: Women lack power and position in society, with less control over land used for crop production and livestock grazing. The right to own land is limited by men.
- **Chyulu Hills Landscape Site**: Women have less control of land resources and decision-making; the right to own land is limited by men.

# 11 Legal framework Protecting Women Gender Equality

## 11.1 Legal framework Protecting Women Gender Equality

Since its adoption in 1994, the UNCCD has been recognized as the only multilateral agreement on the environment that addresses gender issues, because of its explicit focus on the roles of women and men in relation to land degradation and desertification. While some governments have emphasized women's empowerment or gender mainstreaming as part of their national action programmes, others have not. The 1994 UNCCD Conference of the Parties (COP) adopted a Gender Action Plan (GAP), and the International Year of Deserts and Desertification urge governments to take action on reducing desertification and land degradation by 2000. The GAP is based on the recognition of the commitment to gender equality already embodied in the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW). It also recognizes that women represent new opportunities for link solutions to poverty with gender equality. In addition, Kenya is party to various international and regional gender instruments, which demonstrates the Government's commitment to gender equality. These include the African Charter on Human Rights and Peoples' Rights; the Convention on Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW); the Beijing Platform for Action; the Optional Protocol to CEDAW; the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and People's Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa; and the Optional Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa.

On 27th August 2015, Kenya promulgated a new constitutional dispensation. This was hailed as the second republic. It ended the 20 years old struggle by Kenyans clamouring for a new constitutional order. It among other things brought in recognition women's rights as human rights. The Constitution of Kenya (2010) is the supreme law of the land and it provides for gender equality at all levels of leadership. The decentralization of power created 47 County governments this has revolutionized leadership at the lower level, bringing many women into public leadership space.

Other additional gains for women from the 2010 Constitution include:

- Equality in leadership with 33% as the critical mass preferred for women leadership
- Equality in representation
- Equality in employment
- Equal access to education
- All discriminatory customary practices are prohibited
- Maternal and child health services are guaranteed
- Women rights to inheritance and to own land is guaranteed
- Equal pay for equal work
- Requirement for both public and private entities to comply with the inclusion principles

The Constitution of Kenya (2010) under the Bill of Rights (Chapter 4) recognizes the rights and fundamental freedoms of all persons without distinction as to race, place of origin, gender or religion on any ground, including sex. Furthermore, Article 27 in the Bill of Rights stipulates that women and men have the right to equal treatment including the right to equal opportunities in political, economic and social life.

The Government has established various institutions with specific mandates to promote gender equality and women empowerment. These include the Directorate General of Gender in the Ministry of Devolution and Planning; National Gender and Equality Commission (NGEC); Kenya National Human Rights Commission (KNHRC); and the Anti-Female Genital Mutilation (AFGM) Board. Gender equality and empowerment of women.

# Institutional Framework to promote gender equality and freedom from Discrimination

In order to facilitate implementation of gender equality and freedom from discrimination, the government has put in place the State Department of Gender under the Ministry of public service and national values.

- Institutionalize gender mainstreaming in ministries, departments and agencies as well as all other institutions.
- Promote the development and review of gender policies and legislations
- Promote research; collection and analysis, storage and dissemination of sex disaggregated data
- Coordinate programmes for reduction of Sexual and Gender Based Violence (SGBV)
- Develop and implement a national strategy on sexual and reproductive health and youth
- Set up mechanisms to build the capacity of National and County level actors, monitor compliance and report on progress in ensuring accountability on equality and non-discrimination

The Constitution established an independent commission, the National Gender and Equality Commission with the mandate to promote gender equality and freedom from discrimination and to hold the government accountable for its performance. Some parliamentary caucuses are in existence. These are the Kenya Women Parliamentarians Association (KWPA) and the Kenya Parliamentary Caucus on Disability. Both contribute to Parliament's work in the area of integrating gender into laws and policies. Similar caucus exist at County level.

Civil Society Organizations (CSOs), private sector and religious organizations are also responsible for both implementation and act as accountability bodies in measuring service delivery performance. The CSOs have a specific mandate to implement and monitor the gender-mainstreaming mandate within a specific parliamentary committee for effectiveness. In the Kenya National Action Plan on HIV/AIDS, 2013-2017, the government has taken responsibility of gender equality but its engagement was only limited to discussing budgets.

The Kenyan poverty levels are very high with women bearing the brunt. Women are unable to access education and health services equally as men. This is a problem that is experienced across all regions. Women with disability and women with HIV and AIDS suffer the discrimination in accessing services. The government has taken steps to empower women to overcome poverty, access leadership, build businesses and live decently. The government has also taken steps towards gender sensitive action and gender responsive budgeting. There are special catalytic funds dedicated to women, persons with disabilities and the youth for development programmes. These are: -


---
**Page 180**
---

a) Women Enterprise Fund (WEF) that provides microfinance credit and other financial support for women.

b) The Youth Enterprise Development Fund (YEDF) that provides credit for young men and women to enable them establish businesses or start a business after graduating from school.

c) The Ability Fund (Kiwasihii word for Ability) that empowers women, persons with disabilities and youth that give seed money to the special interest groups as startup capital.

d) The Social Protection Fund is given as credit and cash transfers to older members of society who are above 65 years old or below 18 years old or disabled.

e) 30% document reservation affirmative action to Special Interest Groups (SIG) that include women, persons with disabilities and the youth. The SIG access 30% value of all public procurement contracts.

f) There is the national Government Affirmative Action Fund, established in 2015 administered by the Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Development for economic empowerment of women, youth, persons with disabilities, children and elderly persons.

g) The National Gender Equality Commission was established in 2016 to incorporate gender mainstreaming targets.

h) In order to promote equality, justice and non-discrimination, the Constitution established an independent commission: the National Gender and Equality Commission (NGEC). This commission is responsible for monitoring freedom from discrimination and to hold the government accountable on implementation.

# Resilience of communities dependent on drylands ecosystems which are vulnerable to climate change

Dryland communities rely highly on ecosystem services, both directly and indirectly, to secure their livelihoods. The same ecosystems face a myriad of threats from urbanisation, expansion of urban infrastructure, land degradation, desertification, overgrazing, and invasive alien species (UNDP, 2009). The livestock sector in Africa, for example, is being affected by climate change, leading to increased vulnerability, reduced pasture availability, and increased disease prevalence in drylands (WISP, 2010).

To survive these changing trends, pastoralists employ a number of highly resilient climate change adaptation strategies. These are based on the principles that they have developed over generations: climatic events, disease outbreaks and social unrest. These resilience strategies try to promote the sustainability of livestock production systems in drylands where the needs depend on while building strong social networks (Hesse and MacGregor, 2006).

Some of these strategies include:

- Building up reserves such as insurance against times of hardship.
- Splitting herds across different locations and movement patterns to spread risks from lack of grazing and exposure to disease.
- Keeping different livestock breeds to make use of different ecological niches.
- Selecting animals for different traits that enable survival in prevailing conditions.
- Livestock are used not only as a source of food, but also for transport, maintenance and building of their herd, to develop and strengthen social relations as a form of social capital.
- Matching the number of animals to the availability of natural pastures and water.

Although pastoralist livelihood strategies are adaptive to changing climates via its vis-dynamic mobility in search of water and pasture, this mobility is declining over the years and undermining community resilience. The decline in mobility is due to increasing human population pressure at key end points; planners have overtime failed to consider the routes of traditional migration pathways for livestock (Mokwena et al., 2013).

Secondly conflict, cross border or quasi-conflictual traditions due to raiding of livestock are other drivers that increase vulnerability to drought and put pressure on supporting ecosystems (Moron, 2007).

Increasing population pressure can also undermine the self-sufficiency of pastoral communities (IPCC, 2014). This is because it reduces their ability to meet their own food security needs and undermines livestock security. The vulnerability of pastoralists to climate change is induced vulnerable by a combination of factors including: lack of access to information; exposure to external multiple pressures – social, economic, environmental and political - which lead to 'encroachment on rangelands; inappropriate land policy; undermining of pastoral culture and values; and the loss of traditional knowledge and skills that are essential for resilience and complementarity' (IPCC, 2014, p67).

Many conflicts in Africa are rooted in poverty and insecurity. 80 percent of major armed conflicts occur within their borders, which adds to existing vulnerabilities (Middelthon et al., 2011). Short-lived insecurity can exacerbate environmental degradation; there is also a strong link between conflict and climate change, with both achieving negative developments (IPCC, 2014). The mobility which is so important to pastoralist ways of life also makes them vulnerable to conflict or a fear of conflict. This is because it limits their ability to protect themselves from attacks (UNDP, 2009).


---
**Page 182**
---

Pressures on land and natural resources, plus increasing poverty have led to increased livestock raiding as pastoralists seek to secure bride wealth payments (Aijun and Ridgeway, 2008).

In northern Kenya a range of coping strategies for responding to drought and longer-term adaptation are being adopted (see Morton, 2007), including:

1) Mobility, herd accumulation
2) Multi-species herds to exploit different ecological niches;
3) The combined labour of men, women and children
4) Intensification and credit access through mobile phonepayers and bank accounts
5) Use of supplementary feed for livestock and intensification of animal disease management via both indigenous and scientific methods
6) Payment for water from powered boreholes
7) Livelihood diversification such as charcoal production
8) Improved mechanisms for sharing livestock products
9) Use of live animals to assist the poorest although this may be declining as risk levels rise within communities.

# Resilience and Climate Financing

## Impacts of County-level funds on building dryland resilience

Pre-established county level funds could prove to be versatile mechanisms through which to channel dryland projects funding to support community driven development and resilience building in drylands. These funds can also provide a means for the counties to have direct access to fund disbursements, supporting the country and county driven focus of resilience. Access to these funds will enable counties to develop their own climate adaptation plans using county government’s authority and responsibility for developing the social and economic aspects of the county according to its local priorities and an per County Integrated development plans (CIDPs).

## Building dryland resilience at County level through climate funds

The devolved climate finance

The Adaptation Consortium (Ada) is one key institution helping to strengthen the institutional arrangements in Kenya that will enable counties to access climate finance. The funds are channeled through the National Drought Management Authority to Arid and Semi-Arid Lands (ASALs) counties. The County Climate Adaptation Fund enables the prioritization of adaptation investments at county level, with a focus on drylands.

At the county level, five Arid and Semi-Arid Counties of Garissa, Isiolo, Kitui, Makueni, and Wajir are now implementing the County Climate Adaptation Fund (CCAF) through the County Drought Management Authority (CDMA). The CCAF is implemented within the County structure by drafting and passing CCF regulations and policies. The five counties have also been accredited to access funds from the Green Climate Fund (GCF), and other climate finance sources, once the National Implementing Entities (NIE) have been accredited. The Department of International Development (UKAID) is providing technical assistance in this regard.

The County Climate Adaptation Fund (CCAF) was initially piloted in Isiolo County and has since been scaled up to include five other counties of Garissa, Kitui, Makueni and Wajir (Ada consortium, 2015)

### Box 7: Piloted Counties under County Climate Adaptation Fund

Isiolo County in Isiolo County is now being implemented in four other dryland counties of Garissa, Isiolo, Makueni and Wajir which will now cover approximately 22% of Kenya’s land area. The CCAF has been piloted in Isiolo County since January 2015 and has so far reached out to over 27 million people and has disbursed KES 348,960,000 worth of climate finance through provision of climate information services and drought risk management interventions.

The CCAF model conforms to the Constitution of Kenya where public participation is a core tenet of the fund. Through the participatory approach, local communities are authorized to prioritize their own projects and implement them in line with the County Integrated Development Plan (CIDP). The communities’ priorities are captured in proposals developed by Ward-level Adaptation Committees (WACs) which are then submitted for approval at the county level. The WACs submit these in order to meet the CAF funding criteria. The five selected counties so far are also taking advantage of Kenya’s devolved system of governance to integrate climate change in their County Integrated Development Plans (CIDPs). In addition, draft policies and regulations are being enacted to integrate climate change into their County Integrated Development Plans (CIDPs). The five counties have already drafted CCF bills and regulations and have already presented them to their respective county assemblies for approval. These will be submitted to the National Assembly for consideration by the county government and can be funded from the county budget. The CCF bills and regulations are expected to give a greater voice of the communities in determining their priority investments (Ada consortium, 2015)


---
**Page 188**
---

3. Key Elements Strengthening Decentralized Planning Processes

1. Establishing County Climate Change Fund (CCCF) - a devolved finance mechanism underpinned by legislation that ensures funds are directed to projects which most vulnerable with women and youth involved in decision making across counties through the CCCF.
2. Integration of Climate Information & Resilience assessment into development planning and budget processes at county level.
3. Monitoring system to track how adaptation builds resilience and strengthens economic development

4. Achievements of Decentralised Planning Processes

The Adaptation Committee has showcased that climate adaptation planning, supported by devolved finance instruments such as the CCCF, can significantly benefit vulnerable people in poor and marginalized dryland households. Key achievements include:

I. Legislation: The Kenya Climate Change Act (2016) has been passed to institutionalize CCCF as public fund by ensuring county planning and budgeting process are aligned with adaptation priorities. This is a huge step towards scaling up, for example, the CCCF legislation commits counties to contribute a certain minimum % of their revenue to the CCCF. The Act also requires that at least 50% of all projects funded by the CCCF must be implemented in areas where people live and work, with the aim of increasing the number of people prioritizing climate change adaptation (content driven, rather than donor driven).

II. The Kenya Climate Change Act (2016) has also established a National Adaptation Fund (NAF) for SDF - This will institutionalize the process and enable counties to be accredited as Executing Entities.

III. Linkage with national level processes in addition to piloting priority action from National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP), Kenya’s NDC, the UNFCCC’s Green Climate Fund Me framework, the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and Ending Drought Emergency (EDE) programme. This will ensure that adaptation priorities are prioritized for scaling up.

IV. The CCCF has scaled to over 1.5 Million vulnerable people in dry lands of Kenya and supporting them with climate adapted resilience projects

V. By providing a dedicated fund for adaptation, the CCCF is being implemented (through Ward Adaptation Planning Committees) and through the county planning and budget process to deliver projects with high adaptation impact and in a cost-effective way (has been done from 2016).

# Gender issues in response to the impact of climate change on drylands

Climate change mitigation measures in the project area could include reforestation, and promotion: development of alternative clean energy sources while adaptation strategies include diversification of livelihoods, improved water management, agro-ecological intensification of water pans, institutionalization of traditional natural resource management structures (e.g. guardian forests), and strengthening local institutions.

Additional measures include the improvement of livestock breeds, restocking, destocking and establishing a Caribou Management Plan.

## Encourage conservation through income generation

Associating credit facilities with natural resource management efforts is one of the best ways of encouraging rural women and men to take an interest in environmentally sound activities.

### Involve local women and men

Local land use management arrangements that incorporate both women's and men's interests should be developed. This demonstrates that women will not be empowered and supported to assume greater local control over resources.

### Awareness raising and education

This should be aimed at attitudinal change in barangay members, particularly women. A cooking demonstration based on the local experience promoted improved cooking stove designs as a way of combating deforestation. Women reported some of the new stoves because they did not take spices to work and could cook more food for their families.

### Strengthening women's groups

Women's groups are successful in increasing household food security. However, local institutions are often not yet strong enough to continue the credit schemes, resulting in a lack of sustainability.

### Promote sustainability

It is essential to promote both empowerment and the cultural traits that lead to sustainability. However, focusing on the traditional roles in order to ensure the sustainability of drylands carries the risk of danger of reinforcing gender gaps.

### Improve women's and men's access to and control over productive resources such as land, water, livestock and other natural resources

Also promoting a better access to resources can contribute to women's and men's empowerment.

# General Findings

## Findings

The focus and scope of the gender analysis is clustered on 3-4 large scale droughted resources that cut across the thirteen county boundaries creating four (4) priority landscape sites for cooperative land use analyses namely: Moyale-Wajir North-Baringo Hills, Sabotawere, Mid Tana river and Chyulu Hills.

Before is a summary of general findings:

1. Gender discrimination is evident as a result of an unequal social, cultural and economic structure and limited political and organizational influence, which translate into women's lack of access to resources and decision-making power in most areas; in general, men are responsible for decision-making and the planning of farming activities, while women have little authority and have to seek their husbands' permission before they carry out any activity. Women's limited access to land and other natural resource areas play a key role in natural resource management and achieving food security. They often grow, collect and sell cash crops such as potatoes, onions, tomatoes, beans etc., are generally responsible for small livestock, vegetable gardens and collecting fuel, fodder and water from distant sources.
2. In the project areas mentioned above it was generally found out that women's traditional roles are particularly crucial in drylands in terms of natural resource management and food security. Women play a key role in decision making and planning of farming activities and livestock management but in other areas, they contribute to household income by selling their produce. This empowers women to assume new roles and responsibilities on the farm and rangelands. Women's access to credit, extension services, agricultural inputs and other financial support services (including credit, extension services etc.) is often restricted.
3. Through gender analysis it was observed that women's limited access to agricultural resources and decision-making power in most areas of the project sites has led to practices that force rural women into a subsistence role and hamper their productivity. As well as limiting their ability to contribute to household income, this also limits their mobility; it was also clear that customary practices and laws have limited women's rights to land despite the fact that they are the ones who cultivate most of the land. Women's insecure land tenure that reduces women's and youth incentives to have no permanent rights to land and other natural resources, lack access to credit, extension services or no access to credit, rural organizations and other agricultural inputs and services.
4. Field experiences suggest that an interesting social and economic transition is under way in the project areas where women are increasingly involved in income generation activities and relying less on agricultural activities for income and more on off-farm employment (as well as selling their produce) to meet household needs, while at the same time meeting their food security needs in an environment of declining land productivity and, at the same time, it reduces the pressure on drylands natural resources.
5. Social mobilization is a key strategy for women's empowerment in obtaining credit. This is a direct consequence of their lacking ownership to land and to their low involvement in development processes. Women's groups are therefore being encouraged by NGOs to promote income-generating activities for women as it is a vital source of household food security, provides an opportunity for social mobilization and organizational skills and empower them through increased revenues and self-esteem.
6. While men are mostly represented in traditional farmers' organizations and are the recipients of most extension services, the establishment of women's groups and supporting women's groups helps women to improve their own livelihoods. Through


---
**Page 191**
---

7\. By recognition of the value of indigenous knowledge, as well as women’s and men’s roles as innovators regarding dryland conservation and farming techniques, then the project has sought to involve women in decision-making processes (at all levels: production and end users) in order to improve tools and techniques aimed at combating desertification. This is a powerful way to mitigate the conditions of change. Involving rural communities, especially the “voices” in resource management, in decision making regarding land use and conservation practices and techniques is a powerful way to mitigate the conditions and the impact of land degradation.

8\. It was observed that removal of gender-related barriers for greater access to resources was critical. Through a gender perspective eight (8) key issues related to access to resources were identified: land tenure, credit, extension services, education, time, information, awareness raising, decision making, support services and market access. These are the main factors that limit women’s participation in agriculture and youth to make long-term investments in land rehabilitation, conservation and maintain soil quality because they have no long term or permanent rights on the land.

9\. Women’s groups have been established as a means of empowering women within 4 project landscapes it was noted that customary practices and laws that limit women’s right to access resources are often not respected. The establishment of women’s groups, which guarantees women rights to land. Providing credit through traditional mutual assistance groups, is one of the best ways of encouraging rural women and men to take an interest in dryland conservation.

10\. Smallholders, particularly women, often face difficulties in obtaining credit due to lack of collateral security. Women are also less likely than men to have access to savings and alternative livelihood possibilities making credit available to small farmers, especially to women, is a key factor for increasing their income and improving their standard of living. The provision of extension services has been hampered despite their multiple roles in dryland management. Women’s groups have if supported well, are capable of tackling extreme livelihood conditions and can be effective in the promotion of sustainable land use practices and irrigation activities. Awareness raising and education concerning desertification can lead to better land use practices and more efficient water use.

11\. It was observed that women in the targeted drylands face the difficulty of turning surplus products into cash income because of their lack of transport and access to markets. Women’s groups have been established as a means of empowering women within 4 project landscapes it was noted that customary practices and laws are rarely geared towards small-scale production or crops grown by women. The establishment of women’s groups, which guarantees women rights to land.

12\. Women living in drylands are particularly concerned as they usually have to walk longer distances to collect water and fuel wood and take on more farming responsibilities in the absence of men who migrate for work. Women’s groups can be effective in empowering and activities that free-up women from heavy workloads, such as the search for water and fuel wood. The establishment of women’s groups can also help them spend time on their gardens, income generating activities or dryland conservation agriculture.

# Specific findings in line with Gender Analysis Framework

1. Men control access to most productive assets in Kenya (World Bank 2003). Most dryland communities in Kenya share a patriarchal culture in which men own the key production resources and make decisions about their use. For example, women may milk the family cow and sell the milk products, but she could not sell the cow itself.
2. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes regarding land use. This means that activities which include the use of productive resources like land or livestock should be considered in terms of gender equality. Therefore, any project aimed at changing relations relating to dryland conservation should include both local men and women as key stakeholders.
3. Access to credit is a key constraint in improving women's economic productivity across the drylands. This is because it is assumed that the project focuses on improving women's access to credit.
4. Women constitute up to 80% of the food production labor in drylands yet benefit from only 15-20% of the income generated by this activity (World Bank, 2003). In some provinces, and cultural sensitivities may prohibit men extension agents from talking directly with women about their agricultural activities.
5. It is proposed that the project targets improving women's access to agriculture inputs.
6. Project staff should be trained in gender equality issues and in the use of Gender Development's across key counties still practicing FGM Program activities that eliminate female genital mutilation (FGM) and promote its replacement with other locally acceptable practices.
7. Literacy varies greatly among counties and is highest in the northern counties. There is a tendency for men to be more literate than women, which means that many do not need to do this work. This is a backward belief that it hindered women from getting involved in agricultural activities. The project should target women who are literate.

10. Social protection schemes show great promise in supporting vulnerable groups in dryland areas and particularly women. This can be implemented by improving women’s access to risk management and insurance products.

11. For productivity gains to be sustainable over time, they must increase their socio-economic position in the household. For example, by linking women to markets for livestock-related products, women are more likely to benefit from increased income. This is particularly acceptable among most dryland communities. More investment is also needed to improve women’s participation in alternative livelihoods, to ensure that their rights and interests are recognized.

12. Increased collaboration and partnership with linked-investing CSOs, academic institutions and other stakeholders can help address the social, economic, political, cultural, pastoralist, and environmental sustainability issues in the drylands. This awareness raising and capacity building will also help women to better understand how they can revitalize them and women’s knowledge and equal rights in particular. In addition, CSOs could assist women in accessing credit and other financial services that would help them challenge discriminatory social norms and harmful practices.

13. Women are often responsible for the majority of unpaid domestic (household) and productive (market) and reproductive (households) labor time. Time poverty and income poverty often reinforce each other, with women spending more hours on household chores than men. This can have a negative impact on their ability to participate in economic activities. The time burden on women’s time burden may negatively impact other areas of her life. Avoiding such activities is not always possible to a minimum, or mitigating them with others should be the hierarchy of the project.

14. It is critical for proposed activities to understand and integrate season calendars and daily routines into their design. This will help ensure that women are included in food activities that reduce the “time poverty” of women. Secondly, time poverty among female-headed households can be reduced by ensuring that men participate more fully in the project can help ensure project benefits are distributed to both poor and better off households. Thirdly, it is important for women’s participation to be considered critical for female-headed households. In other words, the women have to be clearly better off as a result of their participation in the project than they would have been without the activity.

15. Securing equal traditional divisions of labor may help to design the project better to be more socially acceptable. It is women and girls who collect the drinking water in most dryland areas, while men are responsible for livestock management. If this work falls on girls may drop (Nurkina & Finders 2004; Nditira & Nyangama 2015). Improving drinking water collection can help to reduce time poverty and increase women’s participation in the project.

16. The new constitution in August 2010 guarantees equal rights regardless of gender, and for the first time, it also provides for affirmative action to ensure that women are not put at a disadvantage against discrimination. The project should build on Kenya’s legal framework for gender equality.

17. Increasing access to land for women is likely to have larger benefits for local human well-being than doing the same for men. Secondly, the government has encouraged the foundation of women’s groups in order to ensure that women are better represented and may help ensure women benefit at least as much as men from a conservation initiative.

# Project Specific Recommendations

## 18.1 Specific Recommendations

1. Promote the use of efficient technologies targeting women to increase agricultural production, particularly with improved crop varieties and drought-resistant inputs for crops.
2. Enhance practices and technologies for animal production and health, including reproductive management and disease control.
3. Promote capacity building activities on proper livestock breeding, as well as agrometric practices and techniques for improving the productivity of animals.
4. Strengthen surveillance mechanisms at county and ward levels to control pests and diseases in livestock and crops, with a focus on women's participation in implementing sustainable disease control programmes and strategies targeting women and rural communities.
5. In coordination with counties, enforce existing laws governing disease control and implementation of animal health services, including the use of community animal health workers (CAHWs) targeting both men and women.
6. Support the establishment of county-level veterinary service organizations in providing animal health and production services (e.g., veterinarians, government veterinary extension officers, CAHWs, livestock farmers' associations, local agricultural societies, NGOs, and self-help groups [SHGs]).
7. Introduce appropriate storage techniques and practices for reducing storage and post-harvest losses with specific tailored trainings geared towards women.
8. Invest in the development of value-added products from livestock and other food products by women. These should be particularly related to the marketing of livestock products, and support should include training on product processing, packaging, and access to markets and slaughterhouses.
9. Support women's income-generating activities to enhance the diversification of income sources and livelihoods with both on-farm and off-farm productive activities and services. This can be achieved by providing training in business management skills, diversifying the risk exposure and mitigating the negative coping strategies employed by less educated women.
10. Expand access to financial support services targeting women in rural households to contribute towards their economic empowerment. These should include microfinance loans to strengthen and diversify their livelihood base and income potential. This should provide women with opportunities for business development, including livestock matching grants, with a focus on youth and women.
11. Enhance the capacity of women's groups to address issues and youth to access efficient financial products and services, such as access to credit and market information.
12. Enhance environmental sustainability, and improve natural resource management and agricultural productivity by providing training in land use planning and negotiation to secure access to land, life deeds and contracts.
13. For women's groups, provide training on gender-sensitive conflict resolution tasks as pre-requisites for improving the sustainable and equitable use of natural resources (such as land, grazing areas, water sources) that are critical for livestock production.
14. Enhance participation of women and men in rangeland rehabilitation and management activities to improve soil fertility, reduce erosion, increase access to water, and pasture for livestock, and can decrease natural resource-based conflicts and impunity.
15. Facilitate and support community-based management of rangeland and rehabilitation projects by providing training on the sustainable use and conservation of rangelands through cash-for-work programmes that include women participating.

# Gender Action Plan

The Gender Action Plan provides entry points for gender-responsive actions to be taken under each of the activity areas of the Twende project. The plan will be refined in the inception phase of the project to more effectively support youth programming and at that point will become a Gender and Youth Action plan.

The plan is structured around the four main activities of the project, with specific gender responsive actions identified for each activity area. In addition an indicative set of indicators have been outlined to measure and track progress on these actions at the activity level. To avoid developing an unwieldy system three or four of the most critical gender responsive indicators identified here will be incorporated into the detailed M&E plan to be developed at the project level.

In addition to monitoring through collecting indicator data, gender responsiveness of the project will be monitored though a participatory process. A Gender Responsiveness Monitoring and Evaluation Plan will be developed in consultation with the Gender Task Group and subjected to the requisite data quality assessment process.


A gender task group will be established early in the project drawn from different institutions of the project. IUCN will lead that task group which will be responsible for implementing the GAP, training and capacity building on gender and youth programming will be held for all the institutions working on the project. Specific support will be provided by IUCN's Global Gender Office in the gender task group.

| Activity 1.2 Strengthen community institutions to coordinate community planning and to inform and engage men in contributing their unique knowledge to landscape planning | * Integration of gender and the roles/responsibilities around landscape planning. * Engage women in contributing their unique knowledge to landscape planning. * Ensure the involvement of both men and women rangeland management practitioners participating in landscape management activities |

| Number of women and men involved in climate change, landscape planning and watershed risk management activities | NEMA, County Governments, MOALI, ICRIF, IUCN |

Objective

### Objective 1.3 Developing county rangeland restoration plans that are gender-sensitive and combined with climate change data.

#### Actions

- Ensure men and women are equally engaged in developing county rangeland restoration plans.
- Clearly outline the role of women and the youth developing the plans, including their roles in landscape level planning at county level using the SHARED LANDSCAPE PLANNING (SLP) approach.
- The role of women and youth clearly defined in the inter-county forums for landscape planning
- Ensure youth participate in the trainings on landscape planning and ecosystem management.

#### Indicator

- The role of women, men and youth clearly defined in the county rangeland restoration plans.
- Number of women and men participating in landscape level planning at county level using SLP approach.
- Number of women and men participating in training on landscape management plans.

### Responsible Institutions

- IUCN
- NOMA County governments
- Women groups/CSOs


#### Activity 1.4 Establish functioning land management mechanisms with participatory committees to integrate sensitive and adaptive management practices.

#### Actions

- The role of women and youth clearly defined in the inter-county forums for landscape planning.
- Ensure youth participate in the trainings on landscape planning and ecosystem management.
- Ensure youth participation is integrated into committees and groups managing local dryland areas, including water resources.

#### Indicator

- Number of women participating in inter-county forums for participatory rangeland management.
- Number of men and women participating in training on landscape management plans.
- Number of women and men participating in committees and groups managing dryland areas, including water resources.

### Responsible Institutions

- NOMA County governments
- Women groups/CSOs


#### Activity 1.5 Establish participatory monitoring and evaluation systems to support adaptive management.

#### Actions

- The role of women and youth clearly defined in the annual reports.
- Ensure both men and women in government institutions are involved in the development of annual reports.
- Define indicators for measuring and monitoring drylands activities, including those related to the needs of both men and women, and that are sex disaggregated.

#### Indicator

- Number of women participating in the establishment of participatory monitoring systems.
- Number of men and women involved in the monitoring of drylands activities.
- Number of women and men involved in the development of indicators for measuring and monitoring drylands activities include those related to the needs of both men and women, and that are sex disaggregated.

### Responsible Institutions

- IUCN
- NOMA County governments
- MOU county staff


#### Activity 2.1 Implementing community-based rangeland restoration activities

#### Actions

- Ensure communities contribute to engendered community plans for the management of drylands.
- Identify best practices to be shared based on the SLP approach.
- Identify and carry out soil remediation strategies for both men and women.
- Ensure both men and women are equally engaged in planning and implementing restoration activities.

#### Indicator

- Number of women and youth participating in planning and implementing rangeland restoration activities.
- Number of women and youth participating in community-based rangeland restoration activities.

### Responsible Institutions

- Communities, CSO with support

Objective: sensitive landscape management

Activities:

Activity 3.1 Invest in priority value chain development by local communities

* Develop improved methods to increase productivity and incomes from milk, meat, and skins processing by women through training and extension services.
* Develop improved management systems for insect pests, diseases, and weeds that are gender-sensitive and environmentally friendly.
* Develop improved quantity and nutrient quality of feed/forage production and utilization through the involvement of feed/fodder preparation and manufacture technologies developed by women, especially crops, vegetable, fruits, and legumes.
* Identify and address constraints in agriculture methods to increase resilience and benefit both men and women without increasing vulnerability.
* Women's involvement in strengthening market information systems for livestock products, including marketing, quality management, and sustainable harvesting of natural resources.

Activity 3.2 Provide services and infrastructure for restoration enterprises by women's groups

* Establish women-based grass seed production and distribution enterprises.
* Training women and men in grass seed production.
* Grant management training for women and youth.
* Focus on resource mobilization for women and the youth.
* Identification of female community members who are interested in starting a business.
* Ensure training/mentoring on business development, including access to credit and markets that provide equality to both men and women.
* Establish market infrastructure that is relevant for both start-ups and women's needs.

Indicator: to develop a long-term rangeland extension strategy

Responsible Institutions:

* CI, CSOs, Women's Groups
* CI, Justdigit, CSOs

# Annex 1. Gender Responsiveness Action Tool (GReACT) for TWENDE Implementation

The purpose of this tool is to help TWENDE implementing the Gender Action Plan, implement and monitor their interventions in a gender-responsive manner. Activities have been grouped according to "type": e.g., assessments, training, policy advisory, etc. For each type of activity, this tool provides an indication of associated gender-responsive objectives and gender-responsive actions to be taken in preparing and implementing these activities. Examples of gender-responsive methods are further illustrated with examples of concrete methods to carry them out. The tool will be refined in the inception phase of the project.

## How to use this tool in context where activities are both ongoing and new activities are being designed/coming online:

- Half-day to full day workshop, either with several implementing partners at a time (global to cluster level), or with a single implementing partner or TWENDE secretariat.
  - Session 1 (30 min): Introduce revised Gender Guidance note and the Gender responsiveness action tool
  - Session 2 (90 mins): Group work and discussion on what partners have been doing so far within each type of activity, and using this tool to make it simple to use/easy to update. Report to plenary and discussion; feedback on possible refinements to the tool.
  - Session 3 (90 minutes): Group work: each IP identifies the set of concrete actions it will incorporate in its workplan. Share back with plenary and discussion.
- Session 4 (45 minutes): Monitoring and reporting on gender-responsive interventions in TWENDE
